We derive such a method that optimizes the mapping based on Neyman's chi-square divergence between the densities representing the uncertainties of the estimated and the actual locations. The densities are represented as channel vectors computed with a basis function approach. The mapping between these vectors is updated with each new pair of images such that fast convergence and high accuracy are achieved. The resulting algorithm runs in real-time and is superior to state-of-the-art methods in terms of convergence and accuracy in a number of experiments.

</details>


### [50] [Towards Physics-Faithful Generation of Scientific Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13112)
*Minghui Zhang,Jinxin Shi,Yifan Chang,Liangliang Zhao,Yuandong Pu,Qian Yu,Ming Hu,Hanxiao Zhang,Yun Gu,Yirong Chen,Yu Qiao,Bo Zhang,Xiangchao Yan,Bin Fu,Yihao Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A physics-faithful diagram generator using SP-CoT structured reasoning and a large annotated dataset, improving faithfulness on scientific diagrams via explicit per-subdiscipline reasoning and a new benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-image models trained on web data generate visually plausible but physically incorrect diagrams, hindering education and science communication.

Method: Introduce SP-CoT: a fixed, per-subdiscipline schema guiding multi-step reasoning from scene identification to governing laws; enforce separation of grounded facts vs inferred reasoning; symbolically type mathematics; curate 4.3M physics images with expert annotations; train a unified multimodal backbone; create VeriphyT2IBench with item-specific binary questions per diagram; evaluate on GenExam physics subset and VeriphyT2IBench.

Result: Explicit physics-structured supervision improves physical faithfulness of generated diagrams, demonstrating gains on held-out diagrams per the evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: Structured supervision and dedicated benchmarks enable reliable, physics-faithful diagram generation; SP-CoT provides a scalable, interpretable reasoning scaffold for scientific image synthesis.

Abstract: Text-to-image generation has reached photorealistic quality, yet state-of-the-art systems remain unreliable at producing scientific diagrams, whose value depends not on appearance but on physical faithfulness: correct force directions, valid coordinate systems, consistent thermodynamic states, and equations matching the depicted scenario. Trained on web imagery with physically shallow captions, generic models produce diagrams that look plausible but are physically wrong, harmful in education and scientific communication. We present Princigram, a physics-faithful scientific-diagram generator, and its data pipeline. Our central advance is Structured Physical Chain-of-Thought (SP-CoT): a per-subdiscipline schema that decomposes a physics diagram into an explicit multi-step reasoning chain across six subdisciplines, from scene identification through force or process analysis to governing laws and synthesis. Unlike free-form chain-of-thought, SP-CoT follows a fixed schema with strict fidelity rules that separate visually grounded facts from physically inferred reasoning and type all mathematics symbolically; it serves both as dense training supervision and, at inference, as a structured "thinking" prompt. With it we curate and structurally annotate 4.3 million physics images, of which 115,037 carry expert-level annotation, and adapt a unified multimodal backbone. We further introduce VeriphyT2IBench, whose questions are derived from each held-out diagram's own structured annotation: each diagram becomes an item-specific bank of binary questions about its objects, forces, and states, so a judge model's score decomposes into named physical facts rather than one holistic number. On the physics subset of GenExam and on VeriphyT2IBench, Princigram shows that explicit physics-structured supervision improves the physical faithfulness of generated scientific diagrams.

</details>


### [51] [EgoMonth: A Month-Level Egocentric Video Benchmark for Long-Term Spatiotemporal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13113)
*Weitao Chen,Hu Jiaxin,Xie Tianyidan,Yang Li,Yuyi Qian,Banghao Xu,Ziheng Tang,Shenyi Wang,Mingyue Yu,Duo Li,Jiacheng Shi,Gao Wang,Zhan Xu,Zhicheng Qiu,Xuanfu Li,Jian Yang,Lanjun Wang,Zili Yi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces EgoMonth, a month-level egocentric video benchmark for long-term memory and continuity; 300+ hours from 20 participants over 20–120 days, with 1,443 QA, across 14 tasks in three cognitive levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether multimodal LLMs can maintain consistent long-term spatiotemporal memory across days/weeks using continuous egocentric video data, addressing a gap in existing short-clip, non-continuous benchmarks.

Method: Create EgoMonth dataset of first-person daily-life videos; design a cognitively grounded 14-task evaluation framework organized into Schema Consolidation, Episodic Indexing, and Cascading Reasoning; evaluate state-of-the-art MLLMs (open/closed) on macro-averaged accuracy against human baselines.

Result: Top models (e.g., Gemini 2.5 Pro) reach 71.8% macro-average accuracy, 22.4 points below corrected human baseline 94.2%; some tasks near 25% chance; models perform poorly on Route Reasoning, Cross-view Spatial Reasoning, and Direction Judgement.

Conclusion: Current MLLMs function as lossy summarizers rather than faithful long-term memorizers; there is a need for architectures with genuine long-term spatiotemporal memory and improved evaluation benchmarks to assess memory across days/weeks.

Abstract: Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have led to substantial progress in video understanding, accompanied by a growing number of long video benchmarks. However, existing benchmarks rely predominantly on web-sourced videos that lack inter-clip spatiotemporal continuity, making it difficult to assess whether models can maintain consistent memory across days or weeks of real-world experience. We introduce EgoMonth, the first month-level egocentric video understanding benchmark. EgoMonth comprises over 300 hours of first-person daily-life recordings from 20 participants spanning 20 to 120 days, paired with 1,443 human-crafted multiple-choice question-answer pairs. We design a cognitively grounded 14-task evaluation framework organized into three hierarchical cognitive levels: Schema Consolidation, Episodic Indexing, and Cascading Reasoning. Evaluation of state-of-the-art open-source and closed-source MLLMs reveals that even the best-performing model, Gemini 2.5 Pro, achieves only 71.8% macro-average accuracy, remaining 22.4 percentage points below the corrected human baseline of 94.2%. Several models perform near or below the 25% chance level on tasks such as Route Reasoning, Cross-view Spatial Reasoning, and Direction Judgement, while even the strongest closed-source model remains substantially below human performance. These results indicate that current MLLMs function as lossy summarizers rather than faithful memorizers, highlighting the need for architectures with genuine long-term spatiotemporal memory.

</details>


### [52] [Fast Iterative Five point Relative Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13114)
*Johan Hedborg,Michael Felsberg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces a novel iterative relative pose estimation method for calibrated cameras based on Powell's Dog Leg, aiming to match Nister's five-point algorithm in precision while doubling speed; extends to more points and as a refinement step; evaluated on datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve speed of robust relative pose estimation within RANSAC without sacrificing accuracy, enabling efficient refinement and handling more points.

Method: An iterative optimization using Powell's Dog Leg algorithm applied to the five-point relative pose problem; designed to retain efficient error metrics and extend to >5 points; integrated as refinement step in RANSAC.

Result: Achieves similar precision to Nister's algorithm with about 2x speed; scalable to more than five points; validated on three ground-truth datasets; efficient error metrics preserved.

Conclusion: The Dog Leg based solver offers a faster alternative to Nister's five-point solver, suitable as a refinement step and for multi-point extensions; promising for real-time or large-scale robust pose estimation within RANSAC.

Abstract: Robust estimation of the relative pose between two cameras is a fundamental part of Structure and Motion methods. For calibrated cameras, the five point method together with a robust estimator such as RANSAC gives the best result in most cases. The current state-of-the-art method for solving the relative pose problem from five points is due to Nister [9], because it is faster than other methods and in the RANSAC scheme one can improve precision by increasing the number of iterations. In this paper, we propose a new iterative method, which is based on Powell's Dog Leg algorithm. The new method has the same precision and is approximately twice as fast as Nister's algorithm. The proposed method is easily extended to more than five points while retaining a efficient error metrics. This makes it also very suitable as an refinement step. The proposed algorithm is systematically evaluated on three types of datasets with known ground truth.

</details>


### [53] [QuISE: Defense against Typographic Attacks on VLMs via Query-Irrelevant Semantic Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13119)
*Shubin Lu,Jiaqi Yin,Yihao Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: QuISE is a model-agnostic, training-free defense against typographic attacks on vision-language models. It localizes influential text regions, substitutes them with two semantically distinct, query- and image-irrelevant texts, and uses cross-edited answer consistency to decide the final output, yielding improvements across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typographic attacks mislead vision-language models by injecting adversarial text; existing defenses require model access, retraining, or modifications, limiting applicability to closed-source VLMs. A black-box, training-free approach is desirable.

Method: 1) influence-aware text localization to identify text regions likely affecting the current query; 2) replace with two semantically distinct texts that are irrelevant to both query and image; 3) aggregate final answers by consistency across edited images.

Result: Evaluated on three typographic-attack benchmarks, four attack settings, and four VLMs; QuISE improves defended accuracy and achieves a recovery rate of 67.9-75.0% with a harm rate of 0.5-1.1%.

Conclusion: QuISE provides a practical, model-agnostic, training-free defense against typographic attacks, demonstrating strong robustness across diverse VLMs and attack settings; potential limitations include reliance on effective text region localization and the assumption that replacement texts are neutral to queries and images.

Abstract: Typographic attacks pose a critical threat to vision-language models (VLMs) by injecting misleading text into images and causing models to rely on adversarial textual cues rather than visual evidence. Existing defenses often require model-specific modifications, additional training, or access to internal model components, limiting their applicability to modern closed-source VLMs. In this paper, we propose QuISE, a model-agnostic, training-free black-box defense based on query-irrelevant semantic editing. QuISE first identifies text regions likely to affect the current query through influence-aware text localization. QuISE then replaces these regions with two semantically distinct replacement texts that are irrelevant to both the query and the image. The final answer is determined by answer consistency across the edited images. Extensive experiments on three typographic-attack benchmarks, four attack settings, and four VLMs show that QuISE consistently improves defended accuracy. QuISE achieves a recovery rate of 67.9-75.0% with a harm rate of 0.5-1.1%.

</details>


### [54] [Predicting Signed Distance Functions for Visual Instance Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13135)
*Emil Brissman,Joakim Johnander,Michael Felsberg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a distance-map based approach for instance segmentation that predicts per-pixel distances to object contours in multiple directions to approximate a signed distance function (SDF); thresholding yields segmentation and shows higher foreground IoU than YOLACT on COCO, but mapping distance maps to complete instance segmentation is nontrivial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address variability in object shapes that are not well captured by anchor-based bounding boxes; a distance-map/SDF representation could better capture shape diversity (e.g., long, slender objects).

Method: A neural network is trained to predict, at each pixel, the distance to the closest object contour in several directions. These distance maps are pooled to approximate a signed distance function; thresholding the SDF yields foreground-background segmentation. The segmentation is compared to YOLACT on COCO.

Result: The method achieves higher foreground IoU than YOLACT on COCO, indicating improved segmentation quality for foreground regions. However, while the distance maps carry information about individual instances, converting them into full instance segmentations remains nontrivial.

Conclusion: Distance-map-based, direction-aware distance predictions are a promising direction for handling diverse object shapes in instance segmentation, warranting further research into reliably converting distance maps into complete instance masks.

Abstract: Visual instance segmentation is a challenging problem and becomes even more difficult if objects of interest varies unconstrained in shape. Some objects are well described by a rectangle, however, this is hardly always the case. Consider for instance long, slender objects such as ropes. Anchor-based approaches classify predefined bounding boxes as either negative or positive and thus provide a limited set of shapes that can be handled. Defining anchor-boxes that fit well to all possible shapes leads to an infeasible number of prior boxes. We explore a different approach and propose to train a neural network to compute distance maps along different directions. The network is trained at each pixel to predict the distance to the closest object contour in a given direction. By pooling the distance maps we obtain an approximation to the signed distance function (SDF). The SDF may then be thresholded in order to obtain a foreground-background segmentation. We compare this segmentation to foreground segmentations obtained from the state-of-the-art instance segmentation method YOLACT. On the COCO dataset, our segmentation yields a higher performance in terms of foreground intersection over union (IoU). However, while the distance maps contain information on the individual instances, it is not straightforward to map them to the full instance segmentation. We still believe that this idea is a promising research direction for instance segmentation, as it better captures the different shapes found in the real world.

</details>


### [55] [MergeOver: Post-Training Token Merging for Recursive Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13141)
*Junseo Kim,Uraz Odyurt,Amirreza Yousefzadeh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Post-training MergeOver integrates ToMe token merging into a recursively weight-shared SReT, enabling token reduction without retraining and partially recovering memory/throughput costs, with modest accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ViTs incur large parameter counts and quadratic compute, hindering edge deployment. While recursive weight-sharing and token merging help, combining them without retraining is challenging; this work proposes a post-training solution to merge them.

Method: MergeOver inserts Token Merging (ToMe) into the Sliced Recursive Transformer (SReT) with recursive weight-sharing. It uses an Unmerge tracking stack, constraint-safe merge-rate adjustment, and synchronized token-mass tracking across spatial permutations. A stage-wise single-shot schedule reduces tokens at the first block of each stage and keeps the sequence length fixed during subsequent recursive iterations.

Result: On ImageNet-1K, the configuration achieves a 1.47 percentage point top-1 accuracy drop. On GPU, peak activation memory is reduced by 37.3% (batch 1) and 38.4% (batch 16). Throughput changes: -21.7% (batch 1) and +21.7% (batch 16). On Raspberry Pi 5 (ARM CPU), latency decreases by 2.4% (batch 1) and 17.6% (batch 16).

Conclusion: MergeOver demonstrates that token merging can be effectively integrated into hierarchical recursive transformers at post-training, recovering part of the performance/efficiency costs of recursive weight-sharing and establishing a baseline for combining these techniques without retraining.

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) demonstrate exceptional performance in computer vision but suffer from large parameter counts and quadratic computational complexity, severely limiting their deployment on resource-constrained edge hardware. While recursive weight-sharing reduces parameter counts and token merging mitigates computational and memory bottlenecks, integrating these two paradigms without costly retraining is non-trivial, leaving this intersection largely unexplored. We propose MergeOver, a post-training approach that integrates Token Merging (ToMe) into the recursively weight-shared Sliced Recursive Transformer (SReT). Through an Unmerge tracking stack, constraint-safe merge-rate adjustment, and synchronised token-mass tracking across spatial permutations, MergeOver resolves the spatial and merging constraints of this integration. We further employ a stage-wise single-shot schedule that performs token reduction at the first block of each stage and maintains a fixed sequence length throughout its subsequent recursive iterations. Benchmarked on ImageNet-1K, our selected configuration reduces top-1 accuracy by 1.47 percentage points. On the GPU, it reduces peak activation memory by 37.3% and 38.4% at batch sizes 1 and 16, while throughput decreases by 21.7% at batch size 1 but increases by 21.7% at batch size 16. On a Raspberry Pi 5 (ARM CPU), it reduces latency by 2.4% and 17.6% at batch sizes 1 and 16. These results show that MergeOver can recover a meaningful part of the throughput and memory cost that recursive weight-sharing introduces, without retraining, and provides a baseline for combining token merging with hierarchical recursive transformers.

</details>


### [56] [Geometry-Grounded Unified 3D Perception for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13147)
*Longfei Xu,Xiaohui Wang,Zehao Huang,Han Li,Ya Yang,Naiyan Wang,Si Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoUP presents a geometry-grounded, unified 3D perception framework for multi-camera driving scenes, achieving state-of-the-art results across depth, detection, and occupancy via a geometry-aware latent decoded into metric outputs and trained jointly on multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current image-based perception pipelines rely on backbones trained for semantic recognition and add 3D geometry through downstream modules, risking loss of explicit metric geometry and consistent 3D structure. A unified, geometry-preserving representation is needed to jointly support depth, detection, and occupancy across synchronized multi-camera streams.

Method: GeoUP adapts the VGGT reconstruction-oriented latent to calibrated, streaming multi-camera driving data. It decomposes cross-image interactions into self-, temporal-, and view-attention, and injects calibration-aware raymap encodings to embed metric scale and camera geometry. The geometry-grounded latent is decoded for metric depth estimation, 3D object detection, and semantic occupancy prediction (surface-, instance-, and volume-level reads) within a joint multi-task and multi-dataset training regime.

Result: Empirical evaluation on nuScenes, Argoverse 2, Waymo, KITTI, and DDAD shows state-of-the-art performance in depth, detection, and occupancy, validating the effectiveness of geometry-grounded representations for unified 3D driving perception and demonstrating cross-dataset generalization to diverse sensor setups and perception ranges.

Conclusion: Geometry-grounded latent representations enable unified, metric-consistent 3D perception from multi-camera streams, with strong cross-dataset generalization and SOTA results across multiple tasks (depth, 3D detection, occupancy).

Abstract: Camera-based autonomous driving perception requires a shared representation that preserves metric 3D structure across synchronized multi-camera streams. However, existing image-based frameworks often rely on backbones pretrained for semantic recognition, and introduce 3D geometry through downstream task-specific modules. As a result, their shared representations may fail to preserve explicit metric geometry and consistent 3D scene structure. In this paper, we present a Geometry-grounded Unified 3D Perception (GeoUP) framework that adapts the reconstruction-oriented latent of VGGT to calibrated, streaming multi-camera driving scenes. GeoUP factorizes cross-image interaction into self, temporal, and view attention to capture structurally distinct temporal and cross-view correspondences. It further injects calibration-aware raymap encodings to provide metric scale and camera geometry. The resulting geometry-grounded latent is decoded for metric depth estimation, 3D object detection, and semantic occupancy prediction, corresponding to surface-, instance-, and volume-level readouts of the same 3D scene. Through joint multi-task and multi-dataset training, GeoUP effectively leverages heterogeneous annotations and generalizes across diverse sensor configurations and perception ranges. Extensive experiments on nuScenes, Argoverse 2, Waymo, KITTI, and DDAD demonstrate that GeoUP achieves SOTA performance across detection, occupancy, and depth estimation. These results validate the effectiveness of geometry-grounded representations for unified 3D driving perception.

</details>


### [57] [Less Annotation, More Interpretation: Prior-Guided Concept Bottleneck Models for Interpretable Cancer Imaging Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13148)
*Baoqiang Ma,Kenneth Gilhuijs*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Prior-guided hybrid CBMs with limited concept annotations and class-conditional distribution matching improve concept detection (AUC) in cancer imaging while preserving diagnostic accuracy; prior initialization of the concept-to-diagnosis head is the main contributor; zero-shot VLMs are not sufficient for fine-grained tumor-level concepts; reduces annotation burden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instance-level concept annotations are costly. There is a need for interpretable models that can leverage limited annotations and unlabeled data to maintain diagnostic performance in cancer imaging.

Method: Propose a prior-guided hybrid concept bottleneck model (CBM) that (i) uses limited concept annotations, (ii) applies class-conditional concept distribution matching on unannotated patients, and (iii) initializes the concept-to-diagnosis head with prior information. Evaluated on CBIS-DDSM (masses, calcifications) and LIDC-IDRI (pulmonary nodules) across 0–100% concept annotation, with ablations to identify key components.

Result: In the 0–20% annotation regime, the hybrid CBM outperforms a matched standard CBM in mean concept AUC while keeping diagnostic accuracy near black-box models. At 10% annotation: masses 0.619→0.741, calcifications 0.650→0.787, nodules 0.597→0.642. Ablation shows prior initialization as the main contributor (stabilizes the concept-to-diagnosis head). Zero-shot VLMs remain insufficient for reliable fine-grained tumor-level concept prediction.

Conclusion: Structured priors can substantially reduce the annotation burden for interpretable cancer imaging models without sacrificing diagnostic performance.

Abstract: Concept bottleneck models (CBMs) can improve the transparency of cancer image diagnostic prediction by expressing predictions through radiological concepts. However, their dependence on instance-level concept annotations limits practical applicability. We propose a prior-guided hybrid CBM that integrates limited concept annotations, class-conditional concept distribution matching on unannotated patients, and prior initialization of the concept-to-diagnosis head. We evaluate the method on CBIS-DDSM mammographic masses and calcifications and LIDC-IDRI pulmonary nodules across 0-100% concept annotation. In the clinically relevant 0-20% annotation regime, the hybrid CBM consistently improves mean concept AUC over a matched standard CBM, while maintaining diagnostic performance close to black-box models. At 10% annotation specifically, concept AUC increases from 0.619 to 0.741 for masses, from 0.650 to 0.787 for calcifications, and from 0.597 to 0.642 for pulmonary nodules. Ablation experiments identify prior initialization as the main component contributing to improved concept detection, likely by stabilizing the concept-to-diagnosis head. Zero-shot VLMs remain insufficient for reliable fine-grained tumor-level concept prediction. These findings suggest that structured priors can substantially reduce the annotation burden of interpretable cancer imaging models.

</details>


### [58] [Splat-based Metal Artifact Reduction in Cone-Beam CT via Polychromatic Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13159)
*Kiseok Choi,Inchul Kim,Jaemin Cho,Hyeongjun Cho,Min H. Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Physics-informed, self-calibrating metal artifact reduction for CBCT using Gaussian Splatting; jointly optimizes reconstruction and X-ray spectrum without metal masks; introduces synthetic dataset; shows superior results on synthetic and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Beam hardening from polychromatic X-rays produces severe metal artifacts in CBCT; existing monochromatic reconstructions and neural methods have limitations (artifacts or computational cost).

Method: Integrates polychromatic X-ray projection, material-dependent attenuation, and system response into a Gaussian Splatting framework; jointly learns reconstruction parameters and X-ray spectral characteristics; eliminates need for manual masks; creates a high-fidelity synthetic CBCT dataset via Monte Carlo x-ray simulation.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods in artifact suppression and reconstruction accuracy on both synthetic and real datasets; the first splat-based approach for beam hardening reduction in CBCT.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a practical, accurate, and scalable solution for metal artifact reduction in CBCT with joint calibration and physics-informed modeling, plus a valuable synthetic dataset resource.

Abstract: Cone-beam computed tomography (CBCT) enables volumetric reconstruction from X-ray projections, but suffers from severe artifacts--especially beam hardening--when imaging materials with high attenuation such as metals. These artifacts arise from the polychromatic nature of X-rays and are not properly addressed by conventional monochromatic reconstruction algorithms. While recent neural representation-based methods offer improved reconstruction quality, they are computationally expensive and often impractical for deployment. We propose a novel physics-inspired, self-calibrating metal artifact reduction method that efficiently reconstructs 3D CBCT volumes while correcting beam hardening artifacts. Our method integrates a polychromatic X-ray projection model, material-dependent attenuation profiles, and system response modeling into a Gaussian Splatting framework. Unlike prior work, we eliminate the need for manual metal masks or strong prior assumptions, and we optimize both reconstruction parameters and X-ray spectral characteristics jointly during training. We further introduce a high-fidelity synthetic CBCT dataset generation pipeline validated on Monte-Carlo x-ray simulation toolbox and release new datasets with severe metal-induced artifacts to support the community. This is the first splat-based method for reducing beam hardening in CBCT. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in artifact suppression and reconstruction accuracy.

</details>


### [59] [TRAPSBench: Vision-Language Models Encode but Fail to Express Epistemic Restraint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13167)
*Fnu Pramono,John Cai,Sourabh Kulkarni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VLMs can internally signal when abstention is needed under occluded/chaotic visuals but fail to express it; introduces TRAPSBench and PECS; extensive evaluation across 16 VLMs shows poor spontaneous abstention; bottleneck lies in expression, not perception; linear probes reveal high decode-ability of answerability from hidden states; latent-space interventions can induce/suppress abstention; results replicate across open-weight families; textual uncertainty is easier to detect than visual; closing the output gap likely requires output-stage interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the discrepancy between epistemic uncertainty in perception and explicit abstention in Vision-Language Models; provide robust benchmarks and a metric to force correct abstention behavior in uncertain situations.

Method: Introduce TRAPSBench, a procedurally generated video benchmark with 1,404 matched physics pairs where a targeted change renders the outcome undeterminable from visual evidence. Propose PECS (Penalized Epistemic Calibration Score) requiring correct answers when knowable and abstention when not. Evaluate 16 VLMs across five families; use linear probes to predict answerability from hidden states (AUROC up to 0.91). Apply a single-layer 'void' latent-direction to causally induce or suppress abstention. Replicate findings across Qwen, Gemma, and LLaVA open-weight models. Compare visual vs textual uncertainty.

Result: Best PECS ~0.292; spontaneous abstention is weak across models; perception of answerability is strong in hidden states (AUROC up to 0.91); latent-direction steering causally modulates abstention; findings replicate across open-weight model families; uncertainty is more detectable in text than in visual evidence by ~4x.

Conclusion: Perception-based signals of uncertainty exist, but the capacity to articulate abstention fails at the output layer. Bridging the representation-output gap will likely require targeted output-stage interventions and alignment between internal uncertainty signals and explicit abstention mechanisms.

Abstract: When visual evidence is occluded or chaotic, models should abstain. In this paper, we show that Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can internally distinguish when abstention is required, but fail to express it anyway. We introduce TRAPSBench, a procedurally generated video benchmark of 1,404 matched physics pairs in which a single targeted change renders the outcome undeterminable from the visual evidence. Furthermore, we introduce Penalized Epistemic Calibration Score (PECS), a new robust metric that requires models to both answer correctly when the outcome is knowable, and abstain when the outcome is not. Across 16 VLMs spanning five families, spontaneous restraint is poor: the best PECS is 0.292. The bottleneck is expression, not perception: linear probes decode answerability from hidden states at up to 0.91 AUROC across physics domains; steering a single-layer void direction causally induces or suppresses abstention. Our results replicate across three open-weight families (Qwen, Gemma, LLaVA). The failure is also more pronounced in visual than textual uncertainty: models detect textual impossibility about 4x more readily than missing visual evidence. Closing this representation--output gap likely requires output-stage interventions.

</details>


### [60] [A Controlled Study of Self-Supervised Image and Video Pretraining under Limited Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13183)
*Brunó B. Englert,Gijs Dubbelman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Under resource constraints, DINOv2-style pretraining yields the strongest overall performance; combining image SSL (DINOv2) with video SSL (VideoMAE) improves image tasks but can harm video tasks, indicating a tradeoff between semantic and geometric representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual foundation model training currently relies on very large data, compute, and architectures. It's unclear how SSL objectives perform under limited budgets, and whether joint image-video SSL yields benefits or tradeoffs across tasks.

Method: A controlled study with matched data, architecture, and compute budgets evaluating multiple SSL objectives (contrastive, reconstruction, feature-prediction, diffusion) for both standalone image/video SSL and joint image-video SSL. Evaluation on diverse image and video understanding tasks.

Result: DINOv2-style pretraining provides the strongest overall performance under limited resources. Augmenting DINOv2 with VideoMAE improves image classification and segmentation, but degrades video tracking and camera-pose estimation, revealing a tradeoff between semantic and geometric representation learning.

Conclusion: Combining image and video SSL objectives can be beneficial in resource-limited settings, but current approaches need methods that better balance semantic, temporal, and geometric cues to avoid harming certain video tasks.

Abstract: Visual foundation models are a cornerstone of image and video understanding but typically require large amounts of data and computation. The current scale required for pretraining visual foundation models may be unsustainable or unnecessary, and significant benefits arise when effective models can be obtained with fewer resources. To better understand how self-supervised learning (SSL) objectives behave under resource constraints, we conduct a controlled study of image and video SSL objectives under matched data, architecture, and compute budgets. We compare contrastive, reconstruction, feature-prediction, and diffusion objectives and evaluate both standalone and jointly trained image-video SSL formulations across a diverse set of image and video understanding tasks. Our results show that DINOv2-style pretraining consistently provides the strongest overall performance under limited resources. Furthermore, combining DINOv2 with video SSL objectives such as VideoMAE substantially improves image classification and segmentation performance, but degrades video tracking and camera-pose estimation performance, revealing an important tradeoff between semantic and geometric representation learning. These findings suggest that combining image and video SSL objectives can be beneficial in resource-limited settings, while highlighting the need for improved methods that better balance semantic, temporal, and geometric supervision.

</details>


### [61] [SketchSense: Learning to Interpret Imperfect Sketch Guidance for Image Inpainting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13186)
*Zian Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SketchSense introduces synchronous denoising of RGB and structure streams to interpret imperfect sketches for image inpainting, using bidirectional attention fusion, phrase-level semantic alignment, sketch-aware spatial regulation, and optional signed priors to improve restoration quality and structural fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imperfect sketches mix reliable global intent with local noise (crowded, displaced, incomplete strokes). Existing methods either treat sketches as fixed guidance or require pre-cleaned structures, failing to handle ambiguity during generation.

Method: A dual-stream framework that denoises RGB and structure jointly. Key components include: (1) Bidirectional Attention Fusion linking appearance and structure; (2) a phrase-level objective aligning semantic grounding across streams; (3) Sketch-Aware Spatial Regulation modulating attention and fusion based on local generation state; (4) an optional signed prior to bias feature/attention toward preserve vs. correct interpretation. Training likely uses paired sketch/image data with losses for reconstruction and structure consistency.

Result: Empirical results show substantial gains over existing methods in restoration quality and structural fidelity on natural and structurally complex imagery.

Conclusion: SketchSense provides a robust pathway to interpret imperfect sketch guidance, improving both the visual realism and the fidelity of recovered structure, with components that can be ablated to study their contributions.

Abstract: Sketch-guided image inpainting provides intuitive structural control, yet real sketches often mix reliable global intent with locally crowded, displaced, incomplete, or deliberately unconventional strokes. Existing approaches typically either retain the input sketch as a fixed condition throughout denoising or refine it into a clean structure before RGB synthesis. The former assumes uniformly reliable strokes and can propagate local errors throughout generation; the latter must resolve ambiguous structure before emerging appearance and semantic context become available. We propose SketchSense, a framework that interprets imperfect sketch guidance by synchronously denoising interacting RGB and structure streams. Bidirectional Attention Fusion couples appearance generation with structural recovery, producing a refined structure that exposes the model's evolving sketch interpretation. A phrase-level objective aligns the semantic grounding of the two streams. Sketch-Aware Spatial Regulation further adapts sketch use to local generation states by modulating attention and the fusion process, while an optional signed prior injects preserve-versus-correct intent into feature representations and attention behavior. Experiments on natural and structurally complex imagery show substantial gains over existing methods in both restoration quality and structural fidelity.

</details>


### [62] [Fidelity-Constrained Anchoring for Black-Box Denoisers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13194)
*Masaki Satoh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A fidelity-constrained anchoring method for black-box denoisers blends input and output images and selects the largest blend that respects a local fidelity constraint (PSNR or SSIM). It provides closed-form PSNR control under a local assumption and an iterative, tractable SSIM control; evaluated on DIV2K with Gaussian noise and real denoisers, achieving fidelity control while balancing denoising and naturalness, with SSIM offering more consistent results across noise levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable control of denoiser outputs without retraining a black-box model, ensuring fidelity to the original image while maintaining naturalness and denoising effectiveness.

Method: Linearly blend denoised image with input; choose maximum blending factor that satisfies a local fidelity constraint using PSNR or SSIM. PSNR control yields a closed-form solution under a local constant-blending assumption; SSIM control uses an inverse-SSIM formulation under the same assumption and solves it efficiently via iterative root finding. Evaluated on DIV2K with synthetic Gaussian noise and outputs from Real-ESRGAN and a non-local means denoiser.

Result: Anchoring provides effective fidelity control with a balance between denoising performance and naturalness (excess kurtosis of residuals). SSIM-based anchoring yields more consistent behavior across noise levels than PSNR-based anchoring.

Conclusion: A practical, computation-efficient framework to gate black-box denoisers by fidelity constraints, with SSIM-based anchoring offering more robust performance across varying noise conditions; PSNR anchoring provides a closed-form, fast alternative under the local-blending assumption.

Abstract: We propose a fidelity-constrained framework that anchors the output of a black-box denoiser to its input without retraining and with little additional computation. The method linearly blends the denoised image with the input and selects the maximum blending factor that satisfies a prescribed local fidelity constraint using Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR) or Structural Similarity Index (SSIM). For PSNR control, a closed-form solution is obtained under a local constant-blending assumption. For SSIM control, we derive a tractable formulation based on inverse SSIM under the same assumption and solve it efficiently using iterative root finding. Experiments on DIV2K images with synthetic Gaussian noise and outputs from Real-ESRGAN and a non-local means denoiser show that the proposed anchoring strategy provides effective fidelity control while balancing denoising performance and statistical naturalness, as measured by the excess kurtosis of residual noise. In particular, SSIM-based anchoring yields more consistent behavior across noise levels than PSNR-based anchoring.

</details>


### [63] [HPSD: Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation for Text-Image-to-Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13205)
*Jiazi Bu,Pengyang Ling,Yujie Zhou,Yibin Wang,Yuhang Zang,Xuanlang Dai,Shengyuan Ding,Tianyi Wei,Xiaohang Zhan,Jiaqi Wang,Tong Wu,Dahua Lin,Xingang Pan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation (HPSD) enables a single TI2V model to self-distill privileged TI2V priors into its base T2V capability by letting the model function as both teacher and student under different conditions. The teacher uses TI2V with a high-quality first frame and enhanced prompt, while the student runs the base T2V with a vanilla prompt. The student inherits off-policy teacher trajectory anchors, refines toward its policy, and finally uses velocity-level supervision on self-generated roll-outs, yielding improvements in both T2V and TI2V.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the pitfalls of traditional self-distillation in TI2V systems—off-policy supervision from fixed teacher endpoints and state- or condition-mismatch in on-policy methods—by integrating privileged TI2V priors with precise, policy-corrective supervision within a single model.

Method: Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation (HPSD): a single TI2V model operates as teacher (TI2V mode with high-quality first frame and enhanced prompt) and student (base T2V mode with vanilla prompt). The student inherits anchor points from the teacher’s trajectories, locally refines them toward its own policy, and receives velocity-level supervision on the self-generated roll-outs to align the policy with its current state. This combines off-policy anchors with on-policy policy corrections.

Result: Extensive experiments show that HPSD significantly improves T2V generation quality and yields notable TI2V gains, indicating strengthened base generation ability as a byproduct of the distillation.

Conclusion: HPSD offers a robust, unified self-distillation paradigm that leverages the teacher’s privileged priors while preserving precise policy correction within a single model, enhancing both base T2V and TI2V capabilities and potentially generalizing to TI2V frameworks.

Abstract: Text-Image-to-Video (TI2V) models are an emerging unified architecture, where a single model simultaneously supports text-to-video (T2V) and image-to-video (I2V) generation. Given a high-quality first frame or a detailed textual prompt, TI2V models unlock substantially better visual quality than their T2V mode, raising a natural question: can the capability elicited by such privileged conditions be internalized into the model's own base generation ability? A common approach toward this goal is model self-distillation. However, the most straightforward solution, supervised fine-tuning, follows an off-policy strategy: its supervision is confined to teacher-generated endpoints from a fixed offline distribution rather than student-visited states, lacking precise correction tailored to the evolving policy. Recent on-policy distillation methods instead suffer from condition-state mismatch, where supervision is steered toward the given first frame instead of the student's actual content, misleading the correction. To achieve self-distillation that absorbs the teacher's privileged prior while retaining precise policy correction, in this work, we propose Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation (HPSD), a novel self-distillation framework where a single TI2V model acts as both teacher and student under different conditions: the teacher operates in TI2V mode with a high-quality first frame and an enhanced prompt, while the student runs in the base T2V mode with only the vanilla prompt. Specifically, the student inherits off-policy teacher trajectory points as anchors, locally refines them toward its own policy, and finally receives velocity-level supervision on these self-generated roll-outs. Extensive experiments demonstrate that HPSD significantly improves T2V performance while also delivering notable TI2V gains, effectively strengthening the model's base generation ability.

</details>


### [64] [NARU: A Benchmark for NARrative Evolution and Cultural Nuance Understanding in Japanese Extreme Long Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13210)
*Yuheng Huang,Jianlang Chen,Jiayang Song,Hua Qi,Aza Kai,Vincent Markert,Edison Marrese-Taylor,Jianjun Zhao,Lei Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A new benchmark, NARU, evaluates Narrative evolution and Reasoning on cultural Understanding in Japanese long-form video. It includes 1,481 questions across 155 videos (146.8 hours), with a hierarchical memory-based annotation pipeline and native-speaker verification, revealing gaps in long-range narrative integration and culturally grounded reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks poorly assess joint capabilities for long-range narrative understanding and cultural reasoning in high-context, non-English media. There is a need for a dedicated Japanese long-form video benchmark to drive multimodal language models.

Method: A hierarchical memory-based annotation pipeline converts raw video into structured event, narrative, and cultural annotations. Questions are generated via task-oriented synthesis and iterative shortcut removal. Two native-speaker verification stages involve 68 annotators. Evaluations span eight model configurations.

Result: The study uncovers substantial limitations in long-range narrative integration and culturally grounded reasoning across model configurations, demonstrating the benchmark's effectiveness at exposing persistent gaps in current models.

Conclusion: NARU offers a systematic testing ground for developing multilingual, multimodal LLMs capable of reliably interpreting long-form, high-context video, and highlights the need for advances in narrative-grounded and culture-aware reasoning.

Abstract: Long-form video understanding encompasses tasks that go beyond retrieving isolated events, including tracking an evolving narrative and interpreting social meaning that may remain implicit. However, existing benchmarks rarely evaluate these capabilities jointly, particularly in high-context, non-English media. To address this gap, we introduce NARU, a benchmark designed to evaluate Narrative evolution and Reasoning on cultural Understanding in Japanese long-form video. NARU consists of 1,481 questions grounded in 155 videos totaling 146.8 hours, spanning four narrative and five cultural dimensions. To construct the benchmark at this scale, we propose a hierarchical memory-based annotation pipeline that transforms raw video into structured event, narrative, and cultural annotations, then generates questions via task-oriented synthesis and iterative shortcut removal. The construction process includes two native-speaker verification stages involving 68 annotators. Evaluations across eight model configurations reveal substantial limitations in both long-range narrative integration and culturally grounded reasoning. By exposing these persistent gaps, NARU offers a systematic testing ground for developing MLLMs capable of reliably interpreting long-form, high-context video.

</details>


### [65] [UniCon-Former: Unified Convolution Transformer is All You Need for Hand Gesture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13217)
*Mallika Garg,Debashis Ghosh,Pyari Mohan Pradhan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A unified CNN–Transformer model (UniCon-Former) for dynamic hand gesture recognition that injects convolution projections at transformer stages to form pyramidal, multi-scale features, achieving state-of-the-art results with fewer parameters and MACs on NVGesture and Briareo.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To combine CNNs' local feature extraction with transformers' global context while reducing redundancy and computation, enabling efficient, high-resolution, multi-scale gesture recognition.

Method: At the start of each transformer stage, apply convolution projections to downsample the input to the transformer block, creating a pyramidal structure within each stage. The model learns both local and global representations in a unified framework and is evaluated on NVGesture and Briareo datasets.

Result: State-of-the-art results on NVGesture and Briareo with fewer parameters and MACs compared to vanilla transformers.

Conclusion: UniCon-Former effectively balances local and global feature learning for dynamic hand gesture recognition, delivering robustness and efficiency and reducing resource usage while maintaining high performance.

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) capture local features efficiently but struggle with global context due to their limited receptive field. On the other hand, transformers effectively capture global dependencies through self-attention but suffer from high redundancy and computational costs. Thus, to leverage the advantages of both CNNs and transformers, we propose a unified model (UniCon-Former) that aims to provide robust and efficient performance on dynamic hand gesture recognition. The unified approach helps the model to learn both local and global features. At the beginning of each transformer stage, the convolution projections help in decreasing the dimension of the input vectors of the transformer block. This creates a pyramidal structure at each transformer stage. These features enable the UniCon-Former to reduce resource usage than vanilla transformers, making it flexible for learning multi-scale and high-resolution features, which is required in hand gesture recognition. We have performed experiments with NVGesture and Briareo datasets and achieved state-of-the-art results with fewer parameters and MACs.

</details>


### [66] [Reliability analysis for BraTS-GoAT segmentation: a controlled robustness study of deep-ensemble uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13223)
*Riya Deepak Shet,Le Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Ensembles improve calibration and error-detection under distribution shift for brain-tumor segmentation; official Dice 0.87; emphasizes reliability of uncertainty metrics over dominance of any single method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safety and reliability of medical image segmentation under input shift by evaluating both performance and uncertainty reliability (calibration, error detection).

Method: 5-fold cross-validated nnU-Net baseline with one held-out prediction per case and a 3-seed deep ensemble; per-region mask evaluation aggregated per case; robustness assessment via graded synthetic corruptions; comparison of single model vs ensemble on calibration and error detection.

Result: In-distribution: 3-seed ensemble modestly improves over the strong single model, especially calibration. Under shift: single-model confidence degrades while accuracy and calibration degrade; inter-member disagreement increases (≈25–33% above clean condition). Official validation: 5-fold ensemble achieves whole-tumor Dice 0.87. Generalization gap concentrates on harder regions with missed small satellite lesions. In synthetic study, ensemble disagreement is a more sensitive indicator of acquisition shift than single-model confidence; per-voxel error localization worsens with severity.

Conclusion: The work provides a rigorous, honest reliability comparison across uncertainty methods rather than claiming one method dominates; ensembles offer better calibration and error detection under shift, but small lesions remain challenging.

Abstract: Deep networks segment brain tumours accurately in-distribution, but can fail silently when the input differs from their training data. That risk is central to clinical deployment and is the premise of the BraTS-GoAT generalizability task. We ask not only how well a model segments, but whether its uncertainty knows when it is wrong. On BraTS-GoAT (Task 3) we train a 5-fold cross-validated nnU-Net baseline (one held-out prediction per case) and a 3-seed deep ensemble. Both are evaluated for calibration and error detection on a per-region relevant mask, aggregated per case. In-distribution the 3-seed ensemble improves modestly over the already strong single model on the same held-out split, with the clearest gain in calibration. The separation appears under shift. In a controlled robustness study using graded synthetic corruptions as a proxy for acquisition shift, the single model's confidence stays flat while its accuracy and calibration degrade. Inter-member disagreement instead rises steeply, about a quarter to a third above the clean condition, several times the single model's response. On the official validation leaderboard the 5-fold ensemble of those folds attains whole-tumour Dice 0.87. The generalization gap is concentrated on the harder regions, with a characteristic failure of missing small, satellite lesions on unseen cohorts. In the synthetic study, disagreement among the 3-seed members is a more sensitive case-level indicator of acquisition shift than single-model confidence. Its per-voxel error localisation weakens as severity grows. The contribution is a rigorous, honest reliability comparison rather than a claim that any one uncertainty method dominates.

</details>


### [67] [CoverPrune: Coverage-Driven Token Pruning for 3D VLMs via Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13226)
*Peng Ling,Yingda Yin,Lingting Zhu,Weikai Chen,Shengju Qian,Zeyu Hu,Xin Wang,Wenming Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes CoverPrune: a training-free, optimal-transport-based token pruning framework for 3D VLMs that preserves visual-evidence coverage over diversity, achieving state-of-the-art token efficiency with minimal performance loss; also offers a fast CoverPrune-Lite variant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Massive visual token counts in 3D VLMs cause inference bottlenecks; diversity-based pruning often loses representative tokens and disrupts multi-view consistency and geometry.

Method: Formulates token pruning as OT problem; introduces Feature-Spatial-Temporal transport cost and target capacity; Spatial-Guided Greedy Selection algorithm; covers variants CoverPrune and CoverPrune-Lite with local matching for efficiency; training-free.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple 3D visual-spatial reasoning benchmarks show state-of-the-art token efficiency while maintaining robust reasoning under aggressive pruning budgets.

Conclusion: Presents a paradigm shift from maximizing diversity to preserving visual evidence coverage in 3D VLM token pruning; provides practical, efficient, training-free solution with strong empirical validation.

Abstract: While 3D Vision-Language Models (3D VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable spatial reasoning capabilities, they suffer from massive visual token counts that create severe computational bottlenecks during inference. Existing token pruning methods primarily rely on diversity-based selection, discarding similar tokens to maximize dispersion. However, in 3D environments, this approach frequently drops representative prototype tokens in favor of outliers, breaking the multi-view consistencies and geometric structures essential for spatial reasoning. In this paper, we propose a paradigm shift for 3D VLM token pruning: from maximizing diversity to preserving visual evidence coverage. We introduce CoverPrune, a training-free framework that formulates inference-time token pruning as an Optimal Transport (OT) problem. To overcome the intractable combinatorial subset selection inherent in this formulation, we design the Feature-Spatial-Temporal (FST) transport cost and target capacity, along with an efficient Spatial-Guided Greedy Selection (SGS) algorithm to approximate the OT objective. Furthermore, we propose CoverPrune-Lite, an accelerated variant utilizing spatially structured local matching for minimal overhead. Extensive experiments across multiple 3D visual-spatial reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our methods achieve state-of-the-art token efficiency, maintaining robust reasoning performance even under highly aggressive pruning budgets. Visit our project website at https://github.com/Brucess/CoverPrune.

</details>


### [68] [Reasoning for Social Audio-Visual Question Answering: Where Do We Stand?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13239)
*Koen P. de Vries,Xavier Alameda-Pineda,Estefanía Talavera,Stéphane Lathuilière*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Three key findings on IntentBench for audio-visual social understanding: dataset is noisy (≈7% broken, ≈23% trivially answerable without video); Vanilla SFT baseline matches or outperforms reasoning methods at low cost; substantial priors can be learned from text alone, with captions sometimes matching video inputs. IntentBench-Prime and code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the reliability of chain-of-thought reasoning and current multimodal large language models on social understanding tasks; assess dataset quality and the value of video input vs text priors; establish strong baselines for future fine-tuning techniques.

Method: Quantitative audit of the IntentBench dataset to identify noisy/trivial items; construction of IntentBench-Prime by removing affected questions; empirical comparison across three benchmarks of three baselines: existing reasoning methods (CoT-based) vs Vanilla SFT; ablations comparing video input vs textual captions; cost assessments.

Result: IntentBench is noisy: ~7% broken questions and ~23% trivially answerable without video input; removing these yields IntentBench-Prime. Vanilla SFT baselines match or outperform existing CoT-based reasoning methods at a fraction of the cost. Text-only priors learned from the text modality (e.g., captions) can yield performance on par with Vanilla SFT, when video input is not essential.

Conclusion: Current multimodal LLMs exhibit limitations for social understanding due to dataset noise and reliance on priors from text; simple, cost-effective baselines can be as strong or stronger than complex reasoning methods. The work advocates dataset curation (IntentBench-Prime) and emphasizes the value of text-only baselines for evaluating fine-tuning approaches.

Abstract: Training Multimodal Large Language Models for audio-visual social understanding is a crucial step toward embodied social intelligence. Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has become the dominant approach, with HumanOmniV2 and its IntentBench benchmark as a prominent reference point. In this context, we report three findings. First, IntentBench is highly noisy: $\sim$7% of questions are broken and $\sim$23% are trivially answerable without the video input. We remove the affected questions and release Intentbench-Prime. Second, current reasoning approaches are expensive and surprisingly ineffective. A simple Vanilla SFT baseline matches or outperforms existing reasoning methods across three benchmarks at a fraction of the cost, establishing it as an essential baseline for evaluating novel fine-tuning techniques. Third, our analysis reveals that substantial priors can be learned solely from the text modality and that using a textual caption instead of the video yields performance on par with Vanilla SFT. These surprising findings reveal the limitations of current MLLMs when it comes to social understanding. IntentBench-Prime, Vanilla SFT model, and code are publicly available.

</details>


### [69] [GeoCache: Training-Free Acceleration of Multi-View Texture Diffusion via Geometric Delta Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13255)
*Haotang Li,Zhenyu Qi,Shaohan Henry Wang,Kebin Peng,Yutong Zhao,Zi Wang,Bo Liu,Huanrui Yang,Sen He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free, geometry-aware acceleration for multi-view diffusion that transfers per-step updates across views via anchor-view geometry, achieving faster denoising with maintained fidelity (up to >2x speedups) without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the substantial computational cost of repeated per-view denoiser evaluations in geometry-conditioned multi-view diffusion, where skipping denoising steps traditionally harms cross-view consistency and fidelity.

Method: Introduce GC, a training-free plugin that rotates a subset of anchor views and transports their geometry-aligned per-step updates to other views. Periodic full-view passes and sampler-consistent reconstruction control error accumulation; leverages existing position maps without retraining or architectural changes.

Result: GC delivers stronger speed–fidelity trade-offs than temporal caches and step reduction at operating points above 2x. Reported gains include 2.21x denoiser-loop speedup on Hunyuan3D-2.1 with MV-LPIPS 0.0293 and MV-PSNR 33.60 dB; highest fidelity among accelerated methods above 2x on that dataset. Similar gains in SyncMVD and MVPainter, with the lowest FLOPs and best fidelity among accelerated methods.

Conclusion: Cross-view geometry is a viable and effective acceleration axis for multi-view texture diffusion, enabling significant speedups without retraining or architectural changes.

Abstract: Geometry-conditioned multi-view diffusion enables high-quality 3D texture generation, but its repeated per-view denoiser evaluations introduce substantial computational cost. Existing training-free accelerators primarily exploit temporal redundancy by reusing computation across denoising steps. In multi-view texturing, however, skipping a step also removes the cross-view interaction that continually aligns different observations of the same surface, leading to rapidly degraded consistency and fidelity. Our analysis identifies a complementary source of redundancy: although intermediate features remain view-specific, geometrically corresponding surface points exhibit transferable evolution in their predicted clean signals. Based on this observation, we introduce \gc{}, a training-free plugin that evaluates a rotating subset of anchor views and transports their geometry-aligned per-step $\xz$ updates to the remaining views. Periodic full-view computation controls accumulated error, while sampler-consistent reconstruction preserves the denoising trajectory. \gc{} requires neither retraining nor architectural modification and uses the position maps already available in geometry-conditioned texturing pipelines. Across Hunyuan3D-2.1, SyncMVD, and MVPainter, \gc{} achieves a stronger speed--fidelity trade-off than temporal caches and step reduction at operating points above $2\times$. On Hunyuan3D-2.1, it delivers a $2.21\times$ denoiser-loop speedup with an MV-LPIPS of 0.0293 and an MV-PSNR of 33.60 dB, providing the best fidelity among all tested methods above $2\times$. The same transferred configuration reaches the highest speedup and lowest FLOPs on SyncMVD, while \gc{} achieves the lowest FLOPs and best fidelity among the accelerated methods on MVPainter. These results establish cross-view geometry as an effective acceleration axis for multi-view texture diffusion.

</details>


### [70] [How Good are Foundation Models in Longitudinal MRI Disease Progression Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13309)
*Wafa Al Ghallabi,Ritesh Thawkar,Sara Ghaboura,Omkar Thawakar,Numan Saeed,Dana Al Nuaimi,Ajnas Alkatheeri,Salman Khan,Fahad Shahbaz Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A time-aware, multi-view MRI benchmark for longitudinal radiology assessment, highlighting temporal-spatial reasoning gaps in vision-language models and providing a dataset and evaluation framework for progression tracking and localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiologic decision-making hinges on extracting changes over time across multiple views. Current vision-language benchmarks lack longitudinal, multi-view reasoning and structured localization, limiting clinical relevance.

Method: Assemble Time-Aware Multi-View MRI Benchmark: 3,920 expert-verified QA pairs from 890 patients over 3,200 longitudinal timepoints across seven cohorts (glioblastoma, neurodegeneration, vestibular schwannoma, brain metastases). Supports open-ended, MC, and binary formats. Tasks include locating regions of maximal change, characterizing progression across sequences and views, and providing structured guidance (boundaries, features, confounders). Evaluate 16 vision-language models to assess temporal alignment, change-direction recognition, and volumetric quantification; analyze impact of multi-view inputs on spatial localization and temporal reasoning.

Result: Findings show moderate temporal alignment but systematic failures in recognizing the direction of change and in volumetric quantification. Multi-view inputs improve spatial localization yet degrade temporal reasoning in compact architectures. The benchmark enables evaluation of progression tracking, interval change localization, and temporal ordering.

Conclusion: The Time-Aware MRI Benchmark constitutes a systematic framework for clinically relevant evaluation of longitudinal, multi-view MRI interpretation by LLMs, with publicly available code, splits, and data to foster development toward reliable progression assessment.

Abstract: Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) interpretation is fundamental to clinical decision-making, requiring radiologists to integrate multi-view anatomical planes across sequential timepoints while precisely localizing interval changes. However, existing vision-language benchmarks remain confined to single-timepoint, single-view interpretation, failing to capture the temporal-spatial reasoning essential to radiologic practice. We introduce the Time-Aware Multi-View MRI Benchmark, an evaluation framework unifying multi-view anatomical input, temporal reasoning across longitudinal scans, and structured localization guidance. The benchmark comprises 3,920 expert-verified question-answer pairs derived from 890 patients across over 3,200 longitudinal MRI timepoints, drawn from seven clinical cohorts covering glioblastoma, neurodegeneration, vestibular schwannoma, and brain metastases, in open-ended, multiple-choice, and binary formats, requiring models to identify anatomical regions of maximal change, characterize progression across sequences and views, and provide structured guidance specifying boundaries, imaging features, and confounders. Experiments across 16 vision-language models reveal moderate temporal alignment but systematic failure on change direction recognition and volumetric quantification, while multi-view inputs improve spatial localization yet degrade temporal reasoning in compact architectures. Our benchmark provides a systematic framework for evaluating progression tracking, interval change localization, and temporal ordering, which are essential for clinical deployment. Code, evaluation splits, and the dataset are available at: https://github.com/wafaAlghallabi/Time-Aware-MRI.

</details>


### [71] [AmalthAI: An Open-Source Computer Vision Platform for Cultural Heritage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13343)
*Christos Chatzisavvas,Stelios Alvanos,Efstratios Politis,Panagiotis Rigas,Thomas Pappas,Ioannis Giannoukos,Nikolaos Mitianoudis,Agata Ulanowska,Katarzyna Żebrowska,Nazarij Buławka,Christina Margariti,George Pavlidis,Chairi Kiourt,Anestis Koutsoudis,Vassilis Katsouros,George Ioannakis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: An open-source, self-hosted CV/ML platform (AmalthAI) enabling non-ML cultural heritage experts to train, validate, and review classification, segmentation, and object-detection models with scalable training via Kubeflow/Katib; includes Grad-CAM explanations and a vision-language model for textual localization; tested on a clay textile imprint dataset; code available at GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridging the gap between CV/ML pipelines and cultural heritage domain experts; address data custody concerns and the need for archaeologically meaningful, auditable results.

Method: Integrated platform with dataset management, training, and inference modules; uses Kubeflow and Katib for scalable training and HPO; Grad-CAM for localization; vision-language model to describe the attention region; self-hosted deployment for sensitive data; assessment with expert-driven segmentation and classification tasks on clay textile imprints.

Result: Demonstrated by expert-driven segmentation and classification model training/validation on a custom clay textile imprint dataset; provides implementation and shows feasibility for hypothesis testing; repository available.

Conclusion: AmalthAI offers an accessible, reproducible, and secure CV/ML workflow tailored for CH artifacts, lowering barriers for domain experts to leverage ML while maintaining data custody and interpretability; potential impact on CH analytics with room for expansion.

Abstract: Computer vision (CV) and machine learning (ML) offer new tools for cultural heritage (CH) artifact analysis, but the CV/ML pipeline remains largely inaccessible to CH domain experts, who lack the background to configure, train, or assess models. We present AmalthAI, an open-source CV platform that bridges this gap, enabling non-ML CH experts to independently produce and validate archaeologically meaningful findings. The interface covers dataset management, training, and inference for classification, segmentation, and object detection, with Kubeflow and Katib handling scalable training and hyperparameter search. Grad-CAM localizes the image region behind a prediction, and a vision-language model (VLM) adds a text description of it for expert review. Since archaeological data is often state-owned or rights-encumbered and cannot leave institutional custody, AmalthAI's self-hostable deployment ensures sensitive data is kept within premises. We test the platform on an archaeological use case built on a custom dataset of clay textile imprints, where CH experts trained and validated segmentation, and classification models for hypothesis testing. We provide the implementation code at https://github.com/TEXTaiLES/AmalthAI.

</details>


### [72] [Sign Language Video Synthesis via Loss-Guided Multi-Expert GANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13368)
*Dingzhan Nong,Zhihao Ren,Ziqi Li,Tim Lo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multi-discriminator GAN for sign-language video synthesis with global/hand/head discriminators guiding corresponding generator branches, stabilized by a United Loss consensus (10% weight toward ensemble), and a dual-pathway conv-transformer with AdaptiveFeatureFusion. Trained with an alternating three-mode schedule on a large 156GB dataset, achieving PSNR ~29.8–30.7 with modest VRAM, enabling consumer hardware deployment. Ablations are ongoing; system showcased at HK Frontier Technology Summit 2025.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhance sign-language video synthesis by enabling implicit feature specialization across semantic regions (global, hands, head) while stabilizing GAN training and preserving detail, to improve communication accessibility for individuals with hearing impairments.

Method: A loss-guided multi-expert GAN with three specialized discriminators (global, hand, head) each guiding a corresponding expert branch in the generator. A United Loss consensus regularizes discriminators toward the ensemble average with 0.1 weight. Each branch uses a dual-pathway convolutional-transformer architecture with learnable AdaptiveFeatureFusion. Training uses an alternating three-mode schedule: discriminator updates, holistic generation, and branch-specialized generation. Evaluation on a custom 156GB dataset with filtered test samples. Parameter scales: 0.2B and 1.3B variants; inference VRAM 1.5 GB and 8 GB.

Result: The 0.2B-parameter model achieves 29.8 PSNR; the 1.3B-parameter model achieves 30.7 PSNR. Inference VRAM footprints are 1.5 GB and 8 GB respectively, enabling deployment on consumer-grade hardware. Full ablation studies are ongoing due to long 2–3 month single-GPU training cycles.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates competitive PSNR and practical deployment feasibility via targeted feature specialization and stabilized multi-discriminator training. However, comprehensive ablations are pending, as are further evaluations on generalization and efficiency.

Abstract: This preliminary technical report presents a framework for sign language video synthesis using a loss-guided multi-expert Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) to enhance communication for individuals with hearing impairments. Three specialized discriminators -- global, hand, and head -- each guide a corresponding expert branch in the generator toward a distinct visual region, enabling implicit feature specialization without explicit diversity losses. To stabilize this multi-discriminator system, whose early-phase training otherwise exhibits chaotic dynamics, we introduce a United Loss consensus mechanism that regularizes each discriminator toward the ensemble average at a 10% weight. Each branch further adopts a dual-pathway convolutional-transformer design with learnable AdaptiveFeatureFusion, balancing the stability of convolutions against the detail of windowed self-attention. The generator is trained using an alternating three-mode schedule (discriminator, holistic generation, branch-specialized generation). On a custom 156GB dataset with a filtered test set that removes easy and repetitive samples, our 0.2B-parameter variant achieves 29.8 PSNR and the 1.3B-parameter variant achieves 30.7 PSNR, with inference VRAM footprints of 1.5 GB and 8 GB respectively, enabling deployment on consumer-grade hardware. Full ablation studies remain ongoing due to the 2-3 month training cycle on a single GPU. The system was showcased at the 2025 Hong Kong Frontier Technology Summit.

</details>


### [73] [Reconstructing Historical Manuscripts through MSI: The Potential of Contrast in Assessing Image Quality and Legibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13381)
*Anna Breger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Contrast-based image quality measures closely track expert legibility judgments and full-reference IQA for reconstructed manuscripts, outperforming general IQA methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of evaluating manuscript restoration quality when expert annotations are scarce and readability is subjective.

Method: Two experiments on publicly available multi-spectral manuscript datasets; compare contrast-based quality measures (potential contrast, contrast-to-noise ratio) against general IQA measures and full-reference references, using expert scores and full-reference metrics as evaluations.

Result: Potential contrast shows the strongest correlation with expert ratings; contrast-to-noise ratio shows the strongest agreement with full-reference metrics; overall, contrast-based measures outperform general IQA measures.

Conclusion: Contrast-based IQA metrics are promising objective indicators of manuscript legibility and restoration quality, aiding evaluation when expert labels are limited.

Abstract: Digital restoration of historical manuscript images aims to improve readability while preserving the authenticity of cultural heritage documents. However, evaluating quality of restored manuscripts remains challenging, where readability is often subjective and expert annotations are scarce. This study investigates the suitability of contrast-based image quality measures to assess quality and legibility of reconstructed manuscript images from multi-spectral imaging. Two experiments were conducted with publicly-available data sets, facilitating manual quality scores by experts and full-reference image quality measures as reference evaluations. The results show that potential contrast achieves the highest correlation with expert ratings, while contrast-to-noise ratio demonstrates the strongest agreement with full-reference quality measures. Overall, contrast-based measures consistently outperform general image quality measures, demonstrating their potential as objective indicators of manuscript legibility and reconstruction quality.

</details>


### [74] [When Is a Task Vector Enough? An Empirical Theory of Implicit Multimodal ICL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13385)
*Jiaqian Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes the Selection–Realization Hypothesis for implicit multimodal in-context learning: demonstrations induce a compact family of internal changes, from which the query selects; the model’s computation constrains how the selected change is implemented. A static task vector works when the induced change is largely shared across queries; more expressive interventions are useful for query-specific or distributed structure. Validated on controlled multimodal tasks and natural VQA, enabling cost-aware method selection without test performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify when demonstrations can be compressed into a simple task vector versus requiring richer, query-dependent interventions in implicit multimodal in-context learning; provide an empirical theory to guide method design and selection.

Method: Propose the Selection–Realization Hypothesis: demonstrations induce a compact family of internal changes; the query selects among them, with the computation constraining implementability. Evaluate on controlled multimodal tasks with varying query dependence but fixed primitives/prompt format; contrast correct demonstrations with matched counterfactuals to assess the structure of explicit M-ICL and its predictive power regarding intervention behavior; extend analysis to natural VQA benchmarks to support cost-aware method selection.

Result: Static task-vector success correlates with how much demonstration-induced change is shared across queries. Additional intervention complexity is beneficial when explicit M-ICL contains query-specific or distributed structure that a local additive shift cannot recover. Findings extend to natural VQA benchmarks and enable cost-aware method selection without access to test performance.

Conclusion: Provides a unified empirical theory of when demonstrations can be compressed into a task vector and when a more expressive intervention is warranted; guides design and method choice for implicit multimodal in-context learning and informs cost-aware evaluation.
