Engineer features from spectral variance across methods, wavelength intensity ratios, and environmental factors (temperature, humidity). Apply PCA to retain >90% variance in the first 5–7 components. Train three independent XGBoost-based models for (i) ripening method classification and (ii/iii) quantitative estimation of ripening progression and remaining shelf life.

Result: PCA captures >90% of spectral variance within 5–7 components. For mango, classification accuracy is 95% with carbide-class recall 0.67; for banana, accuracy is 81% with carbide-class recall 0.74.

Conclusion: The proposed non-invasive framework effectively differentiates carbide-ripened from naturally/ethephon-ripened fruit and estimates ripening progression and shelf life. It demonstrates promise for safe fruit ripening monitoring, though generalization to more varieties and real-world conditions warrants further validation.

Abstract: Significant health risks are associated with the illegal, yet commonly practiced use of industrial-grade Calcium Carbide (CaC2) for ripening climacteric fruits like mango and banana, which leaves behind trace residues of arsenic and phosphorus. To address this, the proposed study explores a novel, non-invasive multispectral framework for distinguishing safely ripened fruits (naturally ripened and ethephon-induced) from calcium carbide-ripened samples, while also estimating their ripening progression (in percentage) and remaining shelf life (in days). The spectral profiles of mango (Mangifera indica) and banana (Musa acuminata) at 18 discrete wavelengths in the visible-near infrared (NIR) range (410 nm - 940 nm) are studied using the AS7265x spectral triad sensor. CaC2-treated samples exhibit sharper spectral intensity drops in the visible region, consistent with accelerated chlorophyll degradation and carotenoid development. To characterize these physiological changes, the feature engineering strategy integrates inter-method spectral variance, intensity ratios at distinct wavelengths, and environmental parameters including temperature and humidity. Dimensionality reduction using Principal Component Analysis (PCA) retains >90% of spectral variance within the first 5-7 components. The resulting feature set is used to train three independent eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost)-based learning algorithms for ripening method classification, along with quantitative estimation of remaining shelf life and ripening progression. A classification accuracy of 95% along with carbide class recall of 0.67 is observed for mango samples, while the model achieves an accuracy of 81% and carbide class recall of 0.74 for banana. This instrumentation and data-driven approach demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed non-invasive framework.

</details>


### [149] [Learning Discrete Decisions for MIPs with Constraint-Aware Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13079)
*Vincenzo Di Vito,Mehdi Taghizadeh,Deepjyoti Deka,Kaarthik Sundar,Ferdinando Fioretto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based, problem-agnostic framework (CGD) for mixed-integer optimization that learns the discrete decisions via graph diffusion and uses a training-free feasibility projection in reverse diffusion; after discrete decisions, solves the continuous subproblem; achieves large speedups and improved feasibility/quality on tested tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MIPs are hard due to combinatorial constraints and coupling of discrete/continuous decisions; need scalable, generalizable methods that can produce feasible, high-quality solutions faster than exact MINLP solvers.

Method: Graph-based generative diffusion model to sample discrete decisions; integrate a projection operator (training-free) into the reverse diffusion to enforce feasibility; decouple to a continuous subproblem solvable by standard solvers; CGD is modular and requires appropriate projection operators per problem.

Result: Demonstrates substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality relative to learning-based baselines; achieves up to 425x speedup over state-of-the-art MINLP solvers, on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization.

Conclusion: CGD provides a flexible, scalable framework for a broad class of MIPs; the combination of diffusion-based discrete generation and feasibility projection yields effective solutions; potential applicability across domains with suitable projection operators.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel learning-based approach to approximately solve instances of mixed-integer optimization problems. These problems are computationally challenging, as they require jointly determining discrete and continuous decisions while satisfying complex combinatorial constraints. The proposed method relies on a graph-based generative diffusion model that learns the discrete component of mixed-integer optimization problems while integrating a training-free feasibility projection operator directly into the reverse diffusion process to steer intermediate samples toward the feasible set throughout generation. Once the discrete decisions are generated, the remaining optimization reduces to a continuous problem that can be solved efficiently (relative to the original problem) using existing numerical methods. The resulting framework named Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD), is problem-agnostic and can accommodate a broad class of mixed-integer optimization problems through suitable projection operators. We evaluate CGD on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization, demonstrating substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality over learning-based baselines while achieving speedups of up to $425\times$ over state-of-the-art numerical solvers for MINLPs.

</details>


### [150] [Sampling Luck Masquerades as Allocation Gain: Auditing Test-Time Budget Allocation for Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13087)
*Jinhyung Bae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Non-uniform budget allocation inflates in-sample gains for NCO solvers; correcting measurement reveals true gains mainly under distribution shift (~11-12% in best-of-k); small exploratory gains with probing; authors provide a bias-correction method, reporting checklist, and release data/code/preregistration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether a fixed total budget of samples, allocated non-uniformly across instances, yields genuine performance gains for neural combinatorial optimization solvers and assess measurement bias.

Method: Compare oracle allocation on stored in-sample and out-of-sample data across three solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100; perform pre-registered confirmatory experiments; use negative control; test under distribution shift (mixed uniform/clustered instances); assess signal acquisition cost; propose correction procedure and reporting checklist; release data/code and preregistration record; also test an exploratory policy with a cost.

Result: In-distribution: measured gains are inflated; true gain near zero; out-of-sample: gains indistinguishable from zero. Under distribution shift: held-out statistics improve best-of-k by 11.5% (AM) and 12.0% (SymNCO); negative control shows -0.3%. Correction preserves real gains; a 20-sample probing strategy yields 3.4% and 4.6%; improves over frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points.

Conclusion: Measurement bias from allocation budgets exists; corrections and preregistration are essential; allocation guided by held-out statistics can yield meaningful gains under distribution shift; encourages transparent reporting and provides resources (data/code/preregistration).

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) solvers report the best of many sampled solutions per instance, and the sample count is, by convention, identical for every instance. Whether a non-uniform allocation of a fixed total budget would buy anything has not been measured. We measure it, and we audit the measurement itself.
  First, on in-distribution workloads the allocation headroom is not detectable. Across three pretrained solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100, an oracle allocation computed and evaluated on the same stored samples reports a 2.2-2.6% gain with intervals excluding zero; measured out of sample the same gain is indistinguishable from zero (0.457, 0.015, -0.512 percent). Following the customary in-sample procedure, all three solvers would have supported a published 2%-level gain that does not exist. We calibrate this bias against an instance-wise null in which the true gain is zero by construction; over the ranges we test it does not shrink with more samples or more instances.
  Second, the same correction that removes the phantom gains preserves a real one. Under distribution shift (a workload mixing uniform and clustered instances), a pre-registered confirmatory experiment finds that allocation guided by held-out sample statistics improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM, primary endpoint; 95% CI [7.4, 19.7]) and 12.0% (SymNCO, replication) at equal evaluation budget, with the signal-acquisition cost not charged; a pre-registered negative control (POMO, an order of magnitude more robust to shift) shows -0.3% [-0.7, 0.24]. The gain exceeds a frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points [1.9, 7.7]. An exploratory policy charging a 20-sample probe against the same budget retains 3.4% (AM) and 4.6% (SymNCO).
  We give a correction procedure and a reporting checklist, and release all data, code, and the pre-registration record.

</details>


### [151] [FlowLOB: Efficient and Controllable Limit Order Book Generation with Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13096)
*Zhuohan Wang,Andreea Bacalum,Ollie Olby,Carmine Ventre,Namid Stillman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowLOB is a conditional flow-matching model that generates LOB trajectories. It achieves high realism with low sampling cost, generalizes to unseen instruments (zero-shot), and supports controllable scenario manipulation, outperforming baselines (two learned, two agent-based) and diffusion under equal budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LOB simulators need to combine realism, sampling efficiency, controllable scenario generation, and generalization beyond training instruments; existing agent-based and deep generative models provide partial coverage.

Method: Train a conditional flow-matching generator (FlowLOB) on HKEX symbols at 0.1s, 1s, and 10s frequencies in tick-relative representation; compare against a diffusion model using identical data, architecture, and budget; sample with the same fixed-step ODE solvers; evaluate realism across distributional metrics, counterfactual controllability via a tail-focused test, and zero-shot transfer to a held-out symbol; perform ablations on architecture and learning rate.

Result: FlowLOB achieves best quality with only 10 ODE steps; diffusion requires many more steps to reach similar fidelity. At the efficient operating point, FlowLOB surpasses two learned and two agent-based baselines in most distributional metrics at fine sampling frequencies. It satisfies the counterfactual controllability criterion in most settings and transfers zero-shot to a held-out instrument. Ablations show sensitivity to network architecture and learning rate.

Conclusion: Conditional flow-matching is an effective and efficient approach for LOB trajectory generation, delivering realistic, controllable, and generalizable simulations suitable for practical deployment, with competitive performance against diffusion under equal budgets.

Abstract: Limit order book (LOB) simulators are most useful to practitioners when they combine realistic market dynamics, computationally efficient sampling, controllable scenario generation, and the ability to generalize beyond the instruments seen during training---properties that existing agent-based and deep generative simulators provide only partially. We present \textbf{FlowLOB}, a conditional \textbf{flow}-matching generator of \textbf{LOB} trajectories, trained on multiple Hong Kong Exchange (HKEX) symbols at three sampling frequencies ($0.1$s, $1$s, $10$s) in tick-relative representation that transfers to unseen instruments. Because flow and diffusion models admit a common formulation, we train both with identical data, architecture, and budget, and sample both through the same fixed-step ODE solvers, yielding a controlled comparison of sampling efficiency and fidelity. Flow matching attains its best quality with only $10$ ODE-solver steps, whereas diffusion needs many more function evaluations to approach the same fidelity. At this efficient operating point, FlowLOB improves realism over baselines, two learned and two agent-based models, in most distributional metrics at the two finer sampling frequencies. We evaluate counterfactual controllability with a distributional test that asks whether changing a scenario condition moves the generated statistic toward the corresponding real tail regime; FlowLOB satisfies this criterion in most tested settings. Both realism and control effects transfer zero-shot on a held-out symbol. We additionally conduct ablation studies on the network architecture and the learning rate.

</details>


### [152] [Branch and Bound for Relational Verification of Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13118)
*Kota Fukuda,Zhenya Zhang,Guanqin Zhang,Jianjun Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SaBRe introduces a branch-and-bound framework for verifying relational neural-network specifications by splitting relational neurons, guided by a dual-based selection strategy to maximize refinement, achieving improved efficiency and solved-instance counts over baselines on 817 problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR, and GTSRB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational specifications (e.g., global robustness) are essential for safety-critical CPS with AI components, but existing abstraction-based verifications are incomplete and may yield false alarms; effective refinement is needed to make reliable verification scalable.

Method: A branch-and-bound framework that (1) splits relational neurons instead of single neurons, and (2) uses a dual-formulation-driven strategy to select the most impactful relational neuron for refinement. Evaluated on 817 verification problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR, and GTSRB.

Result: SaBRe outperforms baseline methods in both the number of solved instances and verification efficiency across the tested benchmarks, demonstrating the practicality and effectiveness of relational-neuron splitting and dual-guided refinement for relational specification verification.

Conclusion: Relational-neuron based refinement within a branch-and-bound framework provides an effective and scalable approach to verifying relational specifications for neural networks in safety-critical CPS, outperforming previous approximation-based methods.

Abstract: Verification of neural networks against relational specifications, such as global robustness, is crucial for safety-critical applications of cyber-physical systems (CPS), given their increasing adoption of AI components. Compared to simple trace properties (e.g., local robustness), verifying relational specifications requires reasoning about the relationship between multiple network inferences, which brings significant technical challenges. Existing research has explored abstraction techniques based on sound and convex over-approximation of neural network outputs; however, since these approaches are inherently incomplete and may raise false alarms, they further underscore the need of effective abstraction refinement.
  In this paper, we propose a branch-and-bound (BaB) framework to mitigate the issue, which iteratively splits the problem until all sub-problems are verified. Specifically, our BaB framework features splitting of relational neurons rather than individual neurons as prior works do, and as the core of our technique, we devise a relational neuron selection strategy based on the dual formulation of the verification problem, which allows us to efficiently select the (most likely) optimal relational neuron that maximizes the refinement brought by problem splitting. We evaluate SaBRe on 817 verification problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR and GTSRB. The results show that SaBRe outperforms different baseline approaches, in terms of the number of solved instances and verification efficiency, which demonstrates the effectiveness of our proposed techniques.

</details>


### [153] [ProME: Prototype-Margin Environments with Repair-Aware Selection for Group-Robust Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13190)
*Qianqian Wang,Yunshan Li,Dawei Huang,Wenwu Gong,Lili Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProME introduces endogenous environments via prototype-margin splitting to optimize worst-group accuracy without training-group labels, with a repair-aware selection process that aligns environment ranking to the deployed predictor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Group-robust learning without access to training-group labels is needed, as existing methods relying on reference models misalign with the deployed predictor and may fail on rare subpopulations.

Method: ERAS (Endogenous Repair-Aware Selection) via Prototype-Margin Environments: split prototype margins at the median to form approximately balanced environments along training trajectories; fit a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank predictors by worst-group validation accuracy; provide a theoretical bound on worst-case risk across inferred environments under a fixed predictor and partition, which transfers to oracle groups under an alignment condition.

Result: ProME yields the highest average worst-group accuracy among methods with the same group-label access; prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples; classifier repair reshapes candidate evaluation; theoretical bound on worst risk that transfers under alignment.

Conclusion: Aligning both environment construction and model selection to the deployed predictor enables robust performance on rare subpopulations, with theoretical guarantees and strong empirical results.

Abstract: Group-robust learning is crucial for maintaining accuracy on rare subpopulations when training-group labels are unavailable. However, existing methods often infer environments from a separate reference model and select representations before fitting the classifier used at deployment, leaving both decisions misaligned with the deployed predictor. In this work, we formulate group robustness without training-group labels as the endogenous environments with repair-aware selection (ERAS) problem, and propose ProME (Prototype-Margin Environments) to align both decisions with the deployed predictor. ProME splits prototype margins at their median to construct approximately balanced environments along the training trajectory, and fits a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank the resulting predictors by validation worst-group accuracy. We theoretically bound the worst risk across the inferred environments for a fixed predictor and partition, showing that this bound transfers to the oracle groups under an explicit alignment condition. Extensive experiments show that prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples, classifier repair reshapes candidate evaluation, and ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among the compared methods with the same group-label access.

</details>


### [154] [Beyond Simulated Benchmarks: Evaluating Motion Representations for Fall Detection Under Real-World Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13197)
*Timilehin B. Aderinola,Ilaria D'Ascanio,Luca Palmerini,Lorenzo Chiari,Jochen Klenk,Clemens Becker,Brian Caulfield,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Under scarce real-world data, interval-based representations deliver strongest real-world fall detection, while kernel/foundation models overfit simulated data; symbolic representations with physically-grounded impact cues offer robust generalization under scarcity and domain shift (with some precision trade-offs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world falls are rare, leading to data scarcity; simulated benchmarks often overestimate performance and fail to generalize; need to assess how representation choices affect robustness and transferability.

Method: Systematic evaluation of accelerometer-based representations (interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, foundation models) using FallAllD (simulated) and FARSEEING (real-world); include a lightweight symbolic representation with impact descriptors; experiments include cross-validation, data scarcity, and cross-dataset transfer.

Result: Highly parameterised kernel & foundation models perform well on simulated data but degrade under scarcity and domain shift; interval-based representation yields strongest absolute real-world performance; augmented symbolic representation with physically-grounded impact descriptors shows minimal degradation under domain shift and preserves detection under extreme scarcity, though with lower precision.

Conclusion: Evaluation beyond simulations is essential; representation choice critically affects deployability under real-world data scarcity; combining interval-like robustness with physically-grounded symbolic cues offers a practical path for robust fall detection in real-world deployments.

Abstract: Falls are a major health concern for older adults, and wearable sensors have been widely explored for detecting falls and enabling timely intervention. However, real-world falls are extremely rare: collecting 100 of them requires an estimated 100,000 days of monitoring, resulting in severely limited labelled data for training machine learning models. Consequently, many approaches rely on simulated datasets, often reporting high laboratory performance but limited real-world generalisation. We present a systematic evaluation of motion representations for wearable fall detection under real-world data scarcity. Using accelerometer signals, we compare interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation model representations. As an interpretable baseline, we additionally investigate a lightweight symbolic representation that converts short motion segments into symbolic sentences augmented with physically-grounded impact descriptors. Experiments use FallAllD, a simulated falls dataset, and FARSEEING, a clinically verified real-world falls dataset. Through cross-validation, controlled data scarcity, and cross-dataset transfer, we examine how representation choices affect robustness under realistic deployment. Our results reveal that highly parameterised kernel and foundation models excel on simulated data but degrade severely under both data scarcity and domain shift. Although the interval-based representation achieves the strongest absolute real-world performance, augmenting a symbolic representation with physically-grounded impact descriptors yields the smallest degradation under domain shift and retains detection sensitivity under extreme scarcity, albeit at lower precision. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating beyond simulated benchmarks and show that representation choice is critical for deployable fall detection given the scarcity of real-world data.

</details>


### [155] [TANGCO: Learning Topology-Aware Capacity Allocation for Overload-driven Cascading Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13212)
*Orkun Irsoy,Leman Akoglu,Osman Yagan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Topology-aware neural policy (TANGCO) optimizes node capacity allocations to resist cascading failures, achieving robust, transferable improvements over hand-designed heuristics with scalable training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cascading failures arise when loads exceed node capacities and redistribute to neighbors; with a fixed total capacity budget, finding the optimal allocation is hard due to non-differentiable, piecewise-constant fail-survive outcomes. A topology-aware, learned policy promises better resilience and transferability across diverse networks.

Method: Introduce TANGCO, a graph neural network policy guided by a cascade simulator and trained with policy-gradient learning, augmented by a heuristic anchor. Evaluated on five synthetic graph families and five real networks (power, road, air, Internet topologies). Also compare with a non-GNN (free-vector) variant and a pre-trained version (TANGCO^pre) trained on synthetic graphs and deployed without per-network fine-tuning.

Result: The learned policy outperforms the best of four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains from 1.6% to 246%. Policies transfer within a family and partially across related topologies; TANGCO^pre matches per-network training when applied to unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size. Free-vector variants perform similarly to heuristics, underscoring the value of graph structure. Analysis yields an improved closed-form heuristic and clarifies regimes where topology-aware learning remains beneficial.

Conclusion: A topology-aware GNN policy can effectively allocate capacity to mitigate cascades, offering strong performance, transferability, and scalable training. Graph structure provides gains beyond naive search, and the approach illuminates when local risk suffices or topology-aware policies are essential.

Abstract: Networked systems, from power grids to traffic networks and cloud clusters, carry loads across nodes with limited capacity. A node whose load exceeds its capacity fails and sheds its load onto its neighbors, which can trigger a system-wide cascade. We study how to allocate a fixed capacity budget across nodes to resist these cascades under local load redistribution. The problem is difficult because no optimal allocation is known, and the fail-or-survive objective is non-differentiable and piecewise constant, so exact and gradient-based optimization methods do not directly apply. We introduce TANGCO (Topology-Aware Neural Graph-Guided Capacity Optimization), which uses a graph neural network policy trained through the cascade simulator with policy-gradient learning and a heuristic anchor. We evaluate TANGCO on five synthetic graph families and five real networks spanning power, road, air, and Internet topologies. The learned policy improves on the best of four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and in 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains ranging from 1.6% to 246%. The learned policies transfer to unseen graphs within a family and partially across related topologies, and TANGCO$^{pre}$, pre-trained on synthetic graphs, matches per-network training on unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size, and TANGCO$^{pre}$ allocates on a new network with no per-target training, matching the deployment cost of a hand-designed heuristic. Free-vector variants without the GNN, stay close to the heuristics, so the graph representation carries the gain beyond numerical search. Finally, analysis of the learned allocations identifies when local risk is sufficient, leads to an improved closed-form heuristic, and reveals the regimes where a topology-aware learned policy remains necessary.

</details>


### [156] [History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13215)
*Tianshuo Zhang,Xianglei Xing,Wenzhe Zhai,Jia Gao,He Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiLNN extends Lagrangian Neural Networks to forecast long-horizon dynamics from position-only data by using a history-based recurrent encoder to infer hidden velocities and adapt system parameters (mass matrix, potential, damping). A differentiable RK4 rollout enables end-to-end training with multi-step supervision and energy regularization, achieving improved long-term accuracy and energy consistency across conservative, dissipative, and variable-parameter systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accurate long-horizon prediction of mechanical systems from partial observations (positions only) by inferring latent velocities and time-varying physical parameters, addressing the limitations of Lagrangian NNs that require full state inputs and static parameters.

Method: Introduce History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks (HiLNN) with a recurrent encoder that processes history of positions to produce a latent context. This context reconstructs the unobserved initial velocity and modulates the mass matrix, potential energy, and damping coefficients of a structured Lagrangian system. Use a differentiable RK4 rollout for dynamics, and train end-to-end with multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization.

Result: Empirical evaluation on conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems shows HiLNN achieves superior long-term prediction accuracy and better energy-profile fidelity than state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: Incorporating history-based latent context enables LNNs to handle partial observations and changing parameters, improving long-horizon predictive performance and energy conservation without sacrificing physical plausibility.

Abstract: Forecasting the long-horizon evolution of mechanical systems from position-only observations is a pivotal yet difficult task, as hidden velocities and trajectory-specific physical properties must be inferred simultaneously. Although physics-guided neural networks like Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) guarantee physical plausibility, they generally require complete state inputs and lack adaptability to changing system parameters. To break these limitations, we introduce History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks (HiLNN). Grounded in the insight that temporal position sequences implicitly encode underlying dynamics, HiLNN employs a recurrent encoder to extract a latent context from history. This context not only reconstructs the unobserved initial velocity but also adaptively modulates the mass matrix, potential energy, and damping coefficients of a structured Lagrangian system. By leveraging a differentiable RK4 rollout scheme, the entire pipeline is optimized end-to-end under multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization. Empirical evaluations across conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems show that HiLNN delivers superior long-term prediction accuracy and maintains precise energy profiles compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/yingtian22/History-informed-LNN.

</details>


### [157] [Knowledge-guided Pattern Discovery via Coupled Tensor Factorizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13234)
*Gaute Johannessen,Geert Roelof van der Ploeg,Evrim Acar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Knowledge-guided coupled tensor factorization: integrating simulated data from a computational model with real multiway data to improve pattern discovery and to reveal model-data discrepancies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many complex biological systems yield multiway data (subjects x metabolites x time). Purely data-driven tensor factorizations miss prior knowledge embedded in computational models. Integrating simulated data with real data can improve pattern extraction and diagnose model-data mismatch.

Method: Jointly factorizes real data tensor and simulated data tensor using linear coupling in a coupled tensor factorization framework, leveraging both data sources to guide discovery.

Result: Guided analysis improves pattern discovery in noisy real data and highlights potential discrepancies between the real measurements and the computational model’s simulations.

Conclusion: Incorporating prior computational models via linear-coupled tensor factorization enhances interpretability and model validation, yielding better patterns and exposing data-model gaps.

Abstract: In order to understand complex systems such as the human metabolome or human brain, different sensing technologies are used, generating complex data. These datasets are often multiway, i.e., with more than two axes of variation such as a subjects by metabolites by time array. While tensor factorizations have successfully revealed interpretable patterns from such complex data, they have so far been mainly data-driven. On the other hand, there is more to data -- there are computational models (of these systems), which are rich sources of prior information. In this paper, we introduce a knowledge-guided approach that brings together data and computational models by jointly analyzing real data and simulated data (generated using a computational model) using coupled tensor factorizations with linear coupling. Our experiments on real metabolomics measurements demonstrate that guiding the analysis of such noisy data with simulated data improves the pattern discovery performance while also revealing potential discrepancies between data and computational models.

</details>


### [158] [Novel Knowledge-Guided Generative Methods for Synthetic Transcriptomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13256)
*Francesca Pia Panaccione,Sofia Mongardi,Marco Masseroli,Pietro Pinoli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MK-TGAN and related GAN variants with graph-based prior knowledge improve synthetic transcriptomic data realism and utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical datasets are often biased, imbalanced, or restricted by ethics/legal constraints; synthetic data can augment data availability while preserving biological relevance.

Method: Compare three GAN variants for transcriptomic data; introduce MK-TGAN, a multi-kernel GAN that incorporates prior biological knowledge via gene graphs using Graph Neural Networks; benchmark against alternatives.

Result: MK-TGAN achieves superior realism and utility for synthetic samples; explicit integration of prior knowledge via graphs improves performance across metrics.

Conclusion: Integrating biological prior knowledge into GAN-based data generators enhances synthetic transcriptomic data quality, with MK-TGAN as a leading approach for downstream analyses.

Abstract: As biomedical research increasingly relies on data-intensive tools, the quality and utility of datasets are critical. Challenges such as imbalances, biases, and ethical or legal constraints often limit access to high-quality data. Synthetic data generation can help overcome these limitations. Here, we present a comparative analysis of generative models for transcriptomic data, investigating strategies to incorporate prior biological knowledge via gene graphs. This ensures that synthetic data capture real-world gene patterns, maintaining their usefulness for downstream tasks. In particular, we introduce and benchmark three variants of the Generative Adversarial Network. Among the alternatives, MK-TGAN - an innovative multi-kernel, Graph Neural Network-based model - stands out for its performance in terms of both the realism and utility of the generated data. Unlike other methods, MK-TGAN leverages prior knowledge graphs by exploiting graph neural networks. Our results show that prior knowledge integration strategies improve performance, and that MK-TGAN consistently produces synthetic samples with superior realism and biological plausibility.

</details>


### [159] [Virtual Temperature Sensors in Power Transformers Using Neural Ordinary Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13260)
*Berk Hadzhamolla,Alexander Johannes Stasik,Signe Riemer-Sørensen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-aware Neural ODE model forecasts transformer thermal behavior by embedding simplified heat-transfer physics into a continuous-time neural dynamics framework, enabling robust, interpretable predictions across diverse transformers with varying designs and cooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate transformer thermal forecasting is essential for reliability and operation but existing methods are either computationally expensive (FEM/CFD), overly simplified (lumped models), or data-hungry/less interpretable (pure ML). A physics-informed continuous-time model aims to combine accuracy, efficiency, and interpretability.

Method: Develop a Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (Neural ODE) framework that incorporates simplified heat-transfer equations directly into the ODE dynamics to model thermal evolution from real-world time-series data. Train on data from fifteen Norwegian transformers with different designs and cooling mechanisms, enabling continuous-time, physics-aware forecasting across heterogeneous units.

Result: Evaluated on datasets from fifteen transformers across Norway with varying designs and cooling configurations. The framework demonstrated standardized, physics-aware, and robust forecasting capabilities for heterogeneous transformer units, indicating good generalization and interpretability across diverse hardware.

Conclusion: The work presents a unified, physics-informed data-driven approach for transformer thermal forecasting that handles diverse geometries and operating conditions, offering interpretable predictions and potential benefits for real-time monitoring and asset management.

Abstract: Accurate modeling and forecasting of power transformer thermal behavior are critical for reliability, asset lifetime, and optimized power system operation. Numerical approaches such as finite element methods (FEM) and computational fluid dynamics (CFD) offer high fidelity but are computationally expensive, require complex mesh generation, and are often impractical for real-time or large-scale applications, particularly when transformer geometries are unknown. Lumped-parameter thermal models are more practical but depend on transformer-specific thermal constants and may fail to capture dynamic responses under varying operating and environmental conditions. Purely data-driven machine learning methods, including artificial neural networks, convolutional neural networks, and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks, have shown success in forecasting transformer temperatures but typically require large volumes of high-quality training data and may produce physically inconsistent or uninterpretable results. This paper develops a physics-aware Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (Neural ODE) framework for forecasting transformer thermal behavior from real-world time-series data. Neural ODEs model system dynamics in continuous time, providing smooth trajectory prediction and a natural representation of continuously evolving thermal dynamics. A key contribution is the integration of simplified heat-transfer equations directly into the Neural ODE formulation. The model is evaluated across datasets from fifteen transformers in different regions of Norway with varying designs and cooling mechanisms. The results demonstrate that the developed Neural ODE framework provides a standardized, physics-aware, and robust forecasting approach for heterogeneous transformer units.

</details>


### [160] [Into the ORBIT for Time Series: Training Regimes for Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13262)
*Hongjie Xia,Yiding Liu,Yifan Hu,Peiyuan Liu,Zewei Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORBIT provides a controllable pre-training distribution for time-series foundation models via Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling and Omni-Range Incremental Training, enabling robust zero-shot forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-training distributions for TSFMs often suffer from domain imbalance, limited context requirements, varying prediction horizons, and missingness. ORBIT addresses this gap by making exposure, contexts, and horizons explicit and adjustable.

Method: Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling controls dataset exposure, samples records, targets, context windows, and prediction horizons. Omni-Range Incremental Training varies context lengths and horizons within a single training stage. The paper trains Falcon-2.0 (univariate encoder-only Transformer) with missingness-aware triple-channel patch tokenization and parallel patch prediction. Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment uses late-layer representations as stop-gradient teachers for shallow layers without extra inference cost.

Result: Evaluations on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench show strong zero-shot forecasting performance across diverse domains and frequencies.

Conclusion: ORBIT enables explicit, controllable pre-training distributions for time-series foundation models, improving cross-domain forecasting. The approach demonstrates practical benefits and introduces techniques (stop-gradient cross-depth alignment) that may generalize to other TSFMs; future work could explore scalability and broader tasks.

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have advanced primarily through architectural innovation, while training regimes for large-scale heterogeneous corpora remain under-explored. As a result, pre-training distributions are often poorly controlled with respect to domain imbalance, context requirements, prediction horizons, and missingness. We introduce ORBIT (Omni-Range Bootstrap Incremental Training), a training paradigm that makes this distribution explicit and controllable. ORBIT combines Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling, which controls dataset exposure and samples records, target variables, context windows, and prediction horizons, with Omni-Range Incremental Training, which varies context lengths and prediction horizons throughout a single training stage. Under ORBIT, we train Falcon-2.0, a simple univariate encoder-only Transformer with missingness-aware triple-channel patch tokenization and parallel patch prediction. We further introduce Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment, a training objective that uses late-layer representations as stop-gradient teachers for shallow layers without additional inference cost. Evaluations on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench demonstrate strong zero-shot forecasting performance across diverse domains and frequencies.

</details>


### [161] [EEG Decoding Using CNN and LSTM Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13285)
*Athanasios Karagounis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A CNN + bi-LSTM hybrid for MI-EEG classification achieving robust 2- and 3-class performance with promising subject-independent decoding, evaluated on public and private datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MI-EEG signals are noisy and weakly informative, making reliable decoding challenging; deep learning can learn representations from raw data, but MI-EEG feature learning remains underexplored; a model that captures spatial, temporal and cross-subject patterns could improve performance and generalization.

Method: A hybrid architecture combining a convolutional neural network (CNN) to extract high-level spatial and temporal features from raw MI-EEG, with a bidirectional LSTM (bi-LSTM) to model temporal dependencies among the features. Evaluated on both public and privately collected datasets for 2- and 3-class MI classification, including assessment of subject-independent decoding.

Result: The CNN+bi-LSTM architecture achieves robust performance for both 2- and 3-class MI classification and shows promising potential for subject-independent decoding across the evaluated methods.

Conclusion: The hybrid CNN+bi-LSTM approach is effective for MI-EEG feature learning, offering strong performance and generalization potential for MI-BCI applications, with demonstrated applicability on multiple datasets.

Abstract: Motor imagery (MI) brain--computer interfaces (BCIs) have emerged as a promising approach for establishing flexible communication pathways between the human brain and external devices , particularly for individuals affected by stroke or neurodegenerative disorders. Reliable decoding of motor-imagery electroencephalography (MI-EEG) remains challenging because EEG recordings contain substantial noise and exhibit complex, weakly informative relationships with the underlying brain activity. Although deep learning provides an effective means of learning representations directly from EEG signals, its application to MI-EEG feature learning remains comparatively limited. This study introduces a hybrid deep-learning architecture that integrates a convolutional neural network (CNN) with a bidirectional long short-term memory (bi-LSTM) network. The CNN is used to learn high-level spatial and temporal representations directly from raw MI-EEG recordings, whereas the bi-LSTM models temporal dependencies and relationships among the extracted features. The proposed approach is evaluated using both a publicly available dataset and a privately acquired dataset obtained with an EEG acquisition system. The experimental results indicate that the CNN\&bi-LSTM architecture provides robust performance for both two- and three-class motor-imagery classification and demonstrates promising subject-independent decoding capability across the evaluated methods.

</details>


### [162] [Large-scale Testing Global Optimization Methods with Black-box Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13296)
*Wojciech Zarzecki,Jarosław Arabas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BBAA tasks offer scalable, high-dimensional benchmarks for global optimization; evolutionary algorithms show efficacy on these tasks, promoting alignment with ML challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks are limited in size and function diversity, risking biased method development; BBAA tasks provide realistic, high-dimensional, black-box objectives aligned with machine learning adversarial contexts.

Method: Empirically evaluate several evolutionary algorithms and other metaheuristics on representative BBAA problems to assess performance and scalability.

Result: A range of evolutionary algorithms and metaheuristics effectively solve BBAA objectives, demonstrating the viability of BBAA-based benchmarks for assessing global optimization methods.

Conclusion: Adopting BBAA benchmarks can reduce bias in optimization research and steer method development toward modern ML challenges.

Abstract: Existing global optimization benchmark suites are of a moderate size and are based on a small number of analytical functions that date back even to the 1970s. This causes a risk of biasing the development of global optimization methods. We argue that the tasks related to the black-box adversarial attack (BBAA) can serve as valuable global optimization benchmark in many-dimensional space. We demonstrate the efficiency of several types of evolutionary algorithms and other metaheuristics in solving example BBAA problems. Thus, we take a step towards convergence of global optimization methods to the challenges and needs that arise in the modern machine learning field.

</details>


### [163] [The Time Value of Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13297)
*Matthew Siper,Ahmed Khalifa,Julian Togelius*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LVPG introduces a long-horizon lineage-aware actor-critic framework for evolutionary search that reasons about the future utility of mutations. It decouples search control into a bootstrapped critic predicting finite-horizon lineage value and an actor that adapts mutation intensity, improving search efficiency under finite budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Immediate-return control fails to recognize the delayed value of mutations that enable high-fitness future lineages; there is a need for long-horizon credit assignment in evolutionary search, modeled as finite-horizon decision making.

Method: A long-horizon actor-critic architecture with a shared generative backbone. A bootstrapped critic head estimates the value of finite-horizon lineage potential from multi-step mutation trees, while an actor head modulates mutation intensity over the remaining search budget. Path-based credit assignment is used to contrast with immediate-return optimization across controlled experiments.

Result: Path-based credit assignment accelerates finite-budget search, increasing validation best-so-far AUC by 0.394 Sharpe units. LVPG yields fewer temporary regressions, recovers from them more often, and produces more selective non-monotonic search and stronger policies under identical budgets.

Conclusion: Finite-horizon lineage value improves search efficiency and policy quality in evolutionary/algorithmic trading contexts; LVPG offers a principled approach for long-horizon credit assignment, outperforming immediate-return baselines on multiple metrics.

Abstract: In evolutionary search, a weak child can be a valuable ancestor that makes high-fitness regions reachable. Immediate-return control is blind to this delayed utility, penalizing mutations through their immediate offspring even when they open productive future lineages. We formalize this hidden dynamic as the time value of evolution within a finite-horizon Markov decision process. To exploit it, we introduce Lineage-Value Policy Gradients (LVPG), a long-horizon actor-critic framework for automated trading policy discovery. Our architecture decouples search control into specialized policy heads over a shared generative backbone: a bootstrapped critic head estimates the value of finite-horizon lineage potential from multi-step mutation trees, while an actor head dynamically modulates mutation intensity over the remaining search budget. We isolate the impact of long-horizon credit assignment against immediate-return optimization across 90 paired runs under matched operators, lineage supervision, folds, seeds, and budgets. Path-based credit assignment substantially accelerates finite-budget search, increasing validation best-so-far AUC by 0.394 Sharpe units. LVPG also produces fewer temporary regressions than immediate-return optimization and recovers from them more often. Finite-horizon lineage value yields more selective non-monotonic search and stronger policies within identical resource constraints.

</details>


### [164] [A Probe Direction Is a Property of Its Prompt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13329)
*Valentin Noël*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-prompt evaluation readings are largely driven by the choice of evaluation prompts rather than by the models themselves, undermining cross-model comparisons. The authors show that prompt design can flip or obscure trends with scale, and they quantify the number of prompts needed for defensible comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Question the validity of activation-based readouts used to detect model-evaluation behavior and to understand whether such readings generalize across models and scaling.

Method: Compare activations using prompts that announce an evaluation against prompts that do not, while keeping task text fixed. Systematically vary the prompt choice, decompose variance across models and prompts, test sensitivity of published trends, and assess whether more evaluation items can fix the measurement. Also examine how scoring splits interact with surface form.

Result: Readings are largely determined by prompt choice; model size trends depend on prompts. Two conflicting studies can be reproduced via prompt design alone. Most variance comes from prompt–model interactions; adding more items cannot repair the measurement. Splits are largely separable from surface form, so signals unrelated to evaluation can still produce substantial scores.

Conclusion: Single-prompt designs cannot support fair model comparisons. The authors provide a specified quantity of prompts required for a defensible comparison.

Abstract: A model that behaves differently when it senses it is being tested would undermine the evaluations we rely on, so recent work has sought to read that sense directly from a model's activations. The standard instrument contrasts activations on prompts that announce an evaluation against prompts that do not, and reports how well the resulting direction separates held-out cases. That number is then compared across models and correlated with scale. We observe that the instrument has a free parameter its readings do not disclose: "a prompt that announces an evaluation" is not a prompt but a choice among many, and nothing in the method fixes which. Holding the task text fixed and varying only that choice, we find that the reported score, and even the direction in which it trends with model size, follows the prompt rather than the model; two published studies that disagree about the sign of that trend are both reproducible from a single design, by choice of prompt alone. Treating the prompt as a facet of a measurement design rather than an implementation detail, we find the model under study accounts for a small share of the variance in the number reported about it, and most of the rest lies in how each model responds to each prompt: collecting more evaluation items cannot repair the measurement, while varying prompts can. A further check finds that the split these probes are scored on is largely separable from surface form alone, so a direction carrying no information about evaluation at all still reproduces a substantial fraction of each published score. We conclude that a single-prompt design cannot support comparison between models, and we give the number of prompts a defensible comparison requires.

</details>


### [165] [Training AI Scientists to Replicate Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13331)
*Damon Falck,Samer Sabri,Anja Surina,Thom Foster,Anya Sims,Sam Devlin,Dylan Rogers,Tantum Collins,Kaloyan Aleksiev,Louis Kirsch,Edward Hughes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Replica, a scalable task space for replicating scientific papers, and Faraday, a 27B AI Scientist that uses coding agents as tools. An auto-generated rubric-based judge aligns with human replication quality and enables post-training to outperform Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 on held-out tasks, with qualitative evidence of a principled scientific approach. The work aims to enable long-horizon scientific innovation with minimal additional harnessing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Replicability underpins scientific reliability and guides subsequent experiments. However, replicating results often reveals underspecified details and requires structured, hypothesis-driven exploration. There is a need for scalable, reward-signal-guided frameworks that can automate and extend replication tasks to accelerate scientific progress.

Method: Develop Replica as a scalable task space for paper replication. Create an auto-generated rubric-based judge with low noise that correlates with human replication assessments. Post-train Faraday, a 27B-parameter AI Scientist that uses coding agents as tools, and evaluate its performance on held-out replication tasks, comparing against Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5. Conduct qualitative analyses of rollouts to characterize the agent’s scientific approach.

Result: Faraday surpasses the baselines (Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5) on held-out replication tasks. The rubric-based judge provides a reliable reward signal with low noise and aligns with human judgments of replication quality. Qualitative analysis indicates Faraday adopts a more scientifically-principled approach during replication tasks.

Conclusion: The work constitutes a stepping stone toward AI agents capable of long-horizon scientific innovation without requiring complex harnesses, advancing AI-assisted research and reproducibility in science.

Abstract: The replicability of papers is a cornerstone of scientific knowledge, ensuring the reliability of existing results and providing a base for further experiments. The act of replication typically illuminates details that were previously underspecified, and thus requires similar hypothesis-driven exploration to open-ended research. In this work, we develop Replica, a scalable task space for paper replication. To provide reward signal, we introduce an auto-generated rubric-based judge that has low noise and agrees with human assessment of replication quality. We post-train Faraday, a 27B-parameter "AI Scientist" agent that leverages coding agents as tools, surpassing the performance of Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 on held-out replication tasks. Qualitative analysis of individual rollouts reveals that Faraday adopts a more scientifically-principled approach. We believe that our results provide a stepping stone towards AI agents capable of long-horizon scientific innovation without requiring complex harnesses.

</details>


### [166] [Neural Quadratic Forms: A Unified Minimal Model for Sudden Learning and Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13335)
*Liu Ziyin,Yizhou Xu,Tomaso Poggio,Isaac Chuang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A universal, symmetry-constrained quadratic dynamics Tr[W W^T A(x)] governs training across architectures; the order parameter M=WW^T follows Lotka-Volterra dynamics when data eigenbases align, with modes activating sequentially; plateaus arise as singular limits of smooth flow, and many unresolved modes yield a predicted power-law in training time. These predictions hold across perceptrons, attention, MoE, and convolutions and are confirmed numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why gradient-descent training on diverse neural architectures exhibits both plateaued steps and smooth power-law progress, by identifying universal low-dimensional variables and a single structure matrix that encodes architecture.

Method: Leverage permutation symmetry of units to derive the leading quadratic expansion of the cost around initialization: Tr[W W^T A(x)], where A(x) is a structure matrix capturing architecture. Reduce dynamics to the order parameter M=WW^T; when data matrices share an eigenbasis, obtain independent Lotka–Volterra modes that activate one after another. Analyze effects of small initial weights on switch-on times and plateaus; show how multiple modes produce power-law behavior in training time.

Result: The dynamics collapse to a universal, architecture-dependent but low-dimensional description. Plateaus correspond to a singular limit of the smooth flow, and mode-switching yields step-like learning; unresolved modes generate power-law training-time exponents. Numerical experiments across different architectures (perceptrons, attention layers, mixtures of experts, convolutions) validate the theory.

Conclusion: Symmetry fixes a universal leading form for training dynamics across architectures, with all architectural details confined to a single structure matrix A(x). This reduces learning dynamics to the order parameter M with a Lotka–Volterra structure under suitable data conditions, unifying disparate architectures and predicting plateau, step, and power-law behaviors observed in practice.

Abstract: Neural networks trained by gradient descent on a smooth cost function can nevertheless learn in steps: the cost holds on long plateaus and then drops abruptly. Meanwhile, training losses instead follow smooth power laws. Variants of both behaviors occur in architectures with very different microscopic structures, which is the signature of a few relevant collective variables. We show that a symmetry fixes what those variables are: a network layer is a sum over interchangeable units, so relabeling the units leaves it unchanged; given smoothness and the condition that a unit's gradient vanish at the origin, symmetry then enforces a universal leading form for the expansion about the near-zero weights present at the start of training, the quadratic $\Tr[WW^{\top}A(x)]$, in which every architectural detail is confined to a single ``structure matrix" $A(x)$ that we compute for each architecture. Perceptrons, attention layers, mixtures of experts, and convolutions become one model at different $A$. Its training dynamics then close on the ``order parameter" $M=WW^{\top}$ and, whenever the data matrices share an eigenbasis, reduce to a Lotka--Volterra equation whose modes switch on one after another. The smaller the initial weights, the further apart the switch-on times, and the plateaus appear as a singular limit of a smooth flow; when many modes are unresolved the same events merge into a power law in training time whose exponent the theory predicts. We confirm both numerically across training methods and architectures.

</details>


### [167] [Where You Measure Decides What You Measure: Position Selection in Ablation-Based SAE Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13337)
*Valentin Noël*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent causality measurement in sparse autoencoders is biased by the measurement token; align evaluation to a fixed token and report its position to enable cross-paper comparability, via a simple ablation-based causal metric and small audit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve reproducibility and comparability of latent interpretability analyses by removing token-position bias in how causality is attributed to latent factors.

Method: Train six autoencoders from one initialization with different fitting choices; match latent semantics by decoder similarity; compare dictionaries from different releases; evaluate how much variance in causality attribution comes from token choice versus latent meaning; show that alignment dramatically reduces variance; test across a sixteenfold range of corpus sizes; propose a one-line code correction and an ablation-based protocol, plus audit of five papers.

Result: The primary source of variance in reported causality comes from the measurement token rather than the latent semantics; aligning measurement tokens collapses variance (from 7.6–11.9% to near zero). Across scales, dictionaries disagree more about where to measure as corpora grow; a simple, standard evaluation fix suffices, but current studies still exhibit token-position bias.

Conclusion: A causal metric must report the measurement position to be interpretable across papers; without it, results describe the token chosen rather than the latent. The authors provide a minimal protocol, a one-line evaluation fix, and an audit of published work to improve cross-study comparability.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders are meant to name the things a language model computes, and the usual way to check that a latent matters is to switch it off and see what changes. But a latent fires at many tokens, and the effect has to be measured at one of them. The convention is to measure where the latent fires hardest. That choice is almost never reported, and it is not made by the experimenter: it is made by the dictionary under evaluation. Change the dictionary and the measurement moves to a different token. We show this is not a detail. Take two sparse autoencoders released by Google for the same model and match their latents by decoder similarity: even among the pairs the two dictionaries encode almost identically, they pick different tokens for a large share of them. Two dictionaries compared under the usual protocol are therefore very often compared at different places. To separate the convention from the dictionaries we train six autoencoders from one initialisation, differing only in fitting choices, so that a latent means the same thing in each. Most of the variance such a comparison reads as "these dictionaries disagree about this latent" turns out to be the position instead: it falls from 7.6% and 11.9% of variance to near zero once every dictionary is measured at the same token. More evaluation data does not rescue it. Across a sixteenfold range of corpus sizes the dictionaries agree less about where to measure, not more, so the problem grows with scale. The correction is one line of evaluation code. We give the protocol an ablation-based causal number must report to be comparable across papers, and an audit of five published papers against it. In short: a causal number reported without its position describes the token it was taken at as much as the latent it was taken from.

</details>


### [168] [Simulation-to-real transfer learning for infrared spectroscopic chemical sensing and analysis from molecules to complex samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13341)
*Yusen Tan,Yixuan Chen,Zheng Fang,Pan Liu,Yifan Li,Qinyu Guo,Zhedong Lin,Yuqiang Li,Xiangxiang Zeng,Tong Wang,Jun Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UltraIR is a 100M+ parameter foundation model for infrared spectroscopy trained on ~60M simulated spectra, enabling simulation-to-real transfer for diverse chemical sensing tasks. It achieves strong data-efficient and zero-shot performance across different instruments and laboratories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IR spectroscopy interpretation is labor-intensive and poorly scalable with conventional ML; existing models are task-specific and data-hungry with poor cross-dataset transfer. A scalable, adaptable foundation model is needed for robust, generalizable chemical sensing from IR spectra.

Method: Pretrain UltraIR on ~60 million simulated IR spectra using (1) spectral reconstruction, (2) molecular fingerprint similarity alignment, and (3) functional-group prediction. Then adapt to downstream objectives with task-specific labels or targets. Evaluate zero-shot and few-shot transfer across multiple tasks and datasets, including different FTIR instruments and laboratories.

Result: UltraIR outperforms conventional ML and task-specific baselines across diverse tasks: functional-group prediction, molecular structure elucidation, physicochemical property prediction, mixture-component identification/quantification, bacterial classification, medicinal-herb origin traceability, constituent quantification, microplastics classification, and soil property prediction. It demonstrates strong performance with limited labeled experimental spectra and zero-shot transfer across instruments and labs.

Conclusion: A scalable foundation-model approach for IR spectroscopy enabling data-efficient, cross-domain chemical sensing from complex real-world samples, with robust simulation-to-real transfer and cross-instrument generalization.

Abstract: Infrared (IR) spectroscopy is widely used for chemical sensing, but extracting reliable chemical information from spectra remains challenging. Conventional interpretation is labor-intensive, relies on prior knowledge and reference spectra, and is difficult to scale, whereas most machine-learning methods are tailored to individual tasks or datasets, require large labeled training sets, and transfer poorly across analytical objectives and experimental datasets. Here we introduce UltraIR, a foundation model for IR spectroscopy with more than 100 million parameters that enables simulation-to-real transfer learning for chemical sensing and analysis from molecules to complex samples. UltraIR is pretrained on approximately 60 million simulated IR spectra using spectral reconstruction, molecular fingerprint similarity alignment, and functional-group prediction, then adapted to downstream objectives with task-specific labels or targets. Across functional-group prediction, molecular structure elucidation, physicochemical property prediction, mixture-component identification and quantification, bacterial classification, medicinal-herb geographic origin traceability and constituent quantification, microplastics classification, and soil property prediction, UltraIR outperforms conventional machine-learning and task-specific deep-learning baselines. It performs strongly with limited labeled experimental spectra and in zero-shot inference for the same analytical task across Fourier-transform infrared spectrometers and laboratories, providing a route to adaptable, data-efficient chemical sensing from complex real-world samples.

</details>


### [169] [When Local Variance Optimality Is Not Enough: RoPE-Aligned Q/K Rotations for Dynamic 4-Bit Quantisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13365)
*Shuhan Wang,Yilin Luo,Nan Xu,Chi Wang Cheung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Pairwise two-channel rotations designed to commute with RoPE do not outperform full-head mixing in four checkpoints; increasing mixing support and aligning the surrogate with the quantiser’s scale statistics are key. The analytic objective controls a position-averaged second moment, but optimal structured surrogates do not guarantee lower quantisation error when the surrogate and mixing support misalign with scale-setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether a RoPE-consistent, pairwise rotation transform can surpass full-head mixing in rotation-based post-training quantisation, and to understand the theoretical commutativity constraints and practical impact on quantisation performance.

Method: Theoretical: show that for distinct RoPE frequencies, no other single-head orthogonal map commutes with RoPE (converse to prior per-pair commutativity results). Practical: for a head-shared parameterisation, derive the rotation angle that minimises the larger channel variance under a pooled-covariance, position-averaged surrogate; verify the analytic minimum in implementation. Empirical: evaluate a head-shared pairwise configuration vs full-head Hadamard in dynamic W4A4KV4; test (a) composing pairwise rotation with Hadamard; (b) estimating the shared angle from K alone; (c) interpolating from two-channel to full-head mixing; measure perplexity, short/long context performance, and quantisation error.

Result: Empirically, the head-shared pairwise configuration does not improve accuracy; it increases perplexity relative to full-head Hadamard at both short and long contexts across four checkpoints. Composing pairwise rotation with Hadamard meets the selected ±0.05-PPL criterion under the default estimator. Estimating the shared angle from K improves pairwise-only performance on every checkpoint but does not close the gap to full-head mixing. The pairwise transform supports only two-channel mixing. Interpolating toward full-head mixing reduces K-range, relative quantisation error, and perplexity degradation as mixing support increases.

Conclusion: Optimality of a structured surrogate does not guarantee reduced quantisation error when the surrogate and mixing support are misaligned with the quantiser’s scale-setting statistic. Greater mixing support and alignment with the quantiser’s scale statistics are needed to reap potential gains from RoPE-consistent transforms.

Abstract: Rotation-based post-training quantisation commonly applies an orthogonal transform across an entire attention head to reduce outlier-induced error. RoPE instead partitions each head into two-dimensional frequency pairs, raising the question of whether a transform respecting this decomposition can improve on full-head mixing. Prior work has established the per-pair rotations that commute with RoPE. We state the converse result that, for distinct frequencies, no other single-head orthogonal map commutes with RoPE. For the head-shared parameterisation used in our experiments, we then derive the rotation angle that minimises the larger channel variance under a pooled-covariance, position-averaged surrogate and verify that the implementation attains its analytic minimum. The evaluated head-shared pairwise configuration does not improve accuracy in the tested dynamic W4A4KV4 setting. Across four checkpoints, replacing the full-head Hadamard with this configuration increases perplexity at both short and long context lengths. Composing the pairwise rotation with the Hadamard satisfies the selected $\pm0.05$-PPL interval criterion under the default estimator. Estimating the shared angle from K alone improves pairwise-only on every checkpoint but does not close its gap to full-head mixing. The analytic objective controls a position-averaged second moment of a pooled calibration covariance, whereas the dynamic quantiser sets its step from a tokenwise group range. The pairwise transform also has only two-channel mixing support. Along a controlled interpolation from two-channel to full-head mixing, K range, relative quantisation error, and perplexity degradation decrease as support increases. These results show that optimality for a structured surrogate need not reduce quantisation error when the surrogate and mixing support are misaligned with the quantiser's scale-setting statistic.

</details>


### [170] [Reduced Matrix Multiplication: Input-Adaptive Matrix-Product Reduction for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13426)
*Zixuan Lan,Yanhong Li,Jiawei Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RMM reduces Transformer inference cost by selecting informative slices along contraction axes; training-free, input-adaptive, with tunable retention ratio; shows speedups with modest accuracy loss; extends to vision-language; attention computations more reducible than MLP; runtime gains on A100; scalable direction for inference-time optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce costly matrix multiplications in Transformer inference without retraining; enable input-adaptive efficiency; account for varying model scales and tasks.

Method: Select informative slices along contraction dimensions during inference, controlling retention ratio; no weight changes; analyze across models from 1B to 70B; apply also to multimodal inference; perform mechanistic ablations; implement custom kernels on NVIDIA A100; measure accuracy-efficiency trade-off and wall-clock speed.

Result: RMM provides smooth trade-off; reduction tolerance depends on model family, task, component; often improves with model scale; robust under discriminative, autoregressive generation, long-context; extends to vision-language; attention-side computations more reducible than MLP; practical runtime gains at longer sequence lengths with wall-clock benchmarks.

Conclusion: RMM is a scalable, input-adaptive inference-time optimization that can deliver speedups without weight changes; promising direction across modalities and model scales; further exploration of component-wise reducibility and kernel-level implementations.

Abstract: Transformer-based language models achieve strong performance but incur substantial inference cost due to repeated high-dimensional matrix multiplications. We propose Reduced Matrix Multiplication (RMM), a training-free, input-adaptive inference method that reduces Transformer matrix products by selecting informative slices along their contraction dimensions, without modifying model weights. Under a simple retention-ratio control, RMM provides a smooth and predictable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. Across language models ranging from 1B to 70B parameters, we find that reduction tolerance depends on the model family, task, component, and retention ratio, although it often improves with model scale. Under moderate reduction, RMM remains robust across the evaluated discriminative, autoregressive generation, and long-context settings. We further show that the same principle extends to multimodal vision-language inference. Mechanistic ablations reveal a structural asymmetry within Transformers: attention-side computations are substantially more reducible than MLP components. Finally, wall-clock benchmarks with custom kernels on an NVIDIA A100 show that these computational savings can translate into practical runtime gains, especially at longer sequence lengths. Together, these results position RMM as a scalable direction for input-adaptive inference-time optimization.

</details>


### [171] [Symmetry-Breaking De Novo Crystal Generation via Markovian Jump Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13457)
*Van Khoa Nguyen,Alexandros Kalousis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based generative model (SbCD) explicitly handles symmetry-breaking to generate full crystallographic structures across space groups, outperforming symmetry-preserving baselines on MP20 and MPTS-52.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative crystals models struggle to produce complete crystallographic specifications and often rely on sampling space groups empirically, leading to weak global symmetry and structural coherence.

Method: SbCD uses a Markovian jump-diffusion process that reverses from lowest-symmetry priors to model symmetry-breaking dynamics, enabling inter-space-group transitions and traversal of different space groups during generation.

Result: In de novo generation on MP20 and MPTS-52, SbCD substantially outperforms a symmetry-preserving counterpart, demonstrating improved global symmetry adherence and structural validity.

Conclusion: Introducing explicit inter-space-group transitions via symmetry-breaking diffusion provides a principled, effective framework for full structure specification in crystalline material generation; the approach shows strong potential for broader applicability in crystal generative modeling.

Abstract: Generating crystals has recently attracted significant interest due to their broad applications in materials science. However, existing generative models struggle to produce complete crystallographic specifications, limiting their ability to capture global symmetry and structural dependencies. In particular, current state-of-the-art approaches generate crystals only up to site symmetries and rely on sampling space groups from empirical distributions during generation. Inspired by \emph{spontaneous symmetry breaking} in physics, where crystals break symmetries under external conditions, we propose a novel diffusion-based framework that generates full structure specifications by reversing from the lowest-symmetry priors. Our method leverages a Markovian jump-diffusion process to model these symmetry-breaking dynamics, enabling it to traverse different space groups in a physically motivated manner. Our model, dubbed \emph{Symmetry-breaking Crystal Diffusion} (SbCD), introduces a principled approach to explicitly incorporate inter-space-group transitions into the generative process. In de novo generation experiments on MP20 and MPTS-52, SbCD outperforms its symmetry-preserving counterpart by a substantial margin, offering a promising perspective for generative modeling of crystalline materials.

</details>


### [172] [Doubly Robust Estimation of Causal Effect on CVR with Targeted Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13461)
*Jiayi Dan,Bo Li,Lu Deng,Yong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a doubly robust causal estimator for chain-structured CVR, addressing sample-selection bias from clicked data and ensuring robustness with flexible estimators; introduces targeted regularization; validated with synthetic and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CVR estimation is biased when using only clicked samples; existing ideal-loss debiasing methods do not guarantee unbiased final estimators, motivating a semiparametric, robust approach for chain outcomes like CVR.

Method: Develops a new doubly robust estimator tailored for chain-structured outcomes in CVR, with semiparametric theory to achieve faster convergence of nuisance estimators; integrates targeted regularization to stabilize training and improve numerical performance; compatible with flexible models such as neural networks.

Result: The proposed estimator demonstrates improved robustness and convergence in both synthetic and real-world experiments; naive loss-debiasing approaches underperform, underscoring the need for the new estimator with theoretical guarantees.
