Conclusion: Provides a theoretically justified, practically applicable estimator for CVR-like chain outcomes, enabling efficient use of full data with flexible estimators; framework offers numerical stability via targeted regularization and may extend to related causal chain problems.

Abstract: Post-click conversion rate (CVR) is a key metric in various scenarios including e-commerce and advertising, reflecting the efficiency and user experience in the second stage of the conversion process. Estimating the causal effect on CVR is therefore of great practical importance. However, directly applying existing causal inference methods to clicked samples introduces sample selection bias and increased variance due to the exclusion of non-click data. Recent studies on CVR prediction introduce "ideal loss", which optimizes model parameters using an unbiased estimate of the loss over the full sample. Nevertheless, there is no guarantee that unbiasedness of the loss implies unbiasedness of the final estimator.
  We revisit this challenge from the perspective of semiparametric theory. Specifically, we develop a new doubly robust causal effect estimator for chain-structured outcomes such as CVR, and derive its theoretical properties in detail. It achieves a faster convergence rate compared to nuisance parameters estimation and is therefore more robust when using flexible nonparametric estimators, including neural networks. Based on these theoretical findings, we further design a framework based on targeted regularization to improve numerical stability and practical applicability.
  Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world data demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of our method. In addition, we find that naively combining loss debiasing with standard causal estimators underperforms our method, highlighting the necessity of developing the new estimator tailored to this CVR-style objective with solid theoretical guarantees.

</details>


### [173] [Concept Drift Detection and Adaptive Retraining of Malware Classification Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13465)
*Christofer Washington Berruz Chungata,Martin Jurecek,Katerina Potika,William B. Andreopoulos,Mark Stamp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The abstract evaluates three concept-drift detectors (OCSVM, MK-Means, MMD) for malware classification and shows drift-aware retraining can match periodic retraining in accuracy while reducing retraining effort; OCSVM generally performs best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Malware constantly evolves, causing concept drift that degrades model performance; efficient drift detection is needed to trigger retraining only when necessary.

Method: Four classifiers (MLP, Random Forest, SVM, XGBoost) are evaluated under static, periodic retraining, and drift-aware retraining using three drift detectors (OCSVM, MK-Means, MMD). Pareto-front analysis assesses the accuracy-efficiency tradeoff; experiments compare detector effectiveness in maintaining performance while reducing retraining.

Result: All three detectors achieve accuracy comparable to periodic retraining while substantially reducing the number of models retrained; OCSVM generally outperforms MK-Means and MMD in drift-aware retraining.

Conclusion: Concept drift detection is feasible and beneficial for malware classification, with OC-SVM-based drift detection offering the strongest efficiency-accuracy balance under drift-aware retraining.

Abstract: Concept drift refers to changes over time in the statistical properties of data, as compared to the data that was used to train a learning model. Machine learning models for malware detection or classification are particularly susceptible to performance degradation caused by concept drift, as attackers constantly modify existing malware. In this chapter, we analyze two machine learning-based approaches to automated concept drift detection-a novel approach based on One-Class Support Vector Machines (OCSVM) and a previously-studied technique based on Minibatch K-Means (MK-Means). For comparison we also consider Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD), a statistical technique for detecting changes in multidimensional data. We conduct an extensive series of experiments comparing the effectiveness of four learning models, namely, Multilayer Perceptron, Random Forest, Support Vector Machines, and eXtreme Gradient Boosting. For each of these models, we consider three distinct scenarios: A static scenario where no model retraining occurs, a periodic scenario where models are constantly retrained irrespective of concept drift, and a drift-aware scenario where models are only retrained when concept drift is detected. Under the drift-aware scenario, we analyze the tradeoff between accuracy and training efficiency using Pareto Front analysis. We find that all three concept drift detection techniques achieve classification accuracy comparable to periodic retraining, while offering substantially greater efficiency in terms of the number of models that must be retrained. In addition, drift-aware retraining based on our OCSVM technique generally outperforms the MK-Means and MMD approaches. Overall, these results provide strong evidence that we can accurately detect concept drift in malware classification models.

</details>


### [174] [Active-Trace Complexity Bounds for Moreau--Yosida Unadjusted Langevin Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13467)
*Yuchen Xin,Zhihua Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the Moreau--Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm (MYULA) for nonsmooth composite targets and shows that discretization error is governed by the active trace B_ref (an average of the a_λ Hessian trace) rather than the global d/λ bound. This yields improved iteration complexity, with tilde-O(ε^{-2}) under curvature-tube conditions for common penalties and tilde-O(ε^{-3}) generically, plus an end-to-end bias bound for the original target π.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To obtain tight nonasymptotic convergence guarantees for MYULA when sampling from nonsmooth composite densities, by replacing a crude global curvature bound with a data-adaptive metric (the active trace B_ref) and deriving end-to-end error bounds that combine smoothing error (via the Moreau envelope) and discretization error.

Method: Define g_λ as the Moreau envelope of the nonsmooth g, consider π_λ and its Hessian trace a_λ, and analyze the leading discretization error of MYULA in terms of B_ref, the average of a_λ along the heat substep starting from π_λ. Derive iteration complexity N in terms of m,L_f,τ_f,G,B_ref,M_λ,ε_alg, and establish a bound for the Moreau bias between π_λ and π. Specialize to structured penalties to show curvature–tube estimates render B_ref independent of λ, yielding improved ε-dependence.

Result: The leading discretization error depends on B_ref rather than d/λ. If M_λ bounds a_λ, then N ≲ (1/m)[L_f + (τ_f+G^2+B_ref)/ε_alg^2 + M_λ/ε_alg], and √m W_2(μ_N, π_λ) ≤ ε_alg. Also √m W_2(π_λ, π) ≤ (G^2 λ)/4, giving an end-to-end ε guarantee with λ ≈ ε/G^2. In general, B_ref ≤ d/λ yields tilde-O(ε^{-3}) accuracy; for structured penalties (piecewise-linear, lasso-type, group, total-variation), curvature–tube estimates make B_ref λ-independent, achieving tilde-O(ε^{-2}).

Conclusion: Using the active-trace B_ref to bound discretization error yields tighter, dimension-aware iteration complexity for MYULA with nonsmooth penalties. Under common structured penalties, the dependence on λ and thus the complexity improves to tilde-O(ε^{-2}); otherwise the universal bound remains tilde-O(ε^{-3}). The work provides practical guidance on choosing λ and demonstrates a path to end-to-end guarantees for π via π_λ.

Abstract: We study the Moreau--Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm (MYULA) for the nonsmooth composite target \[ π(dx)\propto \exp\{-f(x)-g(x)\}\,dx, \qquad x\in\mathbb R^d, \] where \(f\) is \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_f\)-Lipschitz gradient and \(g\) is convex and \(G\)-Lipschitz. Let \(g_λ\) be the Moreau envelope of \(g\), \(π_λ\) the corresponding smoothed target, and \(a_λ=\operatorname{tr}H_λ\), where \(H_λ\) is the a.e./weak Hessian of \(g_λ\). We show that the leading MYULA discretization error is controlled by the reference active trace \(B_{\mathrm{ref}}\), the average of \(a_λ\) along the heat substep of one MYULA update started from \(π_λ\), rather than by the global curvature bound \(d/λ\). If \(M_λ\) is an a.e. upper bound for \(a_λ\), then, up to logarithmic factors, \[ N \lesssim \frac{1}{m} \left[ L_f + \frac{ τ_f+G^2+B_{\mathrm{ref}} }{ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}^2 } + \frac{M_λ}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}} \right], \qquad τ_f:= \sup_x\operatorname{tr}\nabla^2 f(x), \] iterations suffice to ensure \(\sqrt m\,W_2(μ_N,π_λ)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}\), where \(μ_N\) is the law of the \(N\)-th iterate and \(W_2\) is the quadratic Wasserstein distance. We also prove the Moreau-bias bound \[ \sqrt m\,W_2(π_λ,π) \leq \frac{G^2λ}{4}. \] Thus, choosing \(λ\asymp\varepsilon/G^2\) gives an end-to-end guarantee for \(π\). The universal estimate \(B_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq d/λ\) yields \(\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-3})\) accuracy dependence. For the structured piecewise-linear, lasso-type, group, and total-variation penalties considered here, curvature--tube estimates make \(B_{\mathrm{ref}}\) independent of \(λ\), yielding \(\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-2})\) for the same classical MYULA kernel.

</details>


### [175] [Synthetic Persona Pretraining: Alignment from Token Zero](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13482)
*Julian Minder,Viktor Moskvoretskii,Raghav Singhal,Difan Jiao,Andy Arditi,Shaobo Cui,Yiderigun Borjigin,Kartik Bali,Stefan Krsteski,Harsh Raj,Huu Nguyen,Jannik Brinkmann,Ashton Anderson,Roland Aydin,Robert West*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Synthetic Persona Pretraining (SPP) bake-values into models during pretraining by adding value-aligned first-person reflections to training data, then binding the persona via posttraining dialogue data. It improves constitution and jailbreak robustness, reduces misalignment in moral dilemmas, and preserves capabilities; early intervention is crucial and scales with data/budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address deep misalignment by embedding the desired persona into the model's core during pretraining, rather than layering alignment post hoc after priors are formed.

Method: Annotate pretraining documents with normative, value-aligned first-person reflections derived from a value constitution. Pretrain on standard documents plus their reflections using cross-entropy. Then post-train on user–assistant dialogue data to bind the persona to the assistant identity (persona binding). Evaluate on models up to 3B parameters trained on ~500B tokens across alignment and robustness metrics.

Result: SPP improves constitution adherence and jailbreak robustness, reduces misalignment in out-of-distribution moral dilemmas, and preserves capabilities. Early SPP (during pretraining) yields stronger effects than post-hoc alignment; advantages grow with larger pretraining budgets.

Conclusion: Shaping model values during pretraining is critical for alignment. Pretraining-time persona interventions are an effective approach to embed a desired persona and improve alignment without sacrificing performance.

Abstract: As language-model-based AI is increasingly deployed in autonomous settings, aligning its goals and values with those of humans becomes critical. Today, alignment, and the assistant identity itself, are typically introduced only after pretraining, once behavioral priors are already established. This can make values a thin overlay, rather than deeply rooted, and facilitate subsequent misalignment. Pursuing a different paradigm, we introduce Synthetic Persona Pretraining (SPP), which installs the desired assistant persona from token zero in pretraining. First, we annotate pretraining documents with value-aligned first-person reflections derived from a normative value constitution. Second, we pretrain via the standard cross-entropy loss on standard pretraining documents as well as their reflections, which installs the desired persona among a multitude of other personas. Finally, we post-train on user-assistant dialogue data, which binds this desired persona to the assistant identity, a process we call persona binding. By pretraining models up to 3B parameters on 500B tokens, we show that SPP improves constitution following and jailbreak robustness, and reduces the misalignment rate in out-of-distribution moral dilemmas, while preserving capabilities. Early intervention matters: compared with alignment from token zero, introducing SPP only at the end of pretraining yields weaker constitution adherence, does not shift value priorities, and leads to less aligned choices in dilemmas. This advantage depends on persona binding and, importantly, increases with pretraining budget. Overall, our results show that shaping values early is critical for alignment and establish pretraining-time persona interventions as an effective approach to do so.

</details>


### [176] [Sparse Orthogonal Regression Technique: A Spectral Framework for Equation Discovery, Approximation, and Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13504)
*Sabin Roman,Ljupco Todorovski,Saso Dzeroski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SORT is a sparse spectral framework that learns orthonormal-basis expansions from noisy, irregular data by estimating coefficients with L1-regularized regression, enabling data-driven ODE discovery and beyond while highlighting the importance of basis design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the brittleness and inefficiency of library-based, quadrature-dependent sparse identification methods in the face of noisy, irregular data; provide a flexible, basis-driven representation that can support equation discovery, nonlinear approximation, and high-dimensional integration.

Method: Represent vector fields in orthonormal bases and estimate expansion coefficients directly from observations using L1-regularized regression, avoiding explicit quadrature or inner-product evaluation; use a sparse spectral approach (SORT) to facilitate data-driven discovery of ODEs.

Result: SORT matches or improves library-based sparse regressions when the basis is well adapted; demonstrates greater stability under sparse sampling, derivative noise, and representation mismatch; shows dominant low-order coefficients persist as model order grows; provides a compact spectral representation useful for nonlinear approximation and integral estimation.

Conclusion: SORT offers a reusable intermediate representation that shifts focus from selecting among generic terms to designing problem-aligned bases; it supports equation discovery and beyond (approximation and integration) with order-consistent model growth, though it remains sensitive to basis mis-specification.

Abstract: We develop the Sparse Orthogonal Regression Technique (SORT), a sparse spectral framework for learning orthonormal-basis expansions from noisy and irregularly sampled data. SORT estimates expansion coefficients directly from observations using L1-regularized regression, avoiding explicit quadrature or analytic inner-product evaluation. The central application is data-driven discovery of ordinary differential equations: vector fields are represented in chosen orthogonal bases and learned as sparse coefficient expansions. This provides a complementary route to symbolic regression, grammar-based discovery, and SINDy-style sparse identification by first recovering a compact spectral representation, which can later guide searches for simpler analytic forms. Across the dynamical-system experiments, SORT matches or improves upon library-based sparse-regression baselines when the basis is well adapted to the problem, and shows more stable degradation under sparse sampling, noisy derivative estimates, and representation mismatch. Specific examples illustrate why this representation is useful: if a finite library misses the problem-specific nonlinearity, the resulting model can fail. SORT is not immune to mismatch, but it shifts the problem away from brittle selection among generic terms to basis design adapted to the problem domain. The experiments also show that dominant low-order coefficients persist as model order increases, supporting order-consistent model growth. Beyond equation discovery, the same learned expansion supports nonlinear approximation and estimation of complex, high-dimensional integrals by coefficient readout. Overall, SORT provides a reusable intermediate representation for system identification, approximation, and integration, while making basis design an explicit part of the scientific modeling problem.

</details>


### [177] [Intern-S2-Preview: Scientific Agentic Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13505)
*Lei Bai,Jiaqi Cao,Chiyu Chen,Guanzhou Chen,Kai Chen,Guangran Cheng,Erfei Cui,Xuanlang Dai,Shengyuan Ding,Shangheng Du,Yanhui Duan,Yue Fan,Youqing Fang,Quan Gan,Yuanyuan Gao,Jiaye Ge,Lixin Gu,Yuzhe Gu,Qipeng Guo,Junjun He,Xin Hong,Ming Hu,Zhouqi Hua,Haian Huang,Junhao Huang,Zixian Huang,Minxi Jin,Lingkai Kong,Alexander Lam,Zehao Li,Zonglin Li,Tianhao Liang,Dahua Lin,Junyao Lin,Tianyang Lin,Zhouhan Lin,Jiangning Liu,Jin Liu,Kuikun Liu,Wenran Liu,Yifei Liu,Yuhong Liu,Yuhong Liu,Zhoumianze Liu,Ziyan Liu,Ziyu Liu,Haijun Lv,Han Lv,Chengqi Lyu,Le Ma,Ningsheng Ma,Zerun Ma,Haoyang Peng,Runyu Peng,Jifei Shan,Zixin Shang,Kou Shi,Xiang Shi,Qisheng Su,Xuerui Su,Hao Sun,Xiao Sun,Yanan Sun,Yu Sun,Huanze Tang,Yinghao Tang,Wenhui Tian,Zhongbo Tian,Bingli Wang,Haomin Wang,Jiarui Wang,Jingzhi Wang,Rui Wang,Xiquan Wang,Yi Wang,Zhecan Wang,Ziyi Wang,Zun Wang,Rubin Wei,Lianyi Wu,Wen Wu,Yue Wu,Yuhan Wu,Zhenyu Wu,Zijian Wu,Shuhao Xing,Jun Xu,Xingle Xu,Xuenan Xu,Xiangchao Yan,Ziang Yan,Bowen Yang,Danni Yang,Lin Yang,Zhiqi Yang,Qian Yao,Haochen Ye,Peng Ye,Jinhui Yin,Jiashuo Yu,Dingbo Yuan,Fei Yuan,Yuhang Zang,Bo Zhang,Chao Zhang,Chen Zhang,Hongjie Zhang,Junming Zhang,Wenlong Zhang,Wenwei Zhang,Yiming Zhang,Zhuo Zhang,Ziyang Zhang,Haiteng Zhao,Penghao Zhao,Yibo Zhao,Zhonghan Zhao,Zhihang Zhong,Bowen Zhou,Peiheng Zhou,Xin Zhou,Xinyu Zhou,Yunhua Zhou,Dongsheng Zhu,Yicheng Zou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Intern-S2-Preview-397B is a multimodal scientific agentic foundation model with a dedicated memory-augmented path, achieving strong results across scientific, multimodal, and long-horizon tasks; notable gains in SciTS forecasting and Biology-Instructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable AI systems that reason over heterogeneous scientific evidence, interact with tools and environments, and sustain progress across long task horizons.

Method: Multimodal pre-training on rendered scientific documents, interleaved image–text data, and diverse corpora, followed by post-training with supervised fine-tuning, scalable multi-task RL, black-/white-box agentic RL, and on-policy distillation; training stability and efficiency aided by partial rollout with off-policy correction, adaptive length regularization, online speculative decoding, robust multi-task optimization, and trace-aware experience assembly. Architecturally, 397B extends time-series modeling for long sequences and numerical forecasting, while Memory Decoder provides a separate memory-augmented path without altering the frozen backbone.

Result: The model achieves competitive or leading performance across scientific, multimodal, agentic, and general-purpose benchmarks. Time-series modules improve SciTS forecasting. Memory Decoder extension raises Biology-Instructions average from 56.92 to 60.32 without modifying the 397B backbone.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that large, agentic, multimodal foundation models with specialized memory pathways can excel in long-horizon scientific reasoning tasks, with stable training and practical techniques enabling scalable deployment.

Abstract: Scientific discovery increasingly requires AI systems that can reason over scientific evidence of heterogeneous modalities, interact with scientific tools and environments, and sustain progress across long task horizons. We present Intern-S2-Preview, a series of scientific agentic foundation models designed to support multimodal scientific understanding, reasoning, generation, and long-horizon tasks. The training pipeline begins with scientific multimodal pre-training over rendered scientific documents, interleaved image-text data, and diverse scientific corpora. Starting from the pretrained checkpoint, we apply a unified post-training pipeline consisting of supervised fine-tuning, scalable multi-task reinforcement learning (RL), black- and white-box agentic RL, and on-policy distillation. This pipeline is supported by practical techniques that improve rollout and training stability and efficiency, including partial rollout with off-policy correction, adaptive length regularization, online speculative decoding, robust multi-task optimization, and trace-aware experience assembly for agentic tasks. At the architecture level, Intern-S2-Preview-397B extends time series modelling from efficient long-sequence understanding to numerical forecasting, while Memory Decoder is studied as a separate memory-augmented path for rapid scientific specialization without modifying the frozen 397B backbone. Evaluations across scientific, multimodal, agentic, and general-purpose benchmarks show that Intern-S2-Preview-397B achieves competitive or leading results in multiple settings. The time series modules improve scientific signal understanding and forecasting on SciTS, while the separate Intern-MemDec-4B extension improves the Biology-Instructions average score from 56.92 to 60.32 without modifying the frozen 397B backbone.

</details>


### [178] [Intervention-Aware Clinical World Model for Post-Op Outcome Forecasting in Cardiology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13518)
*Yunsung Chung,Yingshuo Liu,Abboud F. Hassan,Han Feng,Mary M. Maleckar,Nassir Marrouche,Jihun Hamm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An intervention-aware world model captures irregular post-procedure trajectories using a structured latent state, enabling horizon-specific recurrence risk prediction and blind-follow-up supervision via latent forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional clinical prediction models map baseline data to an outcome, ignoring irregular, asynchronous post-intervention dynamics. The goal is to model time-varying interventions and measurements to improve risk forecasting.

Method: Encode baseline imaging into a 3D latent state; update state with procedural context, static covariates, elapsed time, and peri-event physiological embeddings. Train with a latent forecasting objective using follow-up imaging as supervision. Apply to AF ablation and DECAAF-II data; evaluate recurrence risk and scar-extent MAE.

Result: AUROC 0.756 and AUPRC 0.777 for 90-day AF recurrence; scar-extent MAE 2.971 percentage points without requiring follow-up MRI at inference. The model supports horizon-specific recurrence-risk queries and retrospective editing of blanking-period records.

Conclusion: An intervention-aware latent state framework effectively captures irregular post-intervention dynamics, enabling improved risk prediction and interpretable querying across horizons, with training-time supervision from follow-up imaging.

Abstract: Many clinical prediction models treat post-intervention outcomes as a one-step mapping from baseline measurements to a future endpoint. However, recovery after a procedure often unfolds as an irregular trajectory: clinical observations, medication changes, repeat interventions, and physiological measurements are recorded asynchronously and can change risk assessment over time. We propose an intervention-aware clinical world model that represents each patient with a structured latent state and evolves it through time-ordered post-intervention events. The model first encodes baseline imaging into a 3D spatial latent state. It then updates this state using procedural context, static covariates, elapsed time, and peri-event physiological embeddings. Follow-up imaging provides training-only supervision through a latent forecasting objective. We apply the framework to atrial fibrillation ablation. During the 90-day recovery window, irregular post-procedure records provide clinically meaningful evidence for long-term recurrence risk. In repeated internal cross-validation on DECAAF-II, our model achieves AUROC 0.756 and AUPRC 0.777 for recurrence prediction. It also achieves a scar-extent MAE of 2.971 percentage points without requiring follow-up MRI intensities at inference. The learned state supports recurrence-risk queries at different horizons and retrospective input editing of blanking-period records.

</details>


### [179] [The data geometry of masking diffusion: Certified-optimal schedules via unmasking growth complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13520)
*Martin J. Wainwright*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces unmasking growth complexity (UGC), a path-resolved data-geometry measure for masking diffusion in discrete sampling, enabling optimized single/multi-block schedules and certified-optimal samplers with KL-error guarantees; yields dimension-dependent gains, including potential ~sqrt(d) improvements with few adaptively placed blocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify data geometry in masking diffusion for discrete sampling and to control discretization error (KL) while optimizing computational schedules; provide adaptive methods that respond to data structure.

Method: Define UGC as a path-resolved measure of data geometry; use log-reveal-odds coordinates to derive optimized single-block and multi-block schedules; estimate UGC increments from samples via KL increments along coupled reveal trajectories; develop certified-optimal samplers achieving prescribed KL error with high probability; collapse the UGC path to aggregate UGC mass and connect to classical multivariate dependence and complexity measures; in the fine-partition limit, relate the squared integral of sqrt(UGC) density to leading-order Euler discretization error.

Result: Certified-optimal samplers achieving a target KL error with favorable iteration complexity; substantial dimension-dependent gains over coarse schedules; potential improvements of order ~sqrt(d) with constant numbers of adaptively placed blocks; connection to classical dependence measures and prior complexity analyses.

Conclusion: UGC provides a unified, geometry-aware framework for discrete diffusion sampling that enables adaptive, provably near-optimal scheduling and sampling, with provable KL-error guarantees and significant dimension-dependent efficiency gains; links new path-based measures to established dependence and discretization theory.

Abstract: We study masking diffusion for discrete sampling and introduce a path-resolved measure of data geometry called the \emph{unmasking growth complexity} ({\textsf{UGC}\xspace}). Its local increments directly control Kullback--Leibler (KL) discretization error, yielding a unified analysis of Bernoulli-subset and fixed-cardinality unmasking schemes. In log-reveal-odds coordinates, this structure yields optimized single-block and multi-block schedules, and quantifies the gains from adapting computational effort to data geometry. Crucially, we show how {\textsf{UGC}\xspace} increments can be estimated from samples via KL increments along coupled reveal trajectories. This leads to \emph{certified-optimal} samplers that achieve a prescribed KL error with high probability and iteration complexity within a constant factor of the corresponding oracle procedure. Collapsing the \ugc path yields the aggregate {\textsf{UGC}\xspace} mass, which connects to classical multivariate dependence measures and complexity measures from previous analyses of discrete diffusion. In the fine-partition limit, the squared integral of the square-root {\textsf{UGC}\xspace} density determines the sharp leading-order optimal Euler discretization error. Examples exhibit substantial dimension-dependent gains over coarse schedules, including $\widetildeΩ(\sqrt{d})$ improvements achievable with a constant number of adaptively placed blocks.

</details>


### [180] [Vero: Can AI Agents Build Formally Verified Software Repositories?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13522)
*Zhe Ye,Hantao Lou,Yuechun Sun,Peiyang Song,Zhengxu Yan,Timothe Kasriel,Qingyang Zhang,Kaiyu Yang,Soonho Kong,Jingxuan He,Dawn Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vero is a repository-level benchmark for joint implementation and proof synthesis across multi-module codebases (Python, Dafny, Verus, Coq); 43 multi-module Lean 4 repositories; includes an audit mechanism; strongest agent solves 27/43; aims to push toward verified software synthesis at scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks cover isolated functions or proof generation with fixed implementations; there is a need to assess coherent implementation-and-proof decisions across real multi-module repositories to improve trustworthy AI-generated software.

Method: Curate 43 real-world, multi-module Lean 4 repositories with fixed API interfaces, formal specs, and reference implementations. Supports proof-only and code-and-proof evaluation; adds an audit mechanism to prove unsatisfiability or incorrectness of provided specs/code to surface latent errors; uses the Lean toolchain for evaluation and provides a curation pipeline and evaluation harness.

Result: The strongest agent fully solves only 27 of 43 instances and closes no specifications on the hardest repositories, indicating substantial room for progress toward repository-scale verified software synthesis.

Conclusion: Vero offers a concrete, publicly available testbed to measure progress toward scalable, repository-level verified software synthesis; currently, agents fall short of solving most challenging multi-module benchmarks.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly used for programming, but do not provide any guarantee on the correctness of generated code. Verified code generation, in which an agent produces both an implementation and a machine-checked proof of its specification, offers a stronger path toward trustworthy AI-generated software. Existing benchmarks in this direction either focus on individual functions or only evaluate proof generation with provided implementations. It is still an open question whether agents can make coherent implementation and proof choices across real multi-module codebases. To bridge this gap, we introduce Vero, the first benchmark to evaluate joint implementation and proof synthesis at the repository level. Vero contains 43 multi-module instances sourced from real-world repositories spanning Python, Dafny, Verus, and Coq, and covering diverse domains from cryptographic protocols to distributed systems. Each instance consists of a multi-module Lean 4 repository with predetermined API interfaces, manually curated formal specifications, and reference implementations, supporting both proof-only and code-and-proof evaluation modes. To improve benchmark reliability, Vero also includes an audit mechanism where agents are allowed to formally prove unsatisfiability of provided specification or incorrectness of reference code, which surfaces and corrects latent code and specification errors during curation. We evaluate frontier coding-agent configurations with Lean toolchain access. The strongest agent fully solves only 27 of 43 instances and closes no specifications on the hardest repositories. Vero provides a concrete testbed for measuring progress toward repository-scale verified software synthesis, where current agents still fall short. We release the benchmark, curation pipeline, and evaluation harness at https://github.com/sunblaze-ucb/vero.

</details>


### [181] [DARTree: Speculative Diffusion Decoding with Autoregressive Draft Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13524)
*Tianyi Li,Yaxin Luo,Xinyi Shang,Zhiqiang Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DARTree is a training-free speculative decoding method that extends autoregressive (AR) correction heads from linear chains to a fixed-width, pruned candidate tree, achieving large lossless speedups and higher token acceptance per verification round without model training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To broaden candidate coverage and preserve causal information during speculative decoding without training, addressing limitations of diffusion-based drafters and existing chain-based corrections.

Method: Construct a fixed-width candidate tree by expanding and scoring all nodes at each depth in a single batch; apply best-first pruning to select the verification tree; decouple AR-head inference from sequential heap operations; leverage a pretrained AR correction head (no training required).

Result: Across seven math, code, and chat benchmarks, DARTree attains the highest average acceptance length and speedup in all model–temperature settings, accepting up to 12.97 tokens per verification round; 98.6% more than DFlash and 27.9% more than Domino; up to 9.73× lossless speedup over locally measured autoregressive decoding.

Conclusion: DARTree delivers a training-free, scalable improvement for speculative decoding by extending chain-based corrections to trees, achieving substantial efficiency gains with broad applicability; effectiveness hinges on AR-head quality and evaluation setup.

Abstract: Speculative decoding losslessly accelerates autoregressive language models by verifying multiple draft tokens in parallel. Diffusion-based drafters further reduce proposal latency by predicting an entire token block in parallel, but their position-wise distributions are marginal rather than conditioned on tokens selected along each draft path. Existing recurrent correction incorporates causal information along a single draft chain, whereas diffusion-based tree construction broadens candidate coverage without carrying this correction along individual branches. We introduce DARTree, a training-free speculative decoding method that extends a pretrained AR correction head from chains to trees. DARTree first constructs a fixed-width candidate tree by expanding and scoring all nodes at each depth in a single batch, and then only applies best-first pruning to select the verification tree, decoupling AR-head inference from sequential heap operations. Across seven math, code, and chat benchmarks, DARTree achieves the highest average acceptance length and speedup in all four model--temperature configurations, accepting up to 12.97 tokens per verification round, 98.6\% more than DFlash and 27.9\% more than Domino in the same setting, and reaching up to 9.73$\times$ lossless speedup over locally measured autoregressive decoding.

</details>


### [182] [Exponential Convex Calibration Dimension for the Multi-Label Jaccard Measure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13549)
*Mingyuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Analyzes the calibration and dimensionality of Jaccard/IoU loss in multi-label settings. Shows the Jac loss matrix is nonsingular with 2^s outcomes and has affine dimension 2^s−1. Exact calibration requires exponential prediction coordinates; offers two polynomial-dimensional surrogates with regret guarantees: an F1→Jaccard transfer and a MinHash-based square-loss surrogate. Establishes lower and upper bounds on the convex calibration dimension, with bounds 2^{s−1} ≤ CCdim(L^{Jac}) ≤ 2^s−1, and (for zero-regret) exponential dimension; for fixed additive regret tolerance, polynomial dimensions are achievable. Provides explicit regret-transfer results and dimension bounds depending on α and ρ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify the complexity of exact and approximate calibration for Jaccard (IoU) based losses in multi-label classification/segmentation, and to develop practical surrogates with provable regret guarantees.

Method: Finite MinHash Gram representation, Boolean Möbius inversion, and Bayes-optimal reporting analysis to study the Jac loss matrix. Derivation of dimension bounds, construction of convex surrogates, and regret-transfer proofs for two schemes (F1-to-Jaccard transfer and MinHash square-loss surrogate). Probability-based and dimension bounds with ρ and α.

Result: Nonsingularity of the Jac loss matrix; affine dimension 2^s−1. Exact calibration requires at least 2^{s−1} dimensions and at most 2^s−1. Two polynomial-dimensional guarantees: (i) F1→Jaccard transfer from an (s^2+1)-dimensional F1 surrogate to a rule with asymptotic Jaccard regret ≤ 3−2√2; (ii) MinHash square-loss surrogate attaining floor(α) Jaccard regret uniformly; with high probability, direct dimension O((s^2+s log(1/ρ))/α^2) or signed variant O((s+log(1/ρ))/α^2).)

Conclusion: Exact Jaccard calibration in multi-label settings incurs exponential dimensionality, but fixed additive regret guarantees are achievable with polynomial dimensional surrogates. The results bridge combinatorial representations (MinHash, Möbius inversion) with learning-theoretic calibration, offering practical surrogates with explicit regret bounds.

Abstract: The per-instance Jaccard score, or intersection over union (IoU), is standard in multi-label classification and binary segmentation. With $s$ labels, its loss matrix has $2^s$ outcomes and reports. Under the convention $\mathrm{Jac}(\varnothing,\varnothing)=1$, we prove that the Jaccard score, shifted-loss, and ordinary loss matrices are nonsingular and that the loss columns have affine dimension $2^s-1$. The proof combines a finite MinHash Gram representation with Boolean Möbius inversion. For exact calibration, we prove $2^{s-1} \leq \mathrm{CCdim}(L^{\mathrm{Jac}}) \leq 2^s-1$. The lower bound uses a factorially weighted distribution with $2^{s-1}+1$ supported outcomes and Bayes-optimal reports. Consequently, every exactly calibrated convex surrogate requires exponentially many prediction coordinates. We also give two polynomial-dimensional approximation guarantees with explicit regret transfers. A new $F_1$-to-Jaccard transfer turns an existing $(s^2+1)$-dimensional $F_1$ surrogate into a polynomial-time rule with asymptotic Jaccard regret at most $3-2\sqrt{2}$. For any $α>0$ and $0<ρ<1$, a MinHash square-loss surrogate attains Jaccard-regret floor $α$ uniformly over arbitrary conditional label distributions. With probability at least $1-ρ$, the direct construction has dimension $O((s^2+s\log(1/ρ))/α^2)$, while a signed variant has dimension $O((s+\log(1/ρ))/α^2)$. Thus zero-regret calibration requires exponential dimension, whereas every fixed additive regret tolerance admits polynomial prediction dimension.

</details>


### [183] [Defensive Boosting for Online Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13554)
*Georgy Noarov,Aaron Roth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Defensive Booster: a single-weak-learner online boosting algorithm that simultaneously achieves gradient-boosting-style Brier-score guarantees and weak-learning-style error guarantees; includes a strongly adaptive variant; demonstrates strong accuracy and much faster runtime on synthetic and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To obtain two incomparable guarantees that online boosting methods provide separately, enabling robust performance for adversarial online binary forecasting while maintaining efficiency.

Method: Introduce the Defensive Booster, an online boosting algorithm that leverages a dual view of boosting. It achieves Brier-score competitiveness with the best predictor in the span of H at the same rate as online gradient boosting, and, when the realized transcript satisfies a smooth weak-learning condition, attains the same rate guarantees for Brier score and randomized classification error as online classification boosting. The analysis uses a smooth reweighting (ex-post hard-core certificate) to capture failure of the weak-learning condition, and a strongly adaptive variant that guarantees these properties on every time interval. The method is computationally efficient, requiring only a single weak-class learner.

Result: The algorithm is competitive with the best predictor in span(H) for Brier score across any adaptive sequence, matching the online gradient boosting rate. Under the smooth weak-learning condition, it achieves the same rate guarantees for Brier score and randomized classification error as online boosting. It is implemented with a single weak learner, offering substantial efficiency gains compared to prior ensembles. Empirical tests on synthetic and real data streams show strong predictive performance and orders-of-magnitude faster runtime.

Conclusion: The Defensive Booster unifies two previously incomparable online-boosting guarantees into a single, efficient framework. It provides robust performance against adversarial data, supports a strongly adaptive variant with interval-wise guarantees, and is validated by experiments showing superior accuracy and speed relative to baselines.

Abstract: We study online probabilistic forecasting of binary outcomes chosen by an adaptive adversary. Given an online learning algorithm for a weak hypothesis class $H$, we would like to efficiently obtain two incomparable guarantees that existing online boosting techniques provide separately. Online gradient boosting competes in Brier score with the best predictor induced by the span of $H$ on every sequence, but promises nothing when the span does not contain an accurate predictor. Online weak-to-strong boosting drives classification error to zero under a weak-learning condition, but promises little when that condition fails.
  We give a simple defensive forecasting algorithm, the Defensive Booster, that obtains both guarantees. On every adaptive sequence, its Brier score is competitive with the best prediction induced by the span of $H$ at the same rate as online gradient boosting; simultaneously, whenever the realized transcript satisfies the smooth weak-learning condition, its Brier score and randomized classification error satisfy the same rate guarantee as online classification boosting. This is achieved by operationalizing the "dual view" of boosting: When the algorithm's randomized classification error is persistently high, its mistake weights form a smooth reweighting on which every weak hypothesis has low edge, yielding an ex-post hard-core certificate that the weak-learning condition fails. We also develop a strongly adaptive variant, which satisfies both guarantees on every time interval. The Defensive Booster is very efficient: it accesses just one weak-class learner, whereas the prior online boosting methods we compare against maintain large weak-learner ensembles. Experiments on synthetic and real data streams demonstrate its strong predictive performance (sometimes substantially improving over all prior baselines) coupled with orders-of-magnitude faster runtime.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [184] [RoboSynChallenge: Mastering Real-World Dexterity via Generalizing Synthesized Manipulation Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12416)
*Runyi Zhao,Ruixin Wu,Chengkun Li,Hongrui Zhang,Ang Li,Ruixing Jin,Yueci Deng,Yingying Guo,Lihe Ding,Shaocong Dong,Tianfan Xue,Yanjun Gao,Yudong Luo,Pascal Poupart,Simo Wu,Kui Jia,Wei-shi Zheng,Guiliang Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified benchmark for generalizable robotic manipulation that pairs large-scale synthetic data with standardized unseen real-world evaluation; baseline policies are provided to ensure reproducibility and comparability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalizable manipulation remains hindered by limited real-world data diversity and scarcity. A unified benchmark that combines scalable synthetic data with rigorous real-world evaluation aims to drive generalization, data efficiency, and sim-to-real transfer.

Method: Generate large-scale synthetic state-action data across tasks and environments; standardize real-world evaluation on unseen instances; provide baseline policy families (Transformer-, Diffusion-, Vision-Language-Action, and World-Action-Model-based) to ensure reproducibility and comparability.

Result: The abstract outlines benchmark design and baseline implementations but does not report empirical results. It claims potential improvements in generalization and data efficiency through the integrated sim-real framework.

Conclusion: RoboSynChallenge seeks to accelerate development of broadly capable, data-efficient, and adaptable manipulation systems, thereby advancing toward general robotic intelligence by linking scalable simulation with rigorous real-world validation.

Abstract: Achieving generalizable robotic manipulation remains a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Despite rapid advances in model architectures and learning algorithms, progress is often limited by the scarcity and narrow diversity of real-world data. The RoboSynChallenge competition introduces a unified benchmark to evaluate and advance the generalizability of manipulation policies across a spectrum of tasks, environments, and difficulty levels. To alleviate the shortage of realistic data, the challenge integrates large-scale synthetic data generation with standardized real-world robotic evaluation. Participants are encouraged to leverage synthesized state-action trials to improve general-purpose policy learning, while final assessments are conducted exclusively on unseen real-world manipulation environments. Baseline implementations, including Transformer-, Diffusion-, Vision-Language-Action, and World-Action-Model-based policies, are provided to ensure reproducibility and comparability. By coupling scalable simulation-based training with rigorous real-world validation, RoboSynChallenge aims to foster the development of broadly capable, data-efficient, and adaptable manipulation systems, thereby paving the way toward truly general robotic intelligence.

</details>


### [185] [Attune: A Self-Annotation Tool for Understanding Robot Operator Attention Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12650)
*Puqi Zhou,Sungsoo Ray Hong,David Porfirio*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Attune is a pre-deployment tool that uses AI-assisted gaze analysis to elicit and summarize operator attention patterns in multi-robot supervision, revealing variability and guiding attention-aware design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Managing operator attention in multi-robot supervision is challenging; there is a lack of empirical guidance on how to modify robot behavior to modulate gaze; aim to calibrate behavior to different attention profiles using gaze as a design cue.

Method: Developed Attune to automatically detect meaningful gaze shifts, provide AI-assisted annotations for why shifts occurred, and output summaries of gaze patterns. Evaluated in a user study where participants annotated triggers for attention.

Result: Observed variation in gaze patterns across participants; Attune effectively characterizes operator attention and can inform design of behavior changes to capture or sustain attention.

Conclusion: Attune demonstrates a data-driven approach to design attention-aware robot behavior; pre-deployment elicitation can guide calibration of robot behavior to operator attention profiles in multi-robot supervision interfaces.

Abstract: Deploying robot fleets in complex, real-world environments requires human operators to supervise multiple robots simultaneously. Managing operator attention is a fundamental challenge of designing multi-robot supervision interfaces, encompassing both feed layout and feed content (i.e., robot behavior design). Thus far, designers lack empirical guidance on the latter-how to change a robot's behavior to capture, sustain, or relinquish operator attention during multi-robot supervision. In our vision of the future, designers should be able to use this guidance to calibrate robot behavior to different operator attention profiles. Treating operator eye gaze as a robot behavior design clue, we created a pre-deployment elicitation tool called Attune. Attune automatically identifies when meaningful gaze shifts occur, provides AI assistance for annotating why shifts occurred, and outputs a summary of operator gaze patterns for operator review. We evaluated Attune through a user study in which participants annotated the visual triggers that drew their attention. Our findings unveil variation in observed gaze patterns and reveal how Attune helps characterize operator attention.

</details>


### [186] [FUSE: Active Functional Affordance Grounding through Adaptive Semantic-Geometric Evidence Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12683)
*Zhou Chen,Sathyanarayanan N. Aakur*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FUSE enables adaptive, uncertainty-driven exploration for active functional affordance grounding by combining explicit uncertainty-based viewpoint selection with a learned amortized planner; evaluated on a Habitat-based benchmark, achieving superior non-oracle grounding with lower computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agents must identify objects by their function rather than identity, requiring active observation collection. Fixed-viewpoint affordance grounding fails under occlusion/incompleteness; there is a need for strategies that select informative viewpoints to reveal functional cues.

Method: Introduce FUSE, an adaptive semantic-geometric evidence acquisition framework that integrates explicit uncertainty-driven exploration with a learned amortized planner to selectively acquire informative viewpoints for grounding functional queries; includes a Habitat-based benchmark for evaluation.

Result: FUSE yields the highest observed non-oracle grounding performance and reduces computation by 1.33x compared to fully explicit exploration; effective across multiple affordance knowledge sources.

Conclusion: Adaptive, evidence-centric exploration is effective for active functional grounding and can substantially reduce computation while maintaining or improving grounding performance; the Habitat benchmark enables robust evaluation across affordance knowledge sources.

Abstract: Embodied agents must often identify and interact with objects based on their function rather than their identity, requiring them to actively acquire observations that reveal discriminative functional evidence. Existing affordance grounding methods operate from fixed viewpoints and lack mechanisms for deciding where to look when functional cues are occluded or incomplete. We introduce Active Functional Affordance Grounding, a new task in which an agent sequentially explores a scene to identify and spatially ground an object satisfying a functional query. To address this problem, we propose FUSE, an adaptive semantic-geometric evidence acquisition framework that combines explicit uncertainty-driven exploration with a learned amortized planner to efficiently select informative viewpoints. We further introduce a Habitat-based benchmark for evaluating active functional grounding. Experiments show that FUSE achieves the highest observed non-oracle grounding performance while reducing computation by 1.33x relative to fully explicit exploration, and remains effective across multiple affordance knowledge sources.

</details>


### [187] [SAP-Nav: Spatial Semantic Representation Meets Active Perception for Hierarchical Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12707)
*Xuetong Pei,Jian Liu,Vidura Munasinghe,Bo Miao,U-Xuan Tan,Wenrui Ding,Na Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAP-Nav enables online, zero-shot hierarchical open-vocabulary navigation with active perception, building a Queryable Spatial-Semantic Representation from observed rooms and using Active Viewpoint Verification to reposition before verifying candidates; achieves state-of-the-art results without training or maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To solve OVON under partial observations by requiring both persistent environment-level grounding and discriminative candidate verification, without task-specific training or precomputed scene maps.

Method: Incrementally build a Queryable Spatial-Semantic Representation from actively acquired room views; apply Active Viewpoint Verification to assess sufficiency of evidence; reposition to more informative viewpoints when needed; supports both hierarchical and standard OVON in a fully online, zero-shot framework without scene maps or task-specific training.

Result: SAP-Nav achieves the best overall performance on LangMap and HM3D-OVON, including a 12.2% Absolute SR improvement over training-based methods on region-level navigation; validated in real-world robot experiments.

Conclusion: SAP-Nav provides a practical, general framework for hierarchical OVON under partial observations, combining active perception with online spatial-semantic reasoning, and requiring no task-specific training or precomputed maps.

Abstract: Hierarchical open-vocabulary object navigation (OVON) requires agents to follow free-form instructions that may specify targets through scene-, room-, region-, and instance-level cues in unseen environments. Although recent work LangMap has formalized this setting, reliably solving it under partial observations remains challenging: spatial grounding requires persistent environment-level evidence, whereas target verification requires clear and discriminative candidate views. We present SAP-Nav, a fully online, zero-shot framework that addresses both requirements through active perception. SAP-Nav incrementally constructs a Queryable Spatial-Semantic Representation from actively acquired room views, enabling spatial semantic queries from any explored location. It further employs Active Viewpoint Verification to assess whether the current observation provides sufficient evidence and, when necessary, reposition the agent to a more informative viewpoint before verifying candidates against category and attribute constraints. Although designed for hierarchical OVON, SAP-Nav supports both hierarchical and standard category-level OVON without task-specific training or precomputed scene maps. Experiments on LangMap and HM3D-OVON show that SAP-Nav achieves the overall best performance, including a 12.2% improvement in SR over training-based methods on region-level navigation. Real-world robot experiments further demonstrate its practical feasibility. Code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [188] [Genetic Fuzzy System-Based Multi-Robot Coordination for Planetary Missions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12755)
*Daegyun Choi,Donghoon Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A decentralized multi-robot transport framework using GA-optimized fuzzy controllers on a 2D traversability map derived from elevation data; aims to minimize total path length while avoiding obstacles, validated across scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce problem dimensionality and enable obstacle-aware, decentralized control for collaborative object transport in unstructured environments using learning-based velocity controllers.

Method: 1) Perform terrain slope analysis on an elevation map to create a 2D traversability map and mark non-traversable regions as obstacles. 2) Train fuzzy inference systems (FIS) to generate MRS velocity for transporting an object toward a target. 3) Optimize FIS parameters with a genetic algorithm across scenarios including local minima, near-obstacle targets, and clutter. 4) Apply trained FIS to the testing traversability map and validate with multiple scenarios.

Result: The FIS models trained via GA are applied to the testing environment and validated across several scenarios, demonstrating feasibility of the approach for decentralized MRS transport with obstacle avoidance.

Conclusion: GA-optimized fuzzy controllers on traversability maps can effectively enable decentralized multi-robot object transport in unstructured environments and generalize to testing scenarios.

Abstract: This paper proposes a decentralized approach for a multi-robot system (MRS) using a genetic fuzzy system to perform a collaborative object transportation task that minimizes the total path length of the MRS in unstructured environment while avoiding obstacles. For an environment given by an elevation map, terrain traversability analysis with respect to the slope is performed to reduce the dimension and identify non-traversable areas that can be considered as obstacles, and the given map is converted into a traversability map in two dimensional space. In the training process, proposed fuzzy inference systems (FISs) to generate the MRS's velocity for transporting an object to a target position are optimized by a genetic algorithm with several scenarios, such as a local minima, a target that is close to an obstacle, and a cluttered environment. The trained FIS models are applied to the testing environment, which is the converted traversability map, and validated using multiple scenarios.

</details>


### [189] [AirForesight: Current-to-Future Spatial Map Imagination with Cross-Space Planning Consistency for UAV-VLN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12835)
*Yutong Liu,Xiaojie Li,Mingzhu Xu,Jianlong Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AirForesight introduces a current-to-future spatial map imagination framework for UAV-VLN that learns a structured current map from multi-view data, predicts future motion, and uses cross-space planning consistency to align predictions with expert actions, yielding strong results on OpenUAV and AerialVLN-S.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current UAV vision-language navigation methods map vision-language inputs directly to actions, lacking explicit scene grounding and future-aware spatial reasoning; there is a need for structured spatial representations that support planning.

Method: The framework learns a structured current-map representation from multi-view observations, supervised by current-map reconstruction and future-trajectory prediction. It uses structured causal attention to propagate current spatial knowledge to future-map reasoning, then aggregates current and future representations to predict the next 3D waypoint. A cross-space planning consistency loss enforces directional agreement between the map-space trajectory and the expert action direction derived from the ground-truth waypoint displacement.

Result: Experiments on OpenUAV and AerialVLN-S show strong performance; ablations validate the effectiveness and stability of the proposed framework.

Conclusion: AirForesight offers a current-to-future imagination approach for UAV-VLN that enables better scene grounding and future-oriented planning through structured map representations, causal attention-guided reasoning, and planning-consistency constraints, with demonstrated improvements over baselines.

Abstract: Unmanned Aerial Vehicle Vision-Language Navigation (UAV-VLN) requires agents to follow language instructions, infer spatial structure from sparse multi-view observations, and execute feasible 3D motion in complex outdoor environments. Despite recent progress with large language models, most existing methods still map vision-language inputs directly to actions, providing limited explicit scene grounding and future-aware spatial reasoning. We propose AirForesight, a current-to-future spatial map imagination framework for UAV-VLN. AirForesight first learns a structured current-map representation from multi-view observations. This representation is jointly supervised by current-map reconstruction and future-trajectory prediction, encouraging it to encode both present scene structure and future motion intent. Under structured causal attention, the current spatial knowledge is propagated to future-map reasoning, and the resulting current and future representations are aggregated to predict the next 3D waypoint. To make spatial imagination more relevant to navigation, we introduce a cross-space planning consistency loss that encourages directional agreement between the predicted map-space trajectory and the expert action direction derived from the ground-truth waypoint displacement. Experiments on OpenUAV and AerialVLN-S, together with extensive ablations, demonstrate strong performance and support the effectiveness and stability of the proposed framework.

</details>


### [190] [ASPIRE-VINS: Adaptive Spline-based Visual-inertial Navigation System With Robust 3D Measurement Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12840)
*Kwangyik Jung,Eungchang Mason Lee,Taekjun Oh,Hyun Myung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ASPIRE-VINS introduces an adaptive, motion-aware continuous-time VINS by combining adaptive knot placement, multi-resolution splines, and 3D measurement-space residuals, achieving competitive trajectory accuracy under varying motion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete-time VINS with fixed knot spacing struggles to simultaneously capture fast dynamics and static intervals; continuous-time splines alleviate this but uniform knots can under- or over-parameterize. A motion-adaptive, continuous-time approach with bearing-consistent measurements is needed for robustness and accuracy.

Method: Proposes ASPIRE-VINS: (1) adaptive knot placement (AKP) to align knots with local motion variation; (2) multi-resolution splines (MRS) to enable bounded, local refinement in tangent space; (3) 3D measurement-space residuals (3D-MSR) to enforce bearing consistency by matching transformed features to calibrated observation rays in 3D space.

Result: Experiments show ASPIRE-VINS achieves competitive or lower trajectory errors compared with baselines across diverse motion and sensing conditions, indicating effective motion-adaptive continuous-time trajectory modeling.

Conclusion: Motion-adaptive continuous-time trajectory modeling with AKP, MRS, and 3D-MSR improves VINS performance, offering accuracy and flexibility under varying dynamics and sensing setups.

Abstract: Visual-inertial navigation systems estimate six-degree-of-freedom motion by fusing visual and inertial data. Modern discrete-time methods with IMU preintegration provide strong accuracy and efficiency, but keyframe-based representations can be less flexible when residuals must be evaluated at arbitrary timestamps or when motion-dependent temporal resolution is needed. Continuous-time splines address this issue by representing the trajectory as a smooth temporal function, but uniformly spaced knots can under-represent rapid dynamics or over-parameterize static intervals. This letter proposes ASPIRE-VINS, a continuous-time VINS framework that combines adaptive knot placement (AKP), multi-resolution splines (MRS), and 3D measurement-space residuals (3D-MSR). AKP allocates knots according to local motion variation, MRS adds bounded local refinement in tangent space, and 3D-MSR provides bearing consistency by aligning transformed features with calibrated observation rays in 3D measurement space. Experiments show that ASPIRE-VINS achieves competitive or lower trajectory errors than the compared baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of motion-adaptive continuous-time trajectory modeling under diverse motion and sensing conditions.

</details>


### [191] [BrainWAM: Action-Space Coordination of Semantic Priors and Predictive Dynamics for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12854)
*Bing Zhan,Shuyao Shang,Jiahao Gu,Shuo Lu,Yuan Xu,Zhao Wang,Yida Wang,Xueyang Zhang,Kun Zhan,Lue Fan,Zhaoxiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BrainWAM introduces a structured action-space coordination framework for autonomous driving that separates semantic reasoning and predictive world modeling into two specialized, action-oriented pathways and synchronizes them at compact action representations, with an asynchronous rectified-flow inference to reduce latency. It achieves state-of-the-art results on NAVSIM v1 and v2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Naive joint token-level attention for combining Vision-Language-Action (VLA) priors and World Action Models (WAMs) leads to attention-allocation mismatch where semantic shortcuts dominate, hindering predictive dynamics. A coordinated, specialized architecture is needed to leverage both semantic priors and predictive dynamics without interference.

Method: BrainWAM comprises two specialized action-oriented pathways: a semantic reasoning pathway and a predictive world modeling pathway, aligned at compact action representations. It employs an asynchronous rectified-flow inference strategy with decoupled video and action denoising to reduce inference latency while preserving context.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on NAVSIM v1 (89.5 PDMS) and NAVSIM v2 (89.6 EPDMS), consistently outperforming both VLA-only and WAM-only baselines.

Conclusion: BrainWAM offers a practical and promising direction for autonomous driving by coordinating specialized systems for semantic reasoning and predictive dynamics, achieving superior planning performance with reduced latency.

Abstract: Autonomous driving requires planning under both semantic constraints and predictive dynamics. Existing end-to-end driving approaches, however, typically emphasize only one side of this requirement: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models exploit VLM priors for semantic reasoning, while World Action Models (WAMs) provide future-aware prediction through generative world modeling. This naturally motivates a unified planner that can leverage both semantic priors and predictive dynamics. However, we find that a naive combination through joint token-level attention suffers from an attention-allocation mismatch, where semantic shortcuts dominate the shared attention space and suppress predictive dynamics. Inspired by neuroscience evidence that complex behavior arises from coordination among functionally specialized systems, we propose BrainWAM, a structured action-space coordination framework that converts semantic reasoning and predictive world modeling into two specialized action-oriented pathways, and aligns them at the level of compact action representations. We further introduce an asynchronous rectified-flow inference strategy with decoupled video and action denoising, which shortens inference latency while preserving planning-relevant predictive context. BrainWAM reaches state-of-the-art performance on both NAVSIM v1 (89.5 PDMS) and NAVSIM v2 (89.6 EPDMS), consistently outperforming VLA-only or WAM-only methods, highlighting BrainWAM as a practical and promising direction for autonomous driving systems.

</details>


### [192] [HumanoidVLN: A Physics-Grounded Simulator and Benchmark for Vision-Language Navigation Across Diverse Humanoid Embodiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12860)
*Quan-Dung Pham,Anh Dao,The-Anh Nguyen,Minh Nguyen-Dinh,Phuong Nam Dang,Tri Pham,Hung Tran,Bach Dao,Tuyen P. Le,Truong Nguyen,Quan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumanoidVLN provides a physics-grounded VLN benchmark and simulator across multiple humanoid embodiments, enabling cross-robot VLN evaluation under realistic locomotion constraints; JanusVLN leads, sim-to-real shows strong correlation; code and data to be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLN benchmarks often use wheeled agents and fixed viewpoints, neglectting bipedal locomotion constraints, varied humanoid morphologies, and motion-induced egocentric distortions; there is a need for a physics-based, cross-embodiment VLN platform to study interaction between navigation policies and locomotion controllers.

Method: Develop HumanoidVLN on NVIDIA Isaac Sim with four humanoid embodiments (Unitree G1, Unitree H1, Internal-A, Internal-B) covering 10–12 lower-body DoF and heights 1.17–1.80 m. Use a hierarchical control stack: a reinforcement learning locomotion policy with interchangeable PD or MPC path trackers. Ensure compatibility with NaVILA, DualVLN, StreamVLN, and JanusVLN. Create environments from artist-designed scenes and 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructions with navigable areas >100 m^2. Generate 933 collision-aware reference episodes via a dual-generator, reviewer, and paraphraser multi-agent pipeline with human-in-the-loop verification, each episode paired with one fine-grained instruction and three coarse-grained variants (formal, natural, casual). Evaluate across four models and four embodiments; perform a 20-episode sim-to-real pilot.

Result: JanusVLN achieves the highest mean success rate of 43.55% and nDTW of 48.38 across configurations. In a 20-episode sim-to-real pilot with DualVLN and Unitree G1, navigation errors correlate strongly (r=0.935), with mean absolute displacement 0.68 m and mean trajectory similarity 0.782 (±0.188) nDTW, indicating meaningful alignment between simulation and real-world execution.

Conclusion: HumanoidVLN demonstrates that VLN performance is shaped by the interplay between navigation models, locomotion controllers, and humanoid embodiment under physical execution. The platform enables rapid integration of new robots and models; code, benchmark, and data will be released upon acceptance to foster cross-embodiment VLN research.

Abstract: Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) for humanoid robots poses challenges existing benchmarks fail to address: bipedal locomotion imposes physical constraints absent from wheeled agents, humanoid morphologies vary across platforms, and egocentric observations are distorted by locomotion-induced camera dynamics. We present HumanoidVLN, a physics-grounded simulator and benchmark for VLN across diverse humanoid embodiments. Built on NVIDIA Isaac Sim, our platform supports an extensible set of humanoid configurations, demonstrated on four robots (Unitree G1, Unitree H1, Internal-A, Internal-B) spanning 10-12 lower-body DoF and heights from 1.17m to 1.80m, via a hierarchical control stack combining a reinforcement learning locomotion policy with interchangeable PD or MPC path trackers. New robots and VLN models integrate with minimal effort; we demonstrate compatibility with NaVILA, DualVLN, StreamVLN, and JanusVLN. Environments are drawn from artist-designed scenes and 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructions, filtered for navigable areas exceeding 100 square meters. Instructions are generated by a dual generator-reviewer plus paraphraser multi-agent pipeline with human-in-the-loop verification, yielding 933 collision-aware reference episodes, each paired with one fine-grained instruction and three coarse-grained stylistic variants (formal, natural, casual). Across four models and four embodiments, JanusVLN achieves the highest mean success rate of 43.55% and nDTW of 48.38. In a 20-episode sim-to-real pilot with DualVLN and the Unitree G1, navigation errors correlate strongly (r=0.935), with a mean absolute difference of 0.68m and mean trajectory similarity of 0.782 (+/-0.188) nDTW. These results highlight the interaction between VLN models, controllers, and humanoid embodiments under physical execution. Code, benchmark, and data will be released upon acceptance at https://humanoid-vln.github.io/.

</details>


### [193] [AMR-Pose: An Active LED Marker-Based Relative Pose Estimation Framework With Probabilistic Switching PnP for Cooperative AUVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12866)
*Zeyu Sha,Xiaorui Wang,Mingyang Yang,Feitian Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AMR-Pose delivers robust, real-time relative pose estimation for cooperative AUVs using an active LED marker and a probabilistic switching PnP on SE(3), validated in water-tank and leader-follower tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater optical degradation undermines vision-based localization; there is need for robust relative pose estimation for cooperative tasks.

Method: Design of a compact LED marker module (red center, three blue peripherals) on leader AUV; detection yields marker observations; propose PSwPnP estimator that fuses probabilistic marker association and visibility-adaptive measurements; applies Lie-group pose propagation on SE(3).

Result: Water-tank experiments with ground-truth MoCap; closed-loop leader-follower experiments; demonstrate accuracy, smoothness, robustness, and real-time capability.

Conclusion: AMR-Pose enables reliable relative pose estimation in challenging underwater environments; combines active markers with probabilistic switching PnP and SE(3) propagation; suitable for cooperative underwater robotics.

Abstract: Reliable relative pose estimation between autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) is critical for cooperative ocean exploration, sampling, and multi-robot coordination. However, achieving robust vision-based relative localization in underwater environments remains challenging due to severe optical degradation, including turbidity, illumination variations, reflections, and intermittent feature occlusions. This paper presents AMR-Pose, an active LED marker-based relative pose estimation framework for cooperative AUVs. A compact marker module consisting of one red central LED and three blue peripheral LEDs is developed and integrated onto the leader AUV to provide distinctive visual features under complex underwater conditions. Building upon the detected marker observations, a probabilistic switching Perspective-n-Point estimator (PSwPnP) is developed by combining Lie-group pose propagation on $SE(3)$, probabilistic marker association, and visibility-adaptive measurement fusion for robust six-degree-of-freedom relative pose estimation. The proposed framework dynamically adapts the estimation process according to marker visibility, maintaining geometric consistency and temporal stability during partial observations and visibility transitions. Extensive water-tank experiments with motion-capture ground truth validate that AMR-Pose achieves accurate, smooth, and robust relative pose estimation under challenging underwater conditions. Closed-loop leader-follower experiments further demonstrate its feasibility for real-time relative pose feedback in cooperative underwater robotics.

</details>


### [194] [Temporal GRPO: Beyond Trajectory-Level Credit in Vision-Language-Action Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13026)
*Yao Zhou,Hang Gao,Fengge Wu,Changwen Zheng,Wenwen Qiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Temporal GRPO mitigates trajectory-level credit aliasing in outcome-driven VLA post-training by aligning rollouts with detected task stages and updating stage-specific intervals, yielding better sample efficiency and task success; effects observed on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO-Long.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In GRPO-based VLA post-training, a rollout-attribution at the trajectory level causes early progress to be penalized by later failures (credit aliasing). Need stage-aware credit assignment to credit only actions within the corresponding stage.

Method: Identify detectable task stages; map each rollout to stage-specific action intervals; compute stage-specific advantages; apply each stage's advantage to its intervals in a single policy update; evaluate on RoboTwin 2.0; use controlled updates on LIBERO-Long to preserve prerequisite stages and localize improvements.

Result: On RoboTwin 2.0, Temporal GRPO improves task success and sample efficiency with consistent gains across horizons. LIBERO-Long experiments show controlled updates preserving shared prerequisites and focusing improvement at the first divergence stage.

Conclusion: Stage-aware credit assignment via Temporal GRPO effectively alleviates trajectory-level credit aliasing, improves learning efficiency and performance in long-horizon VLA tasks, and generalizes across task horizons and task families.

Abstract: Outcome-driven reinforcement learning offers a scalable way to post-train vision-language-action (VLA) policies from sparse task-success feedback. In common GRPO-based VLA post-training, one rollout-level advantage is applied to every action in the trajectory. A rollout that completes several valid stages but fails later can therefore penalize the actions that produced its earlier progress. We call this trajectory-level credit aliasing. Temporal GRPO addresses this problem by constructing detectable task stages, aligning each rollout with stage-specific action intervals, and comparing only rollouts that have entered the same stage. The resulting stage advantages are applied to their corresponding intervals in a single policy update. On RoboTwin 2.0, Temporal GRPO improves task success and sample efficiency, with consistent gains across task horizons. Controlled updates on LIBERO-Long preserve shared prerequisite stages and concentrate improvement at the first stage where rollout outcomes diverge.

</details>


### [195] [H2R-Bench: Benchmarking Human-to-Robot Manipulation Video Generation in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13049)
*Dingyi Rong,Yue Shi,Chaofan Ma,Jiezhang Cao,Zongrui Wang,Zeyu Zhang,Yao Mu,Guangtao Zhai,Ning Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: H2R-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating cross-embodiment human-to-robot manipulation video generation, assessing whether egocentric human demonstrations can be transformed into robot-centric videos under specified embodiments. It evaluates five dimensions of generated videos across eleven models, six manipulation families, and two robot embodiments, revealing current world models struggle with embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale robot manipulation data is hard to collect, while abundant egocentric human manipulation videos exist. Transferring human demonstrations to robots is challenging due to embodiment gaps. Video world models offer a pathway to synthesize robot-centric videos, but cross-embodiment transfer remains underexplored and needs systematic evaluation.

Method: Propose H2R-Bench, a benchmark where each instance includes a human demonstration video, target embodiment constraints, and source-grounded annotations (task goals, action events, functional contacts, object responses). Evaluation covers five dimensions: goal-state completion, action-event completion, functional contact transfer, embodiment correctness, and general video quality. Benchmark eleven state-of-the-art video generation models across six manipulation families and two robot embodiments.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows current video world models are limited in cross-embodiment transfer: leading models often fail to maintain embodiment consistency, functional interactions, and task execution in robot-centric videos.

Conclusion: H2R-Bench provides a systematic diagnostic framework to assess whether video world models can bridge the human-to-robot embodiment gap and convert human manipulation observations into robot-centric training data; highlights gaps and guides future improvement in cross-embodiment video synthesis.

Abstract: Large-scale manipulation data is essential for robot learning, yet collecting robot demonstrations remains expensive and difficult to scale. Meanwhile, abundant egocentric human manipulation videos provide rich behavioral experiences, but transferring them across embodiments remains challenging due to differences between human hands and robotic end-effectors. Recent advances in video world models offer a promising pathway to synthesize robot-centric manipulation videos from human observations, while their cross-embodiment transfer capability remains largely unexplored.
