Therefore, we introduce H2R-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating cross-embodiment human-to-robot manipulation video generation, where models transform egocentric human demonstrations into robot manipulation videos under specified embodiments. Each benchmark instance contains a human demonstration video, target embodiment constraints, and source-grounded annotations covering task goals, action events, functional contacts, and object responses. H2R-Bench evaluates generated videos through five dimensions, including goal-state completion, action-event completion, functional contact transfer, embodiment correctness, and general video quality. We benchmark eleven state-of-the-art video generation models across six manipulation families and two robot embodiments. Our evaluation reveals that current video world models remain limited in human-to-robot manipulation transfer: even leading models often fail in embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution. H2R-Bench provides a systematic diagnostic framework for evaluating whether video world models can bridge the human-to-robot embodiment gap and convert human manipulation observations into robot-centric training resources.

</details>


### [196] [Semantic Radiance Fields as Simulators for Spatial Reasoning in Real-World Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13095)
*Nico Heider,Michał Jan Włodarczyk,Katarzyna Wasielewska-Michniewska,Przemysław Hołda,Martin Schieck,Marcin Paprzycki,Maria Ganzha,Bogdan Franczyk*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Semantic Radiance Fields (SRFs) unify geometry, appearance, and per-class semantics in a single 3D radiance field reconstructed from real scenes, enabling rendering, semantic/occupancy queries, and novel-view synthesis for training/evaluating spatial reasoning in embodied agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between realistic, photorealistic reconstructions and ground-truth semantic data to enable semantically grounded, geometry-faithful simulators for embodied AI.

Method: Lift 2D semantic segmentations from pretrained vision models into a 3D radiance field that jointly encodes geometry, appearance, and per-class semantics; reconstruct SRFs from posed RGB captures of real scenes; support novel-view rendering, semantic queries, and free-space occupancy queries within a unified representation; use SRFs to generate diverse real-world environments for training/evaluation; demonstrate via an orchard apple-reaching task where SRF provides rendering, semantic GT, and occupancy to a physics engine.

Result: SRFs provide a grounded, queryable, and photorealistic representation that enables efficient generation of diverse real-world environments for spatial reasoning benchmarks; the orchard task illustrates end-to-end use including rendering, semantic grounding, and occupancy integration with physics.

Conclusion: SRFs offer a unified representation that combines geometry, appearance, and semantics, enabling realistic, semantically informed simulators for spatial reasoning in embodied agents, with potential to scale diverse, real-world environments for training and evaluation.

Abstract: Training and evaluating spatial reasoning in embodied agents requires diverse environments that are both geometrically faithful and semantically queryable. Synthetic simulators offer ground truth semantics but sacrifice realism; simulators based on reconstructions of real-world environments have realistic appearance but lack ground truth semantics by default. We propose using Semantic Radiance Fields (SRF) as simulators for spatial reasoning agents. SRFs are a representation that unifies these requirements by lifting 2D semantic segmentations from pretrained vision models into a 3D radiance field that jointly encodes geometry, appearance, and per-class semantic identity. The resulting fields are reconstructed from posed RGB captures of real scenes and support novel-view synthesis, semantic and free-space queries within a single grounded representation. This enables the efficient generation of diverse real-world environments to train and evaluate spatial reasoning models. As an example application, we outline an SRF-driven simulator for an orchard apple-reaching task, in which the radiance field supplies camera rendering, semantic ground truth, and occupancy queries to a physics engine.

</details>


### [197] [S2-HWM: Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model for Long-Horizon Surgical Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13103)
*Shuzhe Zhang,Xin Zhu,Yinling Qian,Qiong Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model (S2-HWM) that uses sparse event evidence to coordinate a high-level event manager with a low-level primitive-step worker, enabling variable-duration planning for long-horizon surgical tasks; achieves high success on SurRoL PegTransfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse rewards and irregular interaction make long-horizon manipulation hard; existing world-models operate at fixed-step horizons and manually defined stages can misalign with state-dependent transitions.

Method: Learn sparse event evidence from primitive latent trajectories to coordinate an event-level manager and a primitive-step worker. The event evidence schedules manager goal updates, and each latent goal conditions the worker's primitive actions until the next update. The event evidence forms variable-duration segments for an Event Transition Model (ETM) that predicts the next boundary stochastic state, segment duration, and accumulated segment reward. Chaining event-level predictions extends the imagination horizon; the worker uses a primitive-step actor-critic.

Result: On a SurRoL-based PegTransfer task, S2-HWM achieves 98.7% success rate, outperforming the flat GAS DreamerV3 baseline by 22.7 percentage points.

Conclusion: S2-HWM effectively couples sparse, event-driven planning with primitive-step control to handle variable-duration interactions, extending the planning horizon beyond primitive imagination and improving long-horizon manipulation performance.

Abstract: Long-horizon surgical robot manipulation is challenging because task rewards are sparse, while meaningful interaction changes occur at irregular intervals. Existing world-model agents typically imagine at primitive-step resolution, leaving variable-duration task progress implicit. Manually specified stages can provide intermediate structure, but their task specific boundaries are difficult to align with state-dependent interaction transitions. We propose S2-HWM, a Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model that learns sparse event evidence from primitive latent trajectories to coordinate an event-level manager and a primitive-step worker. The event evidence schedules manager goal updates, and each selected latent goal conditions the worker's primitive actions until the next update. The learned event evidence also forms variable-duration segments for an Event Transition Model (ETM), which predicts the next?boundary stochastic state, segment duration, and accumulated segment reward. Chaining these event-level predictions provides a variable-duration continuation beyond the primitive imagination horizon for manager learning, while the worker retains primitive-step actor-critic learning. On a SurRoL-based PegTransfer task, S2-HWM achieves a success rate of 98.7%, outperforming the flat GAS DreamerV3 baseline by 22.7 percentage points.

</details>


### [198] [FAM-DQ: A Dual-Quadrotor-Based Fully Actuated Aerial Manipulator for High-Torque Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13220)
*Xuwei Yang,Ruoyu Ren,Ziqian Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes FAM-DQ, a dual-quadrotor fully actuated aerial manipulator with decoupled 6-DoF end-effector control and high torque, using two propulsion modules at frame ends and a gear-driven servo for orientation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitations of conventional aerial manipulators: underactuated designs suffer from strong position-attitude coupling; fully actuated designs offer higher control but face structural complexity, payload limits, and insufficient torque.

Method: Arrange two quadrotor propulsion modules at the ends of a central frame connected by passive joints; use a gear-driven servo to regulate end-effector pointing; achieve decoupled 6-DoF control; validate via experiments including trajectory tracking, attitude tracking, static torque measurement, and screw driving.

Result: Achieves maximum torque of 1.019 N·m with total mass 0.447 kg, yielding torque-to-mass ratio of 2.28 N·m/kg; demonstrates decoupled 6-DoF end-effector control, omnidirectional manipulation, and enhanced torque output.

Conclusion: The FAM-DQ design delivers high-torque, decoupled 6-DoF aerial manipulation in a lightweight dual-quadrotor configuration, with experimental results supporting its effectiveness for high-torque physical interaction tasks.

Abstract: Aerial physical interaction requires aerial manipulation platforms to generate large interaction forces and torques while maintaining precise end-effector control. However, conventional underactuated aerial manipulators suffer from strong position-attitude coupling, whereas fully actuated platform designs often face structural complexity, limited payload capacity, and insufficient torque output. This paper presents FAM-DQ, a dual-quadrotor based fully actuated aerial manipulator designed for high-torque physical interaction tasks. By mounting two quadrotor propulsion modules at the ends of a central frame through passive joints, while using a gear-driven servo to regulate the pointing direction, FAM-DQ achieves decoupled $6$-DoF end-effector control with omnidirectional manipulation capability and enhanced torque output. Experiments including trajectory tracking, attitude tracking, static torque measurement, and screw driving validate the proposed design. FAM-DQ achieves a maximum torque of $1.019~\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{m}$ with a total mass of $0.447~\mathrm{kg}$, corresponding to a torque-to-mass ratio of $2.28~\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{m/kg}$.

</details>


### [199] [Manufacturing Complex Airtight Soft Pneumatic Actuators for Soft Robotics: Process Evaluation and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13233)
*Mohammed Abboodi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A comparative, manufacturing-focused evaluation of fabrication routes for complex soft pneumatic actuators, identifying process-specific limitations and optimization strategies, with FDM as the most adaptable route and a design-for-manufacturing framework guiding route selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable geometric fidelity, compliance, structural integrity, and airtightness in soft pneumatic actuators by systematically evaluating fabrication methods and isolating intrinsic process limitations from fixable defects.

Method: Process screening, baseline fabrication, failure analysis, and process improvement across heat-shrink forming, silicone casting, powder- and liquid-based additive manufacturing, and FDM; assessment of enclosure air-tightness, wall thickness effects, extrusion-path architecture, and internal access for post-processing.

Result: Each fabrication route has distinct limitations: heat-shrink forming limited by geometric conformity; silicone casting constrained by mold access and bonded interfaces; powder-based methods struggle with residual material in enclosed passages; DLP (digital light processing) limited by material properties and post-processing; FDM is the most adaptable route, with defects reducible via optimization. Airtightness depends on contour geometry and extrusion-path architecture; design choices affecting internal access for post-processing, e.g., support-free geometries.

Conclusion: A practical design-for-manufacturing approach—selecting fabrication routes by actuator architecture and manufacturing constraints—guides development of complex, flexible, and airtight soft pneumatic actuators for soft robotics.

Abstract: Manufacturing complex soft pneumatic actuators remains challenging because geometric fidelity, compliance, structural integrity, and airtightness must be achieved simultaneously. This study presents a manufacturing-focused evaluation of several fabrication routes for complex pneumatic structures, including heat-shrink forming, silicone casting, powder- and liquid-based additive manufacturing, and fused deposition modeling (FDM). The processes were assessed through process screening, baseline fabrication, failure analysis, and process improvement to distinguish inherent process limitations from correctable manufacturing defects. Heat-shrink forming was limited by geometric conformity, casting by mold accessibility and bonded interfaces, powder-based methods by residual material trapped within enclosed passages, and digital light processing by the material properties and post-processing requirements of the investigated system. FDM provided the most adaptable route because its dominant defects could be progressively reduced through process optimization. The results further showed that airtightness depends not only on nominal wall thickness but also on extrusion-path architecture, while support-free geometry is important when access for internal post-processing is limited. These findings establish a practical design-for-manufacturing approach in which process selection is guided by the compatibility between actuator architecture and manufacturing constraints. The proposed approach provides practical guidance for developing complex, flexible, and airtight soft pneumatic actuators for soft robotic applications

</details>


### [200] [Predictive Relative-Velocity Steering for Safe Robotic Manipulator Teleoperation in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13284)
*Changhao Hu,Zeyi Liu,Songqiao Hu,Shuang Liu,Zihan Meng,Xiao He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lightweight, end-effector velocity-level proactive collision-avoidance framework for teleoperation using TTC-based prediction with overshoot protection and Rodrigues rotation to deflect direction while preserving speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety risks in teleoperation due to latency and limited operator attention necessitate fast, reliable collision avoidance that can operate at high control rates and compensate for perception delays.

Method: Preprocess point cloud; predict potential collisions via time-to-collision (TTC) with integrated overshoot protection; rotate the relative-velocity vector using Rodrigues' rotation formula to deflect direction without reducing magnitude; this mitigates deadlock seen in traditional APF methods. The prediction module compensates for point-cloud processing latency; the design remains lightweight for high-frequency control.

Result: Simulation results show higher end-effector collision-avoidance rates compared with baseline methods. Experimental validation on a physical robot confirms the approach effectively avoids collisions in practice.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a lightweight, modular solution for proactive collision avoidance at the end-effector velocity level, addressing latency and deadlock issues while preserving motion magnitude and enabling high-frequency teleoperation control.

Abstract: Recent advances in teleoperation have enabled robotic manipulators to perform dexterous, human-arm-like motions. However, human operators may fail to avoid suddenly appearing obstacles promptly and effectively, particularly under network latency or limited attention, thereby creating safety risks. To address this issue, we propose a lightweight and modular framework for proactive collision avoidance, operating directly at the end-effector velocity-command level. After preprocessing the point cloud, the framework first predicts potential collisions based on time-to-collision (TTC) with integrated overshoot protection, and subsequently rotates the relative-velocity vector using Rodrigues' rotation formula. The deflection changes only the direction of the relative velocity while preserving its magnitude, thereby mitigating the deadlock problem commonly encountered by conventional artificial potential field (APF) methods. The prediction module compensates for point-cloud processing latency introduced by complex teleoperation pipelines, while the lightweight design enables the high-frequency control required for teleoperation. Simulations across diverse scenarios show that the proposed method achieves a higher end-effector collision avoidance rate than the baseline methods. Experiments on a physical robotic system further validate its collision-avoidance effectiveness.

</details>


### [201] [NestDex: Nested Policy Learning with Copilot Assisted Teleoperation for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13362)
*James Zhao,Jinhe Tang,Mingyuan Ba,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NestDex uses a hierarchical, hand-skill-assisted framework to ease demonstration collection for dexterous manipulation and trains a separate outer visuomotor policy for autonomous control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation requires precise, contact-rich finger control synchronized with arm movement, making complete demonstrations hard to collect. Traditional setups demand continuous finger trajectories, creating high burden and inconsistency in demonstrations.

Method: NestDex introduces an inner hand policy that adapts from recent proprioceptive history and is guided by a single-DoF clutch; a vision-language selector activates the appropriate hand skill for each task stage; the outer visuomotor policy is trained from demonstrations to control arm and hand without relying on the inner policies at deployment. A hand-action variational autoencoder compresses high-dimensional hand actions into compact targets while preserving joint-space arm commands. Demonstrations are collected with the operator modulating the active hand skill, reducing burden; at deployment, only the outer policy is used.

Result: Real-world dexterous manipulation experiments show improved demonstration reliability and efficiency, and empirical evaluations indicate effective learning of autonomous policies.

Conclusion: NestDex reduces demonstration burden for dexterous tasks and enables reliable autonomous policy learning by decoupling hand skill demos from the outer controller, with a compression via a hand-action VAE that preserves arm commands.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation promises substantially richer robot interaction with the physical world, but learning these behaviours remains constrained by the difficulty of collecting consistent, complete-task demonstrations. Unlike parallel-jaw manipulation, dexterous tasks require the operator to coordinate arm motion with precise, contact-rich finger behaviour throughout the task. We introduce NestDex, a nested policy-learning framework that reduces this burden by using learned hand skills to assist demonstration collection. The operator controls the arm and regulates the active hand skill through a single-DoF clutch, rather than directly specifying the full finger trajectory. The inner hand policy adapts its motion from the latest proprioceptive history, while a vision-language selector activates the appropriate skill for each task stage. The resulting demonstrations train a separate outer visuomotor policy that controls both the arm and hand without the inner policies at deployment. A hand-action variational autoencoder provides compact hand-action targets while retaining arm commands in joint space. Across real-world dexterous manipulation experiments, NestDex improves demonstration reliability and efficiency, and the resulting empirical evaluations support effective autonomous policy learning. Video Demo are available at project website https://aus.bot/research/nestdex.

</details>


### [202] [FIRE-VLA: Failure-Informed Self-Evolution for Vision-Language-Action Models in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13395)
*Hao Dou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FIRE-VLA uses failure-informed self-distillation to turn unresolved rollout failures into privileged supervision for VLA policy evolution, combining GRPO with a self-distilled teacher of equal size and no external teacher, improving mean L2 error and reducing persistent failures on nuScenes data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address GRPO's limitation when all sampled trajectories are poor by converting failure signals into informative supervision within iterative policy updates, enabling the model to adapt its behavior to previously failing regions without needing a larger external teacher.

Method: Start from Qwen2.5-VL-3B SFT. Maintain teacher and student of equal parameter scale. The teacher observes the hidden future trajectory; supervision follows the student's generated prefix and is restricted to answer tokens. Apply GRPO for every rollout group. Trigger self-distillation from a frozen round-start copy of the same model for low-reward, low-diversity groups. The updated policy serves as the teacher in the next round, enabling the failure distribution to shift with policy improvements without external teachers.

Result: On 6,019 examples from 150 held-out nuScenes scenes, FIRE-VLA preserves comparable single-sample planning performance, reduces G=4 mean L2 from 1.848 m to 1.500 m, and lowers evaluation-persistent failure prevalence from 13.03% to 11.20%. The mean error reduction is driven mainly by rare severe rollouts rather than uniform gains across typical trajectories.

Conclusion: FIRE-VLA offers a self-evolving, failure-aware learning loop for VLA models that leverages privileged supervision from a frozen, round-start copy to address persistent failures, achieving measurable improvements without enlarging the external teacher.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves autonomous-driving vision-language-action (VLA) models by evaluating trajectories sampled from the current policy. Group relative policy optimization (GRPO) learns from reward differences within each rollout group. When all sampled trajectories are poor, this relative signal can rank failures without identifying behavior outside the failed region. We introduce FIRE-VLA, a failure-informed self-evolution framework that converts such unresolved failures into privileged supervision for the next policy. Low-reward, low-diversity groups trigger self-distillation from a frozen round-start copy of the same model. Teacher and student have the same parameter scale, but only the teacher observes the hidden future trajectory. Supervision follows the student's generated prefix and is restricted to answer tokens, while GRPO remains active for every group. The updated policy supplies the teacher for the next round, allowing the routed failure distribution to change with the policy without requiring a larger external teacher. Starting from the same Qwen2.5-VL-3B SFT checkpoint, the comparison matches student rollout and policy-update counts. On 6,019 examples from 150 held-out nuScenes scenes, FIRE-VLA retains comparable single-sample planning, reduces G=4 mean L2 from 1.848 to 1.500 m, and lowers evaluation-persistent failure prevalence from 13.03% to 11.20%. The reduction in mean error arises mainly from rare severe rollouts rather than uniform improvement across ordinary trajectories.

</details>


### [203] [Capstan-driven Continuum Surgical Robot: Design, Modeling, and Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13396)
*Gang Zhang,Yufu Qiu,Junyan Yan,Wenhui Zeng,Wenlong Lu,Shing Shin Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An actuation-perception co-design for capstan-driven continuum robots enabling real-time cable tension sensing and integrated shape/force sensing without space penalty, via compliant motor-bracket, short-thick-beam multibody modeling, and proximal force/torque sensing; validated on single- and dual-segment robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cable tension information is hard to obtain inside compact capstan assemblies; current perception methods either require space or compromise performance.

Method: Introduce a compliant element in the motor mounting bracket to transduce cable reaction forces into measurable deformation for real-time tension sensing; develop a parallel computation framework using a multibody short-thick-beam model to capture shear in short beam segments and interactions among multiple cables for real-time inference; add a proximal multi-axis force/torque sensor as an anchor for stable shape/force sensing; fabricate and test single- and dual-segment capstan-driven continuum robots; perform experiments to assess real-time tip pose, contact force, and contact location.

Result: Real-time tip pose estimation together with contact force and location perception achieved in both single- and dual-segment configurations; cable tension feedback obtained without occupying capstan space, validating integrated perception framework.

Conclusion: Actuation-perception co-design with compliant mounting, advanced modeling, and proximal sensing enables integrated shape/force perception in capstan-driven continuum robots without compromising compact capstan architecture.

Abstract: Shape and force sensing have long been critical bottlenecks in the development of compact capstan-driven continuum surgical robots, primarily due to the difficulty of obtaining cable tension information within the confined capstan assembly. To overcome these challenges, this paper presents an integrated design-modeling-sensing approach based on the concept of actuation-perception co-design. A compliant element is introduced into the motor mounting bracket of the drive system, enabling micro-deformation under the cable reaction force and thereby allowing real-time cable tension measurement without occupying the compact capstan space. To address the modeling complexity arising from unconventional joint configurations introduced by the spatial cable routing strategy, a parallel computation framework based on a multibody short-thick-beam model is proposed, which captures shear effects in short beam segments and synergistic multi-cable interactions while achieving real-time performance. Building on this framework, stable shape and force sensing is achieved by incorporating a proximal multi-axis force/torque sensor as an additional measurement anchor. Following this design-modeling-sensing framework, capstan-driven continuum surgical robots with single- and dual-segment configurations are developed. Experimental results validate the proposed framework in both single- and dual-segment continuum robots, demonstrating real-time tip pose estimation together with contact force and location perception. By enabling cable tension feedback without compromising the compact capstan architecture, the proposed framework makes integrated perception feasible for capstan-driven continuum surgical robots.

</details>


### [204] [Deliberate Practice: Learning Robot Skills under a Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13415)
*Shivam Vats,Sudarshan Harithas,Mete Tuluhan Akbulut,Arvind Raghunathan,George Konidaris*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Deliberate Practice (DP) for budget-constrained autonomous skill learning uses a bilinear program to compute a provably budget-optimal allocation of practice time across skills, maximizing expected cumulative reward on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiently using a limited practice budget to acquire useful policies for complex, long-horizon manipulation tasks, with theoretical guarantees on optimality.

Method: DP estimates mastery time for each skill and the cumulative reward of task plans enabled by skill plans; formulates the allocation as a bilinear program solved with standard solvers to obtain exact budget-optimal decisions.

Result: Simulated and real-world long-horizon manipulation experiments show DP enables robots to use limited practice time more effectively, improving planning and policy quality compared to baselines.

Conclusion: The approach provides provable, budget-optimal allocation of practice time for skill acquisition, leveraging a tractable bilinear formulation to scale to long-horizon tasks.

Abstract: We consider the problem of autonomously learning robot skills under a limited practice budget for sequential tasks. We propose an active skill learning algorithm, \emph{Deliberate Practice (DP)}, that computes a provably \emph{budget-optimal} allocation---practicing skills that maximize expected cumulative reward while being learnable within the budget. DP estimates both the time needed to master skills and the cumulative reward of the task plans that the skills unlock. Computing a budget-optimal allocation is challenging as it requires reasoning about combinatorially many skill plans over a large practice budget. Our key contribution is a bilinear program that can compute this exactly using off-the-shelf solvers. Through simulated and real-world experiments on long-horizon manipulation tasks, we show that our approach allows robots to optimally use limited practice time to acquire useful policies and improve long-horizon planning.

</details>


### [205] [Attention from Action, for Action: Emergent Visual Bottlenecks for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13422)
*Zheyu Zhuang,Ruiyu Wang,Nick Heppert,Johannes Fabian Hahn,Abhinav Valada,Florian T. Pokorny,Danica Kragic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Seeker learns a task- and state-conditioned ROI attention from action supervision to produce progression-aware crops, improving data efficiency and robustness in visuomotor learning beyond label-dependent ROI methods and action-derived crops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Label-free ROI cropping often relies on end-effector-centered crops that can misalign when useful visual evidence lies away from the end-effector or evolves during task progress. A progression-aware, action-supervised ROI readout promises better alignment with control requirements without extra labeling.

Method: From frozen DINOv3 features, Seeker iteratively updates a query by gathering visual evidence from observed actions, producing ROIs conditioned on task state and progress. The ROI then serves for RGB cropping, mask-guided background augmentation, and point-cloud filtering, enabling multiple ROI-based data enhancements.

Result: In both simulation and real robot experiments, Seeker outperforms no-crop, standard augmentation, and action-derived crop baselines. On real robots, in-domain success rises from 48.3% (best baseline) to 76.7%, and robustness to lighting/background shifts improves from 20.0% to 60.0%.

Conclusion: A progression-aware, action-supervised ROI readout can greatly improve data efficiency and robustness in visuomotor learning, providing a strong, label-free alternative to fixed or end-effector-centered cropping strategies.

Abstract: Visual bottlenecks that focus policy inputs on regions of interest (ROIs) can improve data-efficient visuomotor learning by separating where to look from how to act. Many ROI interfaces rely on external spatial labels, such as gaze, object classes, or affordance annotations. Label-free alternatives often derive crops from trajectories by detecting gripper or motion events and centering a fixed crop at the projected end-effector. Such action-derived crops are useful spatial priors that require no additional labels, but they encode fixed choices about event timing, proxy points, and crop scale. When the visual evidence needed for control lies away from the end-effector or changes continuously with task progress, these crops can become misaligned. We propose Seeker, a task- and state-conditioned readout that learns attention from action. Starting from frozen DINOv3 features, Seeker iteratively updates a query with gathered visual evidence, producing progression-aware ROIs solely from action supervision. The learned ROI serves as a spatial interface for RGB cropping, mask-guided background augmentation, and point-cloud filtering. In simulation and the real world, Seeker improves data efficiency and robustness over no-crop, augmentation, and action-derived crop baselines. On real robots, Seeker raises average in-domain success from the best baseline's 48.3% to 76.7% and success under lighting/background shifts from 20.0% to 60.0%.

</details>


### [206] [ContactGuard: Pre-Contact Execution Monitoring with Action-Conditioned Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13438)
*Gehan Zheng,Matthew Johnson-Roberson,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ContactGuard is a pre-contact monitor for chunked visuomotor policies that predicts short-horizon outcomes in latent visual space to abort likely-contact failures before contact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate contact-induced failures in wrist-camera manipulation by detecting impending failures before contact, enabling safe aborts without altering the policy.

Method: Train a latent world model from unlabeled trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions (avoiding pixel-level video prediction). Learn a lightweight failure probe from a small labeled pre-contact clip set. At deployment, predict pre-contact outcomes, roll the model forward with the policy's actions, and abort if the predicted post-contact latent indicates likely failure, without modifying the underlying policy.

Result: ContactGuard yields more accurate failure predictions than direct action-based ablations across real-world tasks and transfers to a live robot as a pre-contact abort signal without changing the policy.

Conclusion: A data-efficient pre-contact monitoring approach using latent visual predictions and a small failure probe can effectively prevent contact-driven failures in manipulation while preserving the original policy.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation failures are often detected only after the robot has committed to contact. This is especially limiting in wrist-camera setups: close gripper--object views help observe contact, but a poor approach may already push, miss, slip, or disturb the object before conventional detectors react. We introduce \emph{ContactGuard}, a pre-contact execution monitor for chunked visuomotor policies. Given the policy's planned action chunk, ContactGuard predicts its short-horizon consequence in latent visual space and aborts if the predicted future latent indicates likely failure. Its latent world model is trained from unlabelled robot trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions, avoiding pixel-level video prediction. A lightweight failure probe is then trained from a small labelled set of pre-contact clips. At deployment, ContactGuard anchors prediction before an imminent contact event, rolls the model forward under the policy's own actions, and verifies the predicted post-contact latent. Across real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, ContactGuard predicts failure more accurately than direct and corrupted-action ablations, and transfers to live robot as a pre-contact abort signal without modifying the underlying policy.

</details>


### [207] [Mind the Context: Continual Learning of Socially Appropriate Robot Actions via Environmental-Social Disentanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13448)
*Rafal Robert Karpinski,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Nikhil Churamani,Yiming Luo,Maartje M. A. de Graaf,Davide Dell'Anna,Hatice Gunes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EDD (Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch) tackles domain-incremental continual learning for socially appropriate robot actions by disentangling environmental and social-agent cues with replay-based rehearsal; it outperforms baselines across several indoor domains; code is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots must continually learn to act appropriately across diverse environments where environmental and social cues jointly determine action appropriateness. In domain-incremental settings, previous domains are extended with new contexts, making forgetting a critical issue. Existing CL work on social actions lacks explicit disentanglement of environment vs social cues and domain-order analyses.

Method: Propose Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch (EDD) with two branches that separately model environmental context and social-agent related knowledge. Uses replay-based rehearsal to mitigate forgetting as it learns action appropriateness across multiple indoor domains (e.g., living room, meeting room, office, hallway). Conduct ablations to evaluate disentanglement strategies and sensitivity to domain ordering.

Result: EDD outperforms several state-of-the-art continual learning baselines. Ablation studies show the benefit of disentanglement and replay mechanisms; the authors also analyze how domain ordering affects learning.

Conclusion: EDD provides an effective framework for domain-incremental continual learning of socially appropriate robot actions, supported by empirical evidence and ablation analyses; code is publicly available.

Abstract: Social robots are expected to operate across diverse environments, where similar arrangements can imply different socially appropriate actions, e.g., starting a conversation may be acceptable in a crowded home but disruptive in an office meeting. Because such norms and environments cannot all be anticipated in advance, robots require continual learning (CL) to adapt from sequential experience while retaining previously acquired knowledge. Prior work has studied CL for generating socially appropriate robot actions, but it has not addressed domain-incremental settings in which the robot incrementally encounters diverse contexts (e.g., living room, meeting room, office, hallway), where both environmental (e.g., whether the space is open or cluttered with furniture) and social cues (e.g., how people or other agents are positioned around the robot) jointly shape the appropriateness of robot actions. We address this gap with the Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch (EDD) framework. EDD explicitly separates environmental and social-agent related knowledge and uses replay-based rehearsal to mitigate forgetting while learning the appropriateness of robot actions (e.g., cleaning, serving, starting a conversation) across several indoor domains. Experiments show that EDD outperforms several state-of-the-art baselines, and ablation studies further evaluate different disentanglement strategies and the sensitivity to domain ordering. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Cambridge-AFAR/Mind-the-Context.git.

</details>


### [208] [Decoding Task Progress from VLA Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13474)
*Atiksh Bhardwaj,Edward Weiyi Duan,Prithwish Dan,Wei-Chiu Ma,Preston Culbertson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLAs encode task progress in their internal residual stream; a simple linear probe reads this semantic quantity, present even before task-specific training and generalizes to new tasks. It can be modulated by language counterfactuals with multi-prompt data but cannot be used to steer the policy; functions as an effective OOD/task-progress detector for monitoring deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to understand what VLAs internally represent (mechanistic interpretability) and to enable runtime monitoring with lightweight probes, enabling safe deployment and diagnostic tooling.

Method: probe the residual stream of π_{0.5} in the model, train a linear readout to predict task progress (normalized remaining time); test generalization to unseen tasks; evaluate impact of language counterfactuals using multi-prompt data; assess utility as a label-free OOD detector for stalled progress.

Result: task progress can be linearly decoded from activations; the signal exists in the pretrained backbone before task-specific data; a single linear probe generalizes to unseen tasks; language counterfactuals modulate the readout when trained with multi-prompt data; however, the probe does not enable meaningful steering of the policy; the probe functions effectively as an unsupervised OOD detector for stalled progress, competitive with state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: VLAs harbor rich, linearly readable semantic quantities such as task progress; reading these signals provides a lightweight, interpretable path to monitor deployed visuomotor policies and to instrument them with runtime diagnostics.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) are moving rapidly towards deployment as general-purpose manipulation policies, but we currently lack basic tools for understanding what these models represent internally or for monitoring them at runtime. Leveraging ideas from mechanistic interpretability, we probe the residual stream of $π_{0.5}$ and find that task progress, the normalized time remaining in a trajectory, is linearly readable from the activations. We find that this signal is present in the pretrained PaliGemma backbone prior to training on any robot-specific data. A single linear probe generalizes to unseen tasks and varies under language counterfactuals when trained on multi-prompt data, but does not enable meaningful steering of the policy. These properties make the signal directly useful for instrumenting deployed VLAs. We use the probe as a simple label-free OOD detector, which detects stalled task progress, and find it competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our results suggest that VLAs have rich, linearly readable internal representations of semantic quantities like task progress, and that learning to read these signals offers a lightweight, interpretable path toward monitoring deployed visuomotor policies.

</details>


### [209] [A Browser-Native Digital Test Range for Benchmarking 4D Ocean-Glider Planning Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13511)
*Edward Holmberg,Elias Ioup,Mahdi Abdelguerfi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A browser-native, installation-free digital test range reuses a region as a four-dimensional OSSE-like experiment to test ocean-glider planners, with a common plan-to-observation contract, multiple planners, and browser-parity GliderFlight via Pyodide/WebAssembly; results enable dataset-ready pre-deployment experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scarce, non-resettable resources for repeated in-situ testing of ocean-glider planners necessitate a reproducible, scalable, and accessible evaluation platform that unifies planning, sensing, and observing components.

Method: Develop a browser-based digital test range that guides users from domain and mission setup through bathymetry, forcing, tasks, route specification, current-affected execution, observation generation, and scoring. Enforce a common contract linking vehicle, sensing, and evaluator assumptions; export artifacts for datasets. Use a controlled Observing System Simulation Experiment (OSSE) to evaluate five classical planners across two episodes, seeds, and a 60-hour horizon. Validate via a public deployment and achieve browser parity with Pyodide/WebAssembly for GliderFlight 1.2.0.

Result: All 54 missions completed and recovered without hard violations. Planner rankings and dive-policy effects exposed operational-scientific tradeoffs. Public deployment provided real-world audit. GliderFlight achieved native-browser parity, enabling high-fidelity browser-based multi-tier simulation. Artifacts are dataset-ready and traceable to mission-scale experiments.

Conclusion: The approach yields a scientifically traceable and component-qualified operational space suitable for large-scale pre-deployment experimentation and robust planner evaluation.

Abstract: Repeated in-situ evaluation of ocean-glider planners requires scarce vehicles, operators, deployment and recovery resources, and ocean conditions that cannot be reset for competing algorithms. We present a guided, installation-free browser-native digital test range that transforms a selected region into a reproducible four-dimensional experiment. The system leads users from regional domain selection through mission-scoped bathymetry, time/depth forcing, science objectives, optional task decomposition, route specification, current-advected execution, observation generation, and scoring. Its primary contribution is a common plan-to-observation contract unifying vehicle, sensing, and evaluator assumptions across manual routes, transparent built-in algorithms, and imported classical or learned-planner outputs, while exported artifacts form dataset-ready records. A controlled Observing System Simulation Experiment (OSSE) evaluates five classical planners in two episodes, three deterministic seeds, and a calibrated 60-hour horizon. All 54 missions completed and recovered without hard violations, while planner rankings and dive-policy effects revealed operational-scientific tradeoffs. An authentic public deployment supplied a field-referenced audit to scope current kinematic boundaries. Separately, source-locked GliderFlight 1.2.0 achieved native-to-browser parity through Pyodide/WebAssembly, establishing a pathway for high-fidelity multi-tier simulation. The resulting operational space is scientifically traceable and component-qualified for mission-scale pre-deployment experimentation.

</details>


### [210] [HumanTracker: Towards Comprehensive and Human-Aligned Motion Tracking Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13555)
*Dairu Liu,Zekun Qi,Jiayu Zeng,Ruixi Yu,Yu Guan,Yintianrun Zhang,Xuchuan Chen,Sikai Liang,Zekai Li,Chenghuai Lin,Xinqiang Yu,Wenyao Zhang,He Wang,Li Yi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces HumanTracker and HumanScore for perceptually-aligned, scalable humanoid motion tracking evaluation, addressing contact stability issues missed by kinematic metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between per-frame kinematic errors and human perception, and stress-test contact-rich, long-horizon humanoid behaviors.

Method: Assembles ~153 hours of professional-motion data across four labeled motion families; provides fine-grained diagnoses via text labels; trains a preference-aligned metric (HumanScore) on 12k motion pairs (24k motions); benchmarks state-of-the-art trackers.

Result: HumanScore better predicts human preferences and uncovers contact/stability failures that kinematic metrics overlook.

Conclusion: Perceptually aligned, scalable evaluation framework for humanoid tracking that enables better diagnosis of contact, stability, and long-horizon behaviors.

Abstract: Humanoid motion tracking is central to teleoperation and whole-body imitation, yet evaluation often disagrees with what people perceive in videos. Kinematic errors average per-frame pose differences but miss the physical artifacts that matter most, particularly unstable support and incorrect contacts such as foot skating and mistimed touch-downs. Meanwhile, widely used test suites are small and lack the diversity needed to stress contact-rich, long-horizon behaviors. We introduce HumanTracker to make humanoid tracking evaluation both perceptually aligned and scalable. The HumanTracker benchmark contains approximately 153 hours of optical motion trajectories from multiple professional performers, organized into four motion families with text labels for fine-grained diagnosis. We further propose HumanScore, a preference-aligned metric trained on 12K motion pairs containing 24K motions. Across representative state-of-the-art trackers, HumanScore better predicts human preferences and reveals contact and stability failures that kinematic metrics often miss.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [211] [Position: Reasoning is a Learnable Rule-Based Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12325)
*Rachel Lawrence,Jacqueline Maasch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes operational definitions for reasoning in AI, reconciling symbolic/logical traditions with probabilistic generative models, and offers a best-practices checklist for reporting AI reasoning research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address definitional ambiguity in reasoning evaluation to improve construct validity and trustworthy autonomous reasoning; argue for a learnable rule-based perspective.

Method: Synthesize literature to define valid and sound reasoning as a learnable rule-based process; develop a best-practices checklist for communication of AI reasoning research.

Result: Theoretical framework establishing operational definitions and a practical checklist; no empirical results reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: Ambiguity around reasoning definitions can be resolved by adopting the proposed framework and checklist, enabling verifiable progress and credible evaluation.

Abstract: Autonomous reasoning is among the most scientifically and economically motivating topics in AI today. Historically the purview of symbolic AI, recent advances have mainly emerged from deep probabilistic generative models. Despite immense interest and rapid progress, the generative AI community has not clearly converged on operational definitions for reasoning and often implicitly rejects the historical treatment of this topic in logic and verifiable automated reasoning. This position contends that definitional ambiguity leaves the construct validity of reasoning evaluation unverifiable, undermining quantifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning. We also contend that this ambiguity is addressable. To that end, we provide (1) operational definitions based on a synthesis of the literature, positioning valid and sound reasoning as a learnable rule-based process; and (2) a checklist for best practices in the communication of AI reasoning research.

</details>


### [212] [Diagnostic Foundation for Evaluating LLMs' Research Integrity as Co-Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12345)
*Yash Tripathi,Silu Sharma,Sai Sidhanth Manoharan Jayanthi,Shivank Garg,Lin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IntegrityBench reveals substantial integrity failures in frontier models under pressure; misconduct classification, ethical action, and artifact-grounded decision making are dissociated; explicit pressure provokes misconduct, while implicit framing causes over-refusal; accuracy in classification does not predict artifact-grounded performance; deployment risks include facilitating misconduct and eroding trust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify AI alignment with research integrity under institutional pressure across multiple domains and stages, using a multi-faceted benchmark that separates classification, ethical reasoning, and artifact-grounded decision making.

Method: 36 paired tasks across a 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol, spanning 3 domains and 4 research stages; evaluation of 18 frontier model variants on three facets: misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning, and artifact-grounded decision making.

Result: Under peak pressure, models fail roughly 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions; neither scaling nor enhanced reasoning reliably mitigates failures. Explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct; implicit contextual reframing leads to over-refusal of legitimate tasks. Models that fail to classify requests accurately perform as well or better on artifact-grounded decision making (85.7% vs. 79.4%). The three facets appear structurally dissociated, and correct ethical action does not require accurate classification. Frontier models can seem helpful while harboring integrity failures, creating risks of facilitating misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.

Conclusion: Integrity benchmarks reveal two deployment risks—facilitating research misconduct and eroding trust. Effective deployment requires recognizing dissociation among integrity facets and implementing safeguards, as improvements in one facet do not guarantee overall integrity.

Abstract: Language models are increasingly deployed as co-scientists, yet their ability to uphold research integrity under institutional pressure remains unmeasured. We introduce IntegrityBench, a benchmark evaluating misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning and artifact-grounded decision making across 36 paired tasks under a 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol spanning 3 domains and 4 research stages. Evaluating 18 frontier model variants, we find that under peak pressure, models fail roughly 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions, and neither scale nor reasoning ability reliably mitigates this. Explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct, while implicit contextual reframing more often causes over-refusal of legitimate research tasks. Interestingly, models failing to classify research requests accurately perform equally or better on artifact-grounded decision making (85.7 vs. 79.4), suggesting the three facets are structurally dissociated and correct ethical action does not require accurate classification. Frontier models can thus appear helpful while harbouring integrity failures that create two distinct deployment risks: facilitating research misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.

</details>


### [213] [Position: The Alignment Community is Unintentionally Building a Censor's Toolkit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12346)
*Sarah Ball,Phil Hackemann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dual-use of AI alignment methods may enable censorship and informational manipulation; the paper maps alignment techniques to misuse cases, highlights risk due to rapid AI adoption and political shifts, and calls for mitigation strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing deployment of AI as information providers amid economic power asymmetries and authoritarian trends increases the potential for deliberate misuse of alignment tools; need to discuss and address these risks now.

Method: Conceptual analysis mapping current alignment techniques to misuse scenarios; review of potential actual cases; discussion of mitigation strategies; no empirical experiments.

Result: Identifies dual-use pathways and demonstrates how 'perfect' alignment could empower malicious actors; proposes mitigation measures to safeguard against misuse and preserve legitimate benefits.

Conclusion: Urgent call for the AI community to acknowledge and address intentional misuse of alignment mechanisms; adopt governance, transparency, and design safeguards to mitigate dual-use risks.

Abstract: This position paper argues that modern AI alignment methods - originally designed to prevent harmful output - are dual-use technologies that may easily be misused by malicious actors for censorship and manipulation. By mapping current alignment techniques to the possibility and actual cases of misuse, we show that the quest for a "perfectly aligned" model inadvertently also provides malicious actors with an ever-improving tool for informational dominance. We need to discuss this dual-use potential now, as its risk is exacerbated by rapid user adoption of AI as information provider, economic power asymmetries, and a political landscape that increasingly shifts towards authoritarianism. We conclude by urging the community to consider the intentional misuse of AI alignment mechanisms and propose mitigation strategies to safeguard against this dual-use potential.

</details>


### [214] [Agreement Is Not Alignment: Divergent Moral Grounds in Human and LLM Ethical Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12368)
*Octavian M. Machidon,Alina L. Machidon,Vojko Strahovnik,Mateja Centa Strahovnik,Jonas Miklavčič,Marko Robnik Šikonja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: High label agreement masks systematic divergence in moral reasoning between humans and LLMs; alignment requires analyzing rationales, not just final labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the assumption that agreement on final judgments implies shared moral principles; to investigate whether LLMs and humans rely on the same moral grounds across domains.

Method: Use a curated 500-item ETHICS-derived benchmark spanning five moral domains; obtain new human annotator and LLM annotations for both final labels and rationales; compare label-level and rationale-level agreement across frontier and open models; analyze how models redistribute attention among moral categories (e.g., harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, excuses).

Result: Label-level agreement with human majority is often high, but rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in moral grounds; models shift attention across moral categories even when final labels match human judgments.

Conclusion: Agreement on final labels is not equivalent to true alignment; label-based evaluation can be misleading without evaluating underlying rationales, principles, and moral priorities; recommend rationale-aware evaluation for robust alignment assessment.

Abstract: Agreement with human judgments is a common proxy for evaluating the alignment of large language models (LLMs). Yet agreement in final labels does not show that human annotators and models rely on the same moral grounds. Two agents may reach the same judgment while appealing to different principles, contextual assumptions, or interpretations of the situation.
  We test this distinction using a curated 500-item ETHICS-derived benchmark spanning five domains of moral judgment, with new human annotator and LLM annotations of both final labels and supporting rationales. Across frontier and open model families, agreement with human annotator majority labels is often high. However, rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in the moral grounds expressed by human annotators and models. In particular, models redistribute attention across categories such as harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, and excuse relevance, even when their final labels match the human annotator majority.
  Our results show that agreement should not be treated as equivalent to alignment. Label-based evaluation can therefore be misleadingly reassuring unless complemented by analysis of the reasons, principles, and moral priorities expressed in model judgments.

</details>


### [215] [Multi-Agent Scheduling with LLM-Assisted Contract Net Negotiation for Stream Processing in Mobile Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12371)
*Sabeur Lajili,Zaki Brahmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAS-DecStream extends Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, and LLM-assisted refinement for edge-cloud stream scheduling; demonstrates latency violation reductions, no overcommitment, high conflict resolution, and utility gains on Alibaba ASI traces; reveals trade-offs between model/prompt choices in multi-round negotiations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address decentralized, QoS-aware scheduling in heterogeneous mobile edge–cloud environments under workload volatility, resource contention, and QoS constraints.

Method: Proposes MAS-DecStream and LL-MR-CNP extension: semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents generate refined, natural-language offloading proposals from local observations, predicted resource states, and qualitative runtime context, while hard resource/QoS constraints remain deterministic. Evaluations use Alibaba ASI Trace to compare single vs multi-round CNP, rule-based vs LLM-assisted refinement, and fixed-model vs multi-round negotiation.

Result: Latency violations reduced to 3%; no resource overcommitment; conflict-resolution rate 0.91 with 20 agents; utility improved by up to 22% over multi-round rule-based baseline; 25-case study shows model- and prompt-dependent accuracy–cost trade-offs.

Conclusion: Multi-round CNP refinement is the main protocol-level gain; LLM assistance adds value for qualitative and uncertain runtime context.

Abstract: Stream-processing systems increasingly operate across heterogeneous mobile edge--cloud infrastructures, where workload volatility, resource contention, and stringent quality-of-service (QoS) requirements complicate decentralized scheduling. This paper proposes \emph{MAS-DecStream}, whose main contribution is \emph{LLM-MR-CNP}: an extension of the classical Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents refine natural-language offloading proposals from local observations, predicted resource states, and qualitative runtime context, while hard resource and QoS constraints remain deterministic. Experiments derived from the Alibaba ASI Trace evaluate the extension at three levels: single- versus multi-round CNP, rule-based versus LLM-assisted refinement, and fixed-model single- versus multi-round negotiation. Under the evaluated configurations, MAS-DecStream reduces latency violations to 3\%, eliminates resource overcommitment, reaches a conflict-resolution rate of 0.91 with 20 agents, and improves utility by up to 22\% over the multi-round rule-based baseline. A separate 25-case evaluation shows model- and prompt-dependent accuracy--cost trade-offs. The results provide initial evidence that multi-round CNP refinement is the principal protocol-level gain, with LLM assistance adding value for qualitative and uncertain runtime context.

</details>


### [216] [Position: We Need Practical AI Alignment Methods to Mirror Human Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12372)
*Vijay Keswani,Breanna K. Nguyen,Cyrus Cousins,Vincent Conitzer,Walter Sinnott-Armstrong,Jana Schaich Borg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A position paper advocates cognitively-aligned AI that reasons like users and communicates reasoning to improve adoption in high-stakes settings; outlines evidence, reports user survey data, identifies gaps, and proposes a research agenda to achieve alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-stakes decision-making requires AI that users can understand and trust. Cognitive misalignment threatens adoption and justified reliance on AI systems.

Method: Literature review, integration of existing evidence, and presentation of new survey data; proposes a research agenda rather than presenting new experimental results.

Result: Cognitive alignment improves understandability and trustworthiness; many users consider cognitive alignment essential when rationale is important; current alignment methods do not fully meet this need; gaps identified and a roadmap proposed.

Conclusion: Addressing cognitive misalignment is essential for scalable and justified deployment of AI in real-world, high-stakes tasks.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly employed as decision aids, decision delegates, or autonomous decision-makers. This position paper argues that in many settings, particularly high-stakes decision-making, we need accurate cognitively-aligned AI systems that reason similarly to their users, and faithfully communicate their reasoning. We review evidence that cognitive alignment improves understandability and trustworthiness, and provide new survey data showing that many users find cognitive alignment "essential" when an AI's rationale for a judgment or action is important to them. We outline the gaps between existing alignment methods and what is needed to achieve cognitive alignment, and present a research agenda to address these gaps. We argue that cognitive misalignment represents a likely impediment to AI adoption in many envisioned applications, and that addressing it is important for creating AI systems on which users are both willing and justified to rely.

</details>


### [217] [Don't Want Your LLM to Recommend Nuclear Strike? Try Asking It in Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12373)
*Rian Touchent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multilingual safety behavior in LLMs is language-dependent; Japanese prompts can significantly reduce harmful recommendations in some models (Claude), while others show no effect; reasoning language, not input language, drives the effect; English-only evaluation can miss both risks and safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether the language used in prompts affects high-stakes safety decisions in LLMs and whether English-centric evaluation underestimates or overestimates risks.

Method: Single-turn game-theoretic vignettes where a model advises a nuclear-armed nation on striking a defenseless opponent. Prompts are amoral and strategically identical across languages. Tested nine models from six providers. Measured launch rates (likelihood to advise a strike). Included cross-language manipulation: instructing the model to reason in Japanese within an English prompt. Analyzed whether moral vocabulary appears when reasoning in Japanese. 

Result: In Claude Sonnet 4.6, launch rates drop from 40% to 0% in unnecessary-strike scenarios and from 93% to 17% in contested scenarios when prompts are in Japanese; Gemini Pro 3.1 falls from 53% to 13%. When language of reasoning is Japanese (even with English input), launch rates drop from 93% to 37%. Five other models show no language effect and launch in nearly all conditions. The effect requires models already hesitant in English. Five models’ behavior is language-invariant; the “reasoning language” mechanism, not input language, drives the effect. Japanese reasoning also triggers spontaneous moral vocabulary (e.g., “moral cost,” “millions of lives”). 

Conclusion: Safety behavior in LLMs is language-dependent; evaluating safety solely in English can miss languages-induced safeguards or risks. Cross-language prompts can meaningfully alter hazardous recommendations, suggesting the need for multilingual safety evaluation and careful design of prompts to elicit or constrain moral reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used in strategic and advisory contexts, yet their safety alignment is typically evaluated in English only. We test nine models from six providers and ask whether the language of a prompt can change a model's decision in a high-stakes scenario. We use single-turn game-theoretic vignettes in which a model advises a nuclear-armed nation on whether to strike a defenseless opponent. The prompt is intentionally amoral and strategically identical across languages. We find that Japanese prompts reduce launch rates in the Claude model family: Claude Sonnet 4.6 drops from 40% to 0% in scenarios where the strike is unnecessary and from 93% to 17% in contested scenarios, with minimal effect when the strike is strategically rational. The effect extends to Gemini Pro 3.1 (53% to 13%). A cross-language experiment isolates the mechanism: when instructed to reason in Japanese in an English prompt, launch rates drop from 93% to 37%. It is the language the model is asked to reason in, not the language of the input, that drives the effect. When reasoning in Japanese, models spontaneously generate moral vocabulary (''moral cost'', ''millions of lives'') that is entirely absent from the prompt. Five other models show no language effect, but they launch in nearly every condition regardless of language. The effect requires a model that already hesitates in English. These results show that LLM safety behavior is language-dependent, and that evaluating in English alone can miss both risks and safeguards encoded in other languages.

</details>


### [218] [Dual-Flow Transformers: Decoupling the Primary Prefill Path from Additional Decode Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12385)
*Liming Liu,Mingze Wang,Tuo Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dual-Flow Transformer separates prompt processing and continuation-prediction into a primary, write-enabled flow and an auxiliary, cache-free flow activated after the prompt. Sharing core weights but using distinct embeddings, it aims to optimize cumulative inference efficiency and allow phase-specific expert control in MoE models, achieving lower validation loss on matched tokens and exposing a prefill–decode quality trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference cost grows with usage and hardware handles prompt prefill and autoregressive decoding differently. Traditional scaling increases both costs, so decoupling compute between prompt processing and continuation prediction could reduce total cost while preserving prompt-wide computation and persistent KV caches.

Method: A primary flow performs the full causal LM over the prompt and writes a KV cache. An auxiliary flow, activated only from the final prompt position onward, performs continuation prediction without writing or altering persistent state. The two flows share attention, MLP, and output matrices, but have separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling. They also reuse weights and the primary cache to enable grouped execution. In MoE models, primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs are independently controlled. The study examines two regimes: increasing decode compute at fixed prefill compute, and reallocation of a fixed decode budget between flows.

Result: Across matched-token comparisons, Dual-Flow yields lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations. In MoE models, the separation allows independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. The experiments reveal a prefill–decode quality trade-off and demonstrate potential gains from phase-specific expert allocation.

Conclusion: Decoupling computation into prompt-focused and continuation-focused flows with shared core parameters and a persistent KV cache can improve inference efficiency and allow flexible cost–quality trade-offs. The approach is especially beneficial in MoE settings, where expert budgets can be allocated by phase. Further empirical validation and exploration of trade-offs are warranted.

Abstract: As large language models serve more requests, cumulative inference cost is becoming increasingly important relative to one-time training cost. The two inference phases stress hardware differently: prompt prefill is parallel and typically compute-bound, whereas autoregressive decode is sequential and often memory-bandwidth-bound. Conventional width or depth scaling increases both costs together because every added layer is evaluated in both phases. We ask whether additional learned computation can instead be allocated to continuation prediction while preserving the prompt-wide primary computation and a single persistent key-value (KV) cache. We introduce the Dual-Flow Transformer. Its primary flow is a complete causal language model that processes the prompt and writes the KV cache. The auxiliary flow is omitted during prompt processing and activated only from the final prompt position onward, adding continuation-prediction computation without writing persistent state or influencing the primary flow. The two flows share major attention, MLP, and output matrices, while using separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling. Sharing weights and the primary cache also creates opportunities to reuse loaded weights and cached keys and values during grouped execution. Across matched-token comparisons, Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations. In MoE models, the separation makes primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. We study two regimes: increasing decode computation at fixed prefill expert computation, and reallocating a fixed decode expert budget between the two flows. These experiments expose a prefill-decode-quality trade-off and demonstrate the potential of phase-specific expert allocation.

</details>


### [219] [Learning to Adapt Cross-Domain Preferences via Meta-LoRA for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12389)
*Xuefei Wang,Jun Han,Zixuan Wang,Qingkai Zeng,Xiao Wang,Ruijie Wang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA for cross-domain few-shot personalization that calibrates adaptation strength by evidence quality; meta-learns a LoRA initialization as both start and prior center, decomposing priors into user-focused prompts and domain-specific soft tokens to prevent negative transfer; demonstrates strong gains across benchmarks, including HiCUPID with substantial win-rate improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-domain zero- or few-shot personalization risks overfitting with sparse evidence and negative transfer from source-domain artifacts. There is a need to calibrate adaptation magnitude to evidence quality and to disentangle user preferences from domain priors for robust transfer.

Method: PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA: use a meta-learned LoRA initialization as the adaptation start and prior center; adapt update strength according to support-set size and predictive uncertainty; decompose personalization priors into user prompts (stable preferences) and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific conditioning in the hidden space.

Result: Empirical results show consistent gains over strong baselines across multiple benchmarks. On HiCUPID, cross-domain win-rate degradation is reduced by 47.9% relative to the best competing baseline, and win rate under unseen-user cold start improves by 110.2%.

Conclusion: The approach enables calibrated, robust cross-domain personalization by controlling adaptation magnitude, preventing overfitting and negative transfer, and through a functional decomposition of priors into interpretable user and domain components with stable prompts and domain conditioning.

Abstract: Cross-domain zero- or few-shot personalization aims to generate user-preferred responses in unseen conversational domains from only a handful of target-domain interactions. Existing adaptation methods struggle to calibrate update magnitude under sparse evidence and thus overfit, whereas history-transfer methods often entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts, yielding unreliable personalization priors and negative transfer. To calibrate adaptation to evidence quality, we propose PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA, which uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both the adaptation start and prior center, while adjusting update strength according to support-set size and predictive uncertainty. This limits overfitting under sparse or ambiguous evidence while permitting stronger personalization as evidence grows. Controlled adaptation alone does not determine which preferences should transfer across domains or how they should be expressed. We therefore functionally decompose personalization priors into user and domain components, using a human-readable prompt for stable preferences and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific hidden-space conditioning. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and personalization tasks show consistent gains over strong baselines. On HiCUPID, our method reduces cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% relative to the best competing baseline and improves win rate by 110.2% under unseen-user cold start.

</details>


### [220] [Research Assistant: AstraZeneca's Agentic System for R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12395)
*Piotr Grabowski,Mohamed Alameen,Jorge Bretones,Sabina Cardell,Miguel Carmona,Gavin Edwards,Ben Grainger,Sameh Hassan,Erik Jansson,Artur Kuziakhmetov,Albert Maristany,Hebatallah Mohamed,Andriy Nikolov,Sebastian Nilsson,Mark O'Donoghue,James Pacileo,Ashiq Sultan,Alex Voegele,Michael Ughetto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An internal AstraZeneca system, Research Assistant, uses an LLM-backed chat interface to unify diverse biomedical data sources (literature, graphs, chemistry, trials, safety, expression data, internal systems) for fast questions and complex multi-step tasks, grounding responses with provenance and enabling review of sources; outlines architecture, design choices, and deployment lessons at scale for R&D support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable researchers and clinicians to explore heterogeneous biomedical data efficiently, with evidence-backed answers and transparent provenance, to accelerate day-to-day R&D workflows.

Method: Integrated, retrieval-augmented LLM system with a chat interface. Aggregates data from scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry data, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal experimental systems. Supports a fast QA mode and a multi-step research workflow mode. Grounded responses linked to original sources for review.

Result: Demonstrates scalable deployment at AstraZeneca and provides practical design choices and lessons learned for enterprise AI in biomedicine, including evidence grounding and source traceability.

Conclusion: An effective enterprise AI tool for biomedicine that unifies diverse data sources with provenance-aware, multi-mode interaction, offering deployment insights for large organizations.

Abstract: We describe Research Assistant, an internal LLM-based system developed at AstraZeneca to help scientists and clinicians explore biomedical questions across a broad range of data sources. The system provides a chat-style interface that brings together evidence from scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal experimental systems. It supports both a fast mode for direct question answering and a multi-step mode for more complex research tasks. Responses are grounded in retrieved evidence and linked back to the original sources, allowing users to review and further explore the underlying data. In this technical note, we outline the system architecture, the main design choices behind the product, and lessons learned from deploying it at scale to support day-to-day R&D workflows across AstraZeneca.

</details>


### [221] [Large Language Models Can Follow Instructions, But Not Many at Once: Phase Transitions in Compositional Constraint Satisfaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12426)
*Mariya I. Vasileva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introducing Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a zero-LLM-judge benchmark to study how LLM performance degrades as the number of simultaneous constraints increases; findings reveal multiplicative degradation and nonuniform constraint handling across 15 models, 36 constraint types, over k=1-12.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize compositional generalization under constraints, quantify degradation, and identify factors governing performance collapse to guide evaluation and mitigation.

Method: Procedurally generate k simultaneous constraints; each constraint is scored by a deterministic, rule-based verifier; no LLM judges. Evaluate 15 models across 36 constraint types, totaling 369,753 checks for k in 1..12.

Result: Three key results: (1) per-constraint pass rate declines gradually; probability of satisfying all k constraints collapses (e.g., 41% per-constraint pass at k=8; only 5.7% succeed on all eight). (2) Degradation nonuniform: structural constraints lose ~2x more baseline capability per added constraint than lexical constraints, suggesting a comprehension-maintenance gap. (3) Failures are near-independently distributed, making accumulation multiplicative; residual coupling links to shared output features like counting.
