Conclusion: Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond about 5–6 simultaneous constraints; composition matters for safety and schema adherence; results motivate targeted evaluation and design of constraints and maintenance mechanisms to mitigate collapse.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in settings that require simultaneous adherence to multiple explicit constraints - reasoning structure, safety boundaries, output schemas. Individual constraints are handled proficiently, but the compositional regime, where many must hold jointly, remains poorly characterized: how rapidly does performance degrade, what governs the degradation, and can the collapse be mitigated? We introduce Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark that systematically varies the number of simultaneous constraints (k), with every constraint scored by a deterministic, rule-based verifier and zero LLM-judge involvement: 15 models, 36 constraint types, 369,753 checks at k=1-12. Three findings emerge. First, per-constraint pass rate decays gradually and predictably, while the chance of satisfying all k constraints collapses - a model passing individual constraints at ~41% at k=8 succeeds on all eight just 5.7% of the time. Second, constraints do not degrade equally: structural constraints lose 2x more baseline capability per added constraint than lexical ones, ordered by a comprehension-maintenance gap that separates constraints requiring sustained tracking from binary decisions immune to composition. Third, failures are nearly independent, which is what makes the accumulation multiplicative; the residual coupling that does exist tracks shared output features rather than pairwise interference - a wrong sentence count fails every constraint that reads it. Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 simultaneous constraints: probe-level success falls below 50% at 7 constraints for the strongest model, and at 3 or fewer for 12 of 15.

</details>


### [222] [MindMemOS: A Portable and Self-Evolving Memory Operating Layer for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12428)
*Kaichao Liang,Yuqi Cui,Hao Kong,Xinyuan Huang,Guohaotian Hou,Qingcan Kang,Liang Chen,Yiyang Yin,Ke Ye,Jiaquan Guo,Da Chen,Lingan Zeng,Yixing Peng,Rong Yao,Shixiong Kai,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A portable, self-evolving memory layer (MindMemOS) for AI agents that unifies memory via an entity-property-timestructure, enabling scenario-adaptive memory modeling, autonomous memory refinement, and skill evolution through MindMemEvolve and MindSkillEvolve; demonstrates strong benchmark performance and improvement on downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome fixed, development-time memory systems by enabling continuous evolution of memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge during operation, thereby improving long-term autonomy and adaptability.

Method: Introduce MindMemOS with a unified entity-property-timestructure. MindMemEvolve uses validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios; dreaming consolidates memories by merging duplicates and resolving conflicts; implicit corrective feedback acts as human-in-the-loop to revise memories; MindSkillEvolve converts agent trajectories into reusable, progressively refined skills. Evaluation on LOCOMO (94.03%), PersonaMem (70.63%), and SpreadsheetBench (9.2-point improvement).

Result: MindMemOS achieves high accuracy on two memory benchmarks and translates execution traces into reusable skills that improve downstream task performance.

Conclusion: A flexible, self-evolving memory operating layer enables scenario-aware memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, and continuous skill evolution, yielding strong benchmark results and potential for durable long-term AI autonomy.

Abstract: Memory is a core component of AI agents, enabling them to accumulate experience, maintain personalization, and adapt over long-term interactions. However, existing memory systems often remain fixed after development, limiting their ability to adapt their memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge through continued use. We present MindMemOS, a portable and self-evolving memory operating layer that organizes open-world information using a unified entity property timestructure. MindMemOS supports scenario-adaptive memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, autonomous memory refinement, and continuous skill evolution. Its MindMemEvolve algorithm employs validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios, whiledreaming consolidates accumulated memories by merging redundant records and resolving conflicts. In addition, implicit corrective feedback serves as a human-in-the-loop signal for identifying and revising potentially inaccurate or misaligned memories. Its MindSkillEvolve algorithm further transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable and progressively refined skills. MindMemOS achieves 94.03% accuracy on LOCOMO and 70.63% on PersonaMem. MindSkillEvolve improves SpreadsheetBench success by 9.2 percentage points over the initial-skill baseline.

</details>


### [223] [Governed Persistent Memory: Source-Bound State Semantics and Fail-Closed Release for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12476)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal memory with source-bound admission and fail-closed release; provides executable clauses and a formal ReleaseBench, plus end-to-end tests showing governance improves release correctness over ungov baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term agent memory often becomes contradictory, superseded, or deleted; there is a need for auditable, verifiable release semantics to support outgoing claims and avoid stale or conflicting information.

Method: Proposes a five-clause GPM design: ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction/deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh view. Evaluated on a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case ReleaseBench; includes three complete policies. Conducts end-to-end service tests across eight query families, with V3 and V5 arms, generation-date pinning, and no post-freeze reducer amendment. Includes a production-code-independent finite model and a 100,000-trace differential.

Result: GPM achieves full compliance on the ReleaseBench (one policy matches 1,800/3,600 with 50% of violations unmatched). In a sealed end-to-end test, governed deployment yields 2,400/2,400 correct clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungov Qwen2.5-7B, repairing all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (high one-sided lower bounds). V5 reseal maintains 2,400/2,400 per arm. A finite-state exploration covers 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states with no full counterexample, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential yields zero mismatches.

Conclusion: Results are bounded and implementation-specific rather than claims about open-world model accuracy. Governed answers in the sealed service evaluation are deterministic outputs; the 7B result is the ungov comparison, not a claim that the language model itself became perfectly accurate.

Abstract: Long-term agent memory is usually treated as select--store--retrieve, but retrieval does not decide whether contradictory, superseded, retracted, deleted, or stale records may support an outgoing claim. We introduce Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses cover ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction or deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh view at one verified head.
  On a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench, GPM matches all complete outcomes; the strongest of three intentionally simple complete policies matches 1,800/3,600 and makes unmatched releases on 50% of violation cases. A separate sealed end-to-end service evaluation exercises real ingestion and release across eight query families. In its publicly disclosed V3 arm, the governed lane is correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned local Qwen2.5-7B; it repairs all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (one-sided 95% lower bounds 99.875% and 99.834%). A later V5 reseal over Chinese- and English-command arms, with generation-date pinning and no post-freeze reducer amendment, again obtains 2,400/2,400 per arm. A production-code-independent finite model explores 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without a full-contract counterexample, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential yields zero mismatches.
  These are bounded contract and implementation results, not open-world model accuracy or evidence of world truth. Governed answers in the sealed service evaluation are deterministic service outputs; the 7B result is the ungoverned comparison, not a claim that a language model itself became perfectly accurate.

</details>


### [224] [$\varepsilon$-MemEvo: Adaptive Cross-Task Memory Transfer for LLM Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12522)
*Aofan Liu,Shiyuan Song,Yiyan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cross-task memory transfer for LLM program evolution via adaptive memory injection improves performance across tasks with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address isolation and forgetting of search experience in task-specific LLM program evolution; enable cross-task transfer of strategic knowledge through memory-encoded tactics.

Method: Introduce epsilon-MemEvo: stores task-agnostic tactic memories as natural-language summaries; uses adaptive injection gate to decide when and how strongly to inject retrieved memories; evaluates on 8 benchmarks with Leave-One-Out protocol; uses GPT-5 backbone; compares to AdaEvolve.

Result: AUCC improvements: mean relative gain +8.7% across 8 tasks; early-stage convergence +9.4%; ablations show naive memory injection can be harmful; adaptive gating is safe; data-updated posterior interpretable; overhead <1%.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effective cross-task transfer in LLM program evolution with interpretable, low-overhead memory augmentation; adaptive gating mitigates negative transfer; potential to generalize across tasks with differing APIs and evaluators.

Abstract: LLM-based program evolution systems such as FunSearch and AlphaEvolve have shown strong ability to discover novel algorithms, but typically optimize each task in isolation, discarding search experience after completion. We introduce $\varepsilon$-MemEvo, a framework for cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM program evolution. $\varepsilon$-MemEvo stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories: compact natural-language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies rather than raw code, enabling transfer across tasks with different APIs and evaluators. To avoid negative transfer from semantically mismatched memories, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo uses an adaptive injection gate that decides whether retrieved memories should be injected, and at what intensity. We evaluate $\varepsilon$-MemEvo on 8 diverse optimization benchmarks spanning mathematical optimization and systems engineering, using a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol that excludes target-task memory entries. On the primary GPT-5 backbone, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks, with a mean relative gain of +8.7%, and improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average. Ablations show that naive memory injection can fail catastrophically, while adaptive gating remains safe across all five ablation tasks. The data-updated posterior is interpretable in observed states: it favors skip during improving search and shifts from skip to hint across early and late plateaus. These gains incur less than 1% computational overhead.

</details>


### [225] [CAS: A Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555)
*Michael Georgiades,Charalambia Varnava*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Causal Attribution Score (CAS) to convert predictive explanations into causal effect explanations using interventional coalition games and causal Shapley contributions; provides local and global CAS summaries; demonstrates improved accuracy and different rankings from predictive SHAP, highlighting separation of outcome prediction from causal effect heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive explanation methods explain model outputs but do not quantify real-world intervention effects; need a framework to attribute heterogeneous causal effects to features with explicit intervention targets.

Method: Define interventional coalition game; allocate joint intervention contrast via causal Shapley contributions; derive Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two global CAS summaries; compare to existing SHAP rankings; evaluate on synthetic known-truth benchmarks and two DoubleML datasets.

Result: Local CAS achieves lower MAE (0.107 vs 0.173/0.213); advantage grows with interaction strength; predictive SHAP rankings differ materially from Feature-CAS rankings; dep1 example shows shift from global rank 13 to 2; shows CAS captures treatment-effect modifiers rather than predictor strength.

Conclusion: Separating what predicts the outcome from what explains causal effect heterogeneity adds value; CAS provides a practical local-to-global causal reporting layer with explicit intervention targets, improving interpretability of treatment effects.

Abstract: Predictive explanation methods attribute a model output; they do not, by themselves, attribute an intervention effect on the real-world outcome. We introduce the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a compact score architecture for causal explanation. CAS starts from an identified interventional coalition game, allocates the joint intervention contrast with causal Shapley contributions, and converts those raw outcome-scale effects into Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two complementary Global CAS summaries. The innovation is not a new Shapley formula, but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with an explicit intervention target. In the known-truth benchmark, eight repeated primary-interaction simulations (n = 2,200 each, three actions) gave mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 for coalition-aware CAS, compared with 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalisation and 0.213 for a global normalised absolute ATE vector. The paired advantage over one-at-a-time normalisation increased from -0.003 under additivity to 0.091 under strong interactions. On both empirical DoubleML datasets, 401(k) eligibility/net financial assets (n = 9,915) and Pennsylvania reemployment bonus/unemployment duration (n = 5,099), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differed materially from Feature-CAS rankings of treatment-effect modifiers. In Pennsylvania, dep1 (exactly one dependent) moved from predictive global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2 and was the leading local Feature-CAS modifier. These results isolate the added value of separating what predicts the outcome from what explains heterogeneity in an estimated causal effect.

</details>


### [226] [Trie Automata for Constrained Decoding over Large Finite Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12574)
*Xingzi Xu,Karim Bouyarmane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trie automaton accelerates constrained decoding for finite-set outputs, delivering large per-step and compilation speedups, enabling stateless batch serving with 100% output validity across large vocabularies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained decoding for structured outputs requires selecting from large finite sets; existing grammar-compilation backends (e.g., XGrammar) exhibit a depth/size-related latency (cardinality wall) that scales poorly as the set of valid strings grows into thousands.

Method: Introduce a trie automaton that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) using Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks, enabling a stateless serving path and precomputed masks for fast valid-token lookup and decoding.

Result: Per-step valid-token computation reduced to 0.65 μs (vs 5.8 μs with XGrammar); compilation speedups of 2–6.5× for K ≥ 300; end-to-end vLLM throughput of 219 req/s vs 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (29× overall); across seven tokenizer families (32K–262K vocab), compilation remains sub-100 ms up to K = 10,000 with flat per-step cost and guaranteed 100% output validity.

Conclusion: The trie automaton effectively eliminates the cardinality wall for constrained decoding, delivering scalable, fast, and exact constrained outputs suitable for batch serving in large LMs.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly need to generate structured outputs that conform to predefined schemas, with one common constraint being selection from a finite set of valid strings. Current constrained decoding systems handle this through general-purpose grammar compilation, which becomes prohibitively slow as the number of valid values grows into the thousands, a cardinality wall. We introduce the trie automaton, a specialized mechanism that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) via Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks. The trie achieves 7X faster per-step valid-token computation (0.65 us vs. 5.8 us) compared to XGrammar, one of the primary backends in vLLM and SGLang, and 2--6.5X faster compilation at K >= 300. Because precomputed masks enable a stateless serving path that bypasses the guided decoding pipeline, this advantage compounds in batch serving: end-to-end vLLM throughput reaches 219 req/s vs. XGrammar's 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (29X). The 29X combines the algorithmic speedup with integration-path savings that only precomputed masks can unlock. Across seven tokenizer families (32K--262K vocabulary), the trie maintains sub-100ms compilation up to K = 10,000 and flat per-step cost regardless of set size, while guaranteeing 100% output validity.

</details>


### [227] [Reasoning Jury: Multi-Model Consensus for Evaluating Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12585)
*Congchao Wang,Diwakar Singh,Qiaozi Gao,Spyros Matsoukas,Yang Liu,Mahdi Namazifar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Reasoning Jury uses a panel of open-weight LLMs with a moderated deliberation to identify long-reasoning defects, outperforming frontier models in accuracy and at lower cost, enabling richer failure analysis and potentially better data for training and runtime feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for reliable quality judgments of long reasoning traces to improve data curation, reinforce training signals during RL, and monitor model performance. Single-model judges and frontier models face accuracy or accessibility limitations, so a jury-based approach may yield higher fidelity judgments at lower cost.

Method: Assemble a jury of LLMs (e.g., open-weight models like gpt-oss-120b) with a moderator. Jurors deliberate, critique each other's judgments, and modify initial votes. The moderator derives a consensus via deliberation or consolidation. Compare against frontier-model judges on identifying reasoning defects and assess cost.

Result: The jury approach significantly outperforms frontier models at correctly identifying reasoning defects; aggregated jury cost is about 8–15% of frontier-model judging costs. Also enables richer analysis of failure modes and model performance.

Conclusion: Reasoning Jury demonstrates that a moderated, multi-agent judging framework with open-weight models can achieve higher fidelity defect identification at a lower cost, with practical implications for data curation, RL training signals, and runtime feedback in reasoning LLMs.

Abstract: Improving reasoning LLMs requires the ability to judge the quality of long reasoning traces for effective reasoning data curation, strong training signals during reinforcement learning, and an in-depth understanding of reasoning behaviors during model performance evaluation. Additionally, surfacing reasoning mistakes that the model makes would enable improving the model's performance at runtime through providing feedback. Due to the difficulty of this complex task on long reasoning traces, single-model judges (even frontier models) do not do well at identifying reasoning defects. Additionally, leveraging frontier models during online training of reasoning LLMs is generally prohibited due to guardrails in terms of use. In this work, we introduce Reasoning Jury, a system that replaces the single judge with a jury of LLMs and a moderated consensus mechanism, to improve the fidelity of judgments for identifying reasoning defects. In reasoning jury, defects of a reasoning trace and their severity are surfaced through a deliberation where a moderator conducts a discussion amongst the jury where the jurors critique each other's judgments and get to modify their initial votes. The moderator derives a consensus through deliberation amongst jurors or consolidation of judgements. We show that Reasoning Jury with a jury of open-weight models (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) is able to significantly outperform frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, and gemini-3.1-pro) at correctly identifying reasoning defects. Besides accuracy performance improvements, the aggregated cost of the jury (initial verdicts, deliberations, consolidation, etc.) is a fraction (8 to 15%) of the cost of running frontier models in LLM-as-a-judge setup. We also show how these judgements can be leveraged to understand failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks, which allows much deeper understanding of a model's performance.

</details>


### [228] [Auditable agentic AI for evidence-grounded thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12590)
*Haifan Gong,Shiyu Chen,Bodong Wang,Yuqi Wang,Shijie Wang,Guoliang You,Xinyu Xiong,Haowei Wang,Mingzhi Mao,Dexing Kong,Qinghua Liu,Wei Lou,Fei Chen,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Integrated clinician-interactive AI (ThyroidXAgent) coordinates thyroid ultrasound tasks (localization, measurement, risk stratification, reporting) with auditable case-level records; achieves strong segmentation/classification on large multicenter data and improves reporting consistency and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Thyroid ultrasound assessment requires coordinated localization, measurement, risk stratification, and reporting. Most AI systems address tasks in isolation and lack auditable, clinician-correctable workflow support; there is a need for end-to-end agentic AI that can coordinate tools and provide audit trails.

Method: Developed ThyroidXAgent on OpenThyroidDB (≈0.3 million images, 24,000 paired reports). Evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres in the NHC-MISD-TUS cohort. Assessed nodule segmentation (Dice), benign–malignant classification (AUROC), lymph-node metastasis prediction (AUROC), and follicular vs papillary classification (AUROC). Implemented evidence-grounded report generation and introduced ThyClinScore (lesion-level clinical semantic metric). Compared report generation with multimodal language-model baselines.

Result: Mean Dice for nodule segmentation: 87.21%. Benign–malignant AUROC: 0.9466. Lymph-node metastasis AUROC: 0.864. Follicular vs papillary AUROC: 0.805. Report generation with evidence-grounded assembly outperformed LM baselines. ThyroidXAgent improved physician classification accuracy; report diagnostic consistency rose from 70.3% to 86.2%; segmentation time reduced by 35.9% and reporting time by 27.4%.

Conclusion: Auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI can enhance thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting across multicenter datasets, with strong task performance and concrete workflow efficiency gains.

Abstract: Thyroid ultrasound diagnosis requires coordinated lesion localization, measurement, risk stratification and reporting, yet most AI systems address these tasks in isolation and provide limited support for clinical review. We present ThyroidXAgent, a clinician-interactive agentic AI system that coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores their outputs as an auditable case-level evidence record. The system was developed using OpenThyroidDB, a multicentre, multitask resource integrating approximately 0.3 million ultrasound images and 24,000 paired reports, and was evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres in the private NHC-MISD-TUS cohort. Across heterogeneous datasets, ThyroidXAgent achieved a mean Dice score of 87.21 percent for nodule segmentation and a mean AUROC of 0.9466 for benign-malignant classification. The same workflow supported lymph-node metastasis prediction and follicular versus papillary thyroid carcinoma classification, with AUROCs of 0.864 and 0.805, respectively. For report generation, evidence-grounded assembly outperformed multimodal language-model baselines across three cohorts. ThyClinScore, a lesion-level clinical semantic metric introduced here, showed the strongest correlation with a location-aware language-model judge. ThyroidXAgent improved physician classification accuracy, increased report diagnostic consistency from 70.3 percent to 86.2 percent, and reduced segmentation and reporting time by 35.9 percent and 27.4 percent, respectively. These findings support auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting.

</details>


### [229] [DiG-bench: Discovery in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12593)
*Ruairidh M. Battleday,Kai Sandbrink,Jimi Cullen-Drohan,Zihan Yan,Timothy Muller,Clare Maguire,Ales Kubicek,Fraser Greenlee-Scott,Sukrit Sumant,Tri Dao,Jürgen Schmidhuber,Michal Valko,Joshua Tenenbaum,Thomas L. Griffiths,Zeb Kurth-Nelson,James C. R. Whittington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces DiG-bench, a 70-game benchmark for discovery in games with unknown rules and objectives, spanning seven difficulty tiers; 21 games publicly released, 49 kept private for secure evaluation; all games solvable by at least one human on first attempt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses a gap in AI benchmarks that test the ability to discover new knowledge through experimentation in controlled environments where objectives are unknown, enabling measurement of exploratory and rule-discovery capabilities.

Method: A set of 70 independent games encoded as short strings with unique transformation rules that agents must discover through interaction and experimentation. Levels incrementally test whether the rules have been correctly inferred and whether the (unknown) win conditions are satisfied. Seven difficulty tiers are provided to gauge progress from solvable by multiple models to requiring agentic strategies. A subset of 21 games is publicly released; the remainder is reserved privately for secure evaluation.

Result: All 70 games were solvable by at least one human on first attempt. A publicly released subset of 21 games exists, with the remainder kept private for evaluation; this demonstrates human baselines and a scalable benchmark structure.

Conclusion: DiG-bench fills a gap in AI benchmarking by providing a multi-tier, discovery-focused testbed that requires agents to infer unknown rules and objectives through experimentation, with a clear split between public and private evaluation assets to ensure measurement integrity.

Abstract: Discovery---formulating novel generalizations---is a central part of the scientific process. Despite its importance, there is a gap in the current AI benchmark landscape, with few benchmarks directly probing the capacity for discovering new knowledge with experimentation in controlled environments where the objective is unknown. To address this gap, we release a new benchmark: DiG-bench (Discovery in Games). DiG-bench consists of a set of 70 independent games. Each game is encoded as a short string and has unique transformation rules that must be discovered through interaction and experimentation. The levels of the game present a series of challenges to test whether the rules have been discovered, where the win conditions for each level are also unknown. We provide games at seven tiers of difficulty for AI agents. The lowest tier is routinely solvable by multiple models, while the highest tier challenges the best models in agentic harnesses. All 70 games were solved by at least one human on first attempt. A subset of 21 games is released publicly, and the remainder is held private for secure evaluation.

</details>


### [230] [Dead text or binding clause? Measuring and restoring constraint influence in black-box LLM dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12599)
*Haoyuan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A model-API ledger-based framework (contract ledger, per-clause measurement, and a repair ladder) reduces behavioral relapse in constraint revocation for multi-turn dialogues by enabling ahead-of-time compilation of the net constraint state, per-clause adherence probes, and budget-aware repairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address behavioral relapse (revocation inertia) where users revoke constraints but models continue to follow them; there is no per-clause measurement, pre-delivery prediction, or budget-aware repair of revocation.

Method: 1) A contract ledger pairs each constraint with an executable checker and records revocations as tombstones; compiles net constraint state ahead of time into a single specification. 2) A sequential ablation probe measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effect. 3) A repair ladder operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets. 4) Evaluation on a dataset combining NTasks/HumanEval tasks with verified checkers.

Result: Relapse scales with constraint load and model capacity; ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse vs a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline; adaptive ladder interventions add no detectable gain; a relapse-predicting probe achieves AUROC performance; a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect and survives placebo control; revocation failure becomes measurable, predictable, and repairable, with modest delivery overhead and API compute cost per result.

Conclusion: A ledger-enabled, precompiled constraint state plus per-clause measurement yields measurable and repairable control over revocation behavior in dialogue systems; while compilation is effective, ladder-based repairs offer limited additional benefit within budget constraints, and there is an identifiable cost to API usage.

Abstract: Multi-turn dialogues let users revoke constraints as easily as impose them, but revocation does not reliably take effect: models keep enacting withdrawn requirements (occasionally beneath comments asserting their removal), a failure we call \emph{behavioral relapse}, or revocation inertia. No existing instrument measures this influence per clause, predicts it before delivery, or repairs it under matched budgets. \sysname{} closes the three gaps through the model API alone: a contract ledger pairs every constraint with an executable checker, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles the net constraint state ahead of time into a single specification; a sequential ablation probe measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effect; a repair ladder operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets. On \dataname{} (\NTasks{} HumanEval tasks, \NClauses{} verified checkers), relapse at an 8B operating point climbs from \ScaleDelayedMTwo{} to \ScaleDelayedMEight{} as constraint load grows, while stronger models sit at floor. Under matched checkers, model, and budget, ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse against a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline (\RestoreDiff{}, 95\% CI \RestoreDiffCI{}, $p$ \RestoreDiffP{}); adaptive ladder interventions stacked on top add no detectable gain (95\% confidence excludes gains $\geq$ \LadderExcludedGain{}). The probe predicts relapse before delivery (AUROC \AurocPrimary{}); a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect and survives a placebo control. At \CostDeliveryFactor{} delivery overhead and \CostTotalHedged{} of API compute for every result, revocation failure becomes a measurable, predictable, and repairable property of dialogue state rather than an invisible one.

</details>


### [231] [@skills: Attention is all you have](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12610)
*Li Yin,Zhi Li,Zhan Shi,Haoran Zhang,Haebin Seong,Zhangyang,Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes @skills, an open protocol that separates skill content, persistence, and triggering from resident prompts, enabling usage by reading a skill without installation. Uses path-based addressing, Git-tracked copies, and a minimal .gitignore line to incur prompt residency. Includes a hub (atskills.one) for corpus-wide search and ranking, with open spec and AdaL CLI implementation; aims to scale long-tail skills and simplify ownership and adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the bottleneck and saturation of prompt residency caused by the dominant “installation” model, which leaves many skills unusable and forces teams to crowd scarce prompt space.

Method: Introduce a protocol that separates three functions of a skill (content, persistence, triggering). Reading a skill suffices to use it; no installation is required. Operations copy a working replica of the skill to a project's Git-tracked tree; adds a single .gitignore-style line; directories act as menus rather than monolithic units. No manifest, lockfile, or registration is required; the open spec is hosted at a GitHub repo and implemented in the AdaL CLI. A companion hub at atskills.one enables corpus-wide search, ranking, and repository-free hosting; local paths and gh: identities remain usable without the hub.

Result: A design-level, installable package that enables read-based usage of skills without residency. The protocol enables addressing any skill/subtree/collection via paths, supports adaptation and ownership via a Git-tracked copy, and lowers the barrier to discover and reuse skills. The hub provides optional corpus-wide search and ranking, while preserving compatibility with local paths and gh: identities.

Conclusion: The approach promises scalable utilization of a large skill ecosystem by decoupling content from persistence and triggering, reducing prompt residency to a single line, and enabling straightforward adaptation and ownership. Its success hinges on adoption, effective search/ranking in the hub, and handling versioning, security, and consistency without manifests. The optional hub further enhances discovery but remains non-mandatory.

Abstract: There are 56,804 public agent skills today, and teams write many more privately. The dominant delivery model is installation: once installed, a skill's description remains in the system prompt, competing for fewer than 100 reliable trigger slots. This leaves the long tail with no practical path to use and forces teams' own playbooks to compete for the same scarce space. We observe that installation bundles three separable functions: content, persistence, and automatic triggering. Only the last requires prompt residency. We therefore propose @skills, an open protocol that separates them. A path addresses any skill, subtree, or collection, and reading a skill is sufficient to use it, so nothing is installed or made resident. The operation vendors a copy at the same path into a project's Git-tracked tree for adaptation and ownership. The operation adds one .gitignore-style line, the only element that costs prompt residency. A directory is a menu, making bundles ordinary directories rather than all-or-nothing units. The protocol requires no manifest, lockfile, or registration, and SKILL.md remains unchanged. @skills is additive, ships as an installable package, and turns any agent that can read files and run commands into a client through a single instruction file. Its open specification is at https://github.com/SylphAI-Inc/atskills and it is implemented in the AdaL CLI at https://adalagent.ai . Because paths address skills well but cannot find them, the protocol is paired with a free hub at https://atskills.one for corpus-wide search and ranking, repository-free hosting, private and team collections, and one-screen authoring. The hub is optional: gh: and local paths resolve without it, and indexed GitHub skills retain their gh: identities. Install less, use more.

</details>


### [232] [Jagged Judges: Epistemic Stability Under Silence, Pressure, and Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12645)
*Justin Zhao,Himaghna Bhattacharjee,Hannah Korevaar,Bhaktipriya Radharapu,Khalid El-Arini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wiggle Framework provides a unified stress test for epistemic stability of LLM judges, revealing substantial verdict wiggle across three dimensions and showing that pressure to flip verdicts is largely net-corrupting; baseline majority strength is a strong predictor of wiggle; enables apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy on golden data masks stability under re-prompting, challenge, and adaptive pressure. There is a need to quantify robustness of LLM judges across mechanical consistency, single-turn conviction, and multi-turn persistence.

Method: Introduce the Wiggle Framework decomposing robustness into Mechanical Consistency, Single-turn Conviction, and Multi-turn Persistence. Evaluate 9 frontier models across 14 tasks (safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, political-response evaluation). Apply static pushback and adversarial LLM persuaders; measure verdict flips and net-corruption relative to ground truth; analyze majority-strength as predictor.

Result: Verdit wiggle is substantial: models flip 25–71% under static pushback and 62–91% with an adversarial persuader; pressure that changes a verdict is almost always net-corrupting with respect to ground truth. Baseline majority strength is the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating wiggle. Provides the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in judging contexts.

Conclusion: Reveals vulnerability of LLM judges to re-prompting and adversarial pressure; underscores need for robust evaluation frameworks beyond golden data accuracy; offers a practical predictor (majority strength) and a cross-dataset framework for robustness testing.

Abstract: LLM judges have become central infrastructure for model evaluations, online grading, and reward modeling. Judges are typically validated by accuracy on golden data, but accuracy says little about whether they are stable under re-prompting, challenge, or sustained pushback. We introduce the \emph{Wiggle Framework}, a unified stress test for epistemic stability in LLM judges. The framework decomposes judge robustness along three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency (stability under re-prompting and reframing), Single-turn Conviction (stability under a single challenge), and Multi-turn Persistence (stability under sustained or adaptive pressure). We use the framework to study 9 frontier models across 14 judging tasks spanning safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, and political-response evaluation. Every model exhibits substantial wiggle as a judge --- flipping verdicts 25--71\% of the time under static pushback, and 62--91\% with an adversarial LLM persuader. Critically, we find that pressure that succeeds in changing a judge's verdict is almost always net-corrupting with respect to ground truth. Beyond the framework itself, we identify baseline jury majority strength as the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating which items wiggle. Taken together, this is the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in a judging context.

</details>


### [233] [SteerBench-Work: A Benchmark for Agent Steering at Action Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12654)
*Oguz Serdar,Cuneyt Mertayak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SteerBench-Work evaluates LLM action-gating decisions across domains using incident-anchored scenarios and evidence-reversed mirrors; results show asymmetric errors with higher-capability models over-refusing, across 106 cases; public leaderboard at steerbench.com.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calibrate long-running LLM agents' pre-commit decisions (proceed vs hold) to align with safety/policy across diverse workplace domains, recognizing that broad capability does not guarantee proper steering.

Method: Incident-anchored scenarios across developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security; 106 cases anchored to public incidents with evidence-reversed mirrors and calibration controls; models observe proposed action plus evidence and return a gate decision; evaluated across 30 model conditions with boundary-cross vs hold scoring.

Result: Hold on authorized, evidence-cleared work: 28.1%; allow unsafe work: 1.0% across opportunities; hardest cases are risk-resolved commits; models score 63.8% on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents vs 98.5% on the incidents themselves; higher-capability models over-refuse at the commit boundary; more reasoning can repair a weak gate but not a calibrated one.

Conclusion: Steering calibration is not the same as general capability; calibrated gates remain challenging even for strong models, especially under risk-resolved and mirror scenarios. The design highlights the importance of incident-based evaluation and evidence handling; public leaderboard provides benchmark.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents act through tools, and a single step can send an email, merge a pull request, or wire a payment. The steering decision is the pre-commit choice at that boundary: proceed, or hold for human or policy review. We introduce SteerBench-Work, an incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark for that decision in workplace agents across developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security.
  Release v2026-05 contains 106 scenarios anchored in public incidents, paired evidence-reversed mirrors, and calibration controls, with labels split nearly evenly between proceed and hold so the two error directions get near-identical numbers of chances. A model sees the proposed action and the available evidence, returns a gate decision, and is scored on whether it crosses or holds the boundary correctly. Across 30 model conditions the failures run almost entirely in one direction: models wrongly hold authorized, evidence-cleared work on 28.1% of opportunities and wrongly allow unsafe work on 1.0%. The hardest cases are risk-resolved commits, where signed or structured evidence has already cleared a real risk trigger, and models score markedly worse on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents (63.8%) than on the incidents themselves (98.5%). General capability is not the same as steering calibration: higher-capability models often over-refuse at the commit boundary, and more reasoning can repair a weak gate while leaving a calibrated one flat. The public leaderboard is at steerbench.com.

</details>


### [234] [General Probabilities of Causation with Causal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12657)
*Xin Shu,Zhen Lei,Ang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multivalued probabilities of causation bounds tightened using covariates and mediators; empirical validation via toy examples and simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PoCs are often partially identifiable; existing binary bounds and some multivalued extensions leave potential gaps. Incorporating additional causal information can tighten bounds in multivalued settings.

Method: Derive sharper theoretical bounds for multivalued PoCs by integrating information from covariates and mediators; provide formal proofs; illustrate with toy examples; perform simulations to compare with nonbinary bounds.

Result: The proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds; examples and simulations demonstrate improvement across scenarios.

Conclusion: Integrating covariates and mediators yields tighter multivalued PoC bounds, advancing partial identification in causal inference and extending prior binary/multivalued results.

Abstract: Probabilities of causation (PoCs) characterize individual causal responses that cannot be directly observed and therefore generally require partial identification. Tian and Pearl first derived theoretically sharp bounds for binary PoCs, including the probability of necessity (PN), the probability of sufficiency (PS), and the probability of necessity and sufficiency (PNS). Mueller et al. subsequently tightened the bounds for binary PNS by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. More recently, Li and Pearl, as well as Shu et al., extended PoCs to multivalued settings and derived corresponding theoretical bounds. These developments naturally raise the question of whether additional causal knowledge can further tighten the bounds in multivalued settings. This paper addresses this question by deriving tighter bounds for multivalued PoCs through the incorporation of causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. We illustrate the theoretical results with toy examples, while simulation studies further demonstrate that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

</details>


### [235] [Designing AI Pipelines for Decision-Ready ITSM Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12670)
*Archan Dutta,Yash Dharmadhikari,Marat Valiullin,Rahul Guha,Alexander Liss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sociotechnical AI pipeline converts ITSM exports into hierarchical, executive-facing decision-support artifacts with strong stakeholder trust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address information overload in heterogeneous ITSM ticket data by transforming it into actionable insights for sales engineering and customer success, framing ITSM analytics as an information systems problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design.

Method: Design Science Research workflow; LLM-based schema normalization; HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering; hierarchical agglomerative clustering; generation of Main-topics and Sub-topics; stakeholder evaluation on six artifacts by five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success roles.

Result: Produced multilevel decision-support artifacts; four decision-support metrics—interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use—average >4.0/5.0, with trust as the most consistent signal.

Conclusion: Frames ITSM analytics as an IS problem of transformation and abstraction; the sociotechnical AI pipeline yields interpretable, actionable executive-facing insights and demonstrates the value of human-centered design in ITSM analytics.

Abstract: IT service management (ITSM) systems accumulate large volumes of heterogeneous ticket data that are difficult for sales and executive stakeholders to convert into actionable intelligence. This paper presents a sociotechnical AI pipeline, designed and evaluated following design science research principles, that transforms raw ITSM exports into a multilevel decision-support artifact. The pipeline combines LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to generate executive-facing Main-topics and granular Sub-topics. A stakeholder evaluation across six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success roles shows that all four decision-support metrics, interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use, on average exceed 4.0 out of 5.0, with trust as the most consistent signal. The findings position ITSM analytics as an Information Systems (IS) problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design.

</details>


### [236] [On the Expressive Power of Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12671)
*Phokion Kolaitis,Rik Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey describing how circuit complexity is used to analyze the expressive power of multi-layer transformers, framing transformer resources (e.g., attention, precision) in terms of circuit resources, and presenting selected results that map transformers to circuit models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously quantify what transformers can compute by aligning their expressive power with classical circuit complexity classes, leveraging a principled resource-based framework.

Method: Literature survey of results that use circuit complexity concepts to bound or characterize transformer expressivity; frames transformers via resource parameters (attention, precision) and draws mappings to circuit models (gate types, size, depth).

Result: The article overviews selected results linking transformers to circuit complexity, arguing that circuit-based analyses provide a natural and informative lens for transformer expressivity and enabling principled comparisons across models and resource constraints.

Conclusion: Circuit complexity offers a principled framework for understanding transformer expressivity; the paper compiles and highlights existing results and suggests directions for deeper investigation into how attention and precision translate into circuit parameters.

Abstract: Multi-layer transformers form the critical component of essentially all large language models (LLMs) in use today. Because of their ubiquity and computational capability, there is a rapidly growing body of work that aims to precisely calibrate the expressive power of transformers as language recognizers by comparing them against standard models of computation studied for decades by the theoretical computer science community. In this endeavor, circuit complexity has by and large emerged as the "correct" branch of computational complexity to analyze the expressive power of transformers; the reason is that parameterizing transformers by the various resources they use, such as attention and precision, leads to direct comparisons with different classes of circuits parameterized by resources such as type of gates, size, and depth. Here, we present an overview of selected results that delineate the expressive power of transformers using concepts and methods from circuit complexity.

</details>


### [237] [Lines and Ladders: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Framework for Large-Scale Retail Price Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12674)
*Ravi Teja Chunduri,Srikaran Reddy Boya,Deep Narayan Mishra,Ajay Kumar B,Karthik Kumaran,Pranay Kona*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A scalable, context-aware multi-agent framework uses specialized LLM agents to construct Lines and Ladders pricing taxonomies for millions of items, achieving high accuracy and precision in price governance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual governance of price relationships is infeasible with catalogs spanning millions of items; inconsistent pricing harms customer value perception and sales. An automated, scalable approach is needed to maintain price consistency and support an Every Day Low Price strategy.

Method: A 3-agent system employing specialized LLM agents to identify key attributes, extract multi-modal values, and apply hierarchical grouping logic to build coherent Lines and Ladders pricing taxonomies. The agents operate in a context-aware setting and leverage multi-modal inputs to form taxonomies.

Result: In real-world production data, the system achieves F1=0.83 for Lines (outperforming single-agent baselines by reducing cognitive overload). It attains >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in the unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

Conclusion: The framework provides scalable, robust automation for constructing pricing taxonomies, improving price consistency and enabling effective implementation of an Every Day Low Price strategy across large, heterogeneous catalogs.

Abstract: Maintaining price consistency and executing an Every Day Low Price strategy is critical for global retailers. However, with catalogs spanning millions of active items, manual governance of price relationships is infeasible. Inconsistent pricing across item variants distorts customer value perception and cannibalizes sales. To address this, we present a scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework designed to automate the construction of "Lines and Ladders" pricing taxonomies. Our framework employs specialized LLM agents to construct these coherent pricing structures by identifying key attributes, extracting multi-modal values, and applying hierarchical grouping logic. Evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production, our 3-Agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines by mitigating cognitive overload. The system achieves >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in the unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

</details>


### [238] [Privacy-Preserving RAG by Concealing Sensitive Information from External LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675)
*Saleh Almohaimeed,Saad Almohaimeed,Mousa Jari,Fahad Alotaibi,Khalid A. Alobaid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEAG is a privacy-preserving framework for Retrieval-Augmented Generation that replaces sensitive words in queries and retrieved documents with generated aliases before sending to external generators; it uses a lightweight model to locate sensitive entities and create an entity replacement table.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate privacy risks in RAG where external generators can access user queries and retrieved documents containing confidential information.

Method: A lightweight SEAG model locates sensitive entities, generates aliases, and builds an entity replacement table. This table is used to replace sensitive words in both the user's query and the retrieved documents prior to forwarding to external generators. Two datasets were constructed: one for fine-tuning SEAG to generate replacement tables and another for evaluating the full SEAG pipeline.

Result: SEAG achieves over 80% accuracy on the User metric (correct responses while hiding sensitive information). For specific models (Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, Phi-4), total accuracies at hiding all sensitive entities are 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91%, respectively.

Conclusion: SEAG demonstrates the feasibility and effectiveness of privacy-preserving augmentation for RAG by obfuscating sensitive information via entity aliasing, supported by dedicated datasets and empirical results.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is widely used to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in answering user queries. Existing privacy research on RAG has focused on preventing unauthorized users from accessing sensitive data. However, another important problem that is often overlooked in RAG privacy research is that external generators have access to the query and the retrieved documents, which may contain confidential information that could potentially be misused or accessed for unintended purposes. In this paper, we introduce the Sensitive Entity Alias Generator (SEAG), a privacy-preserving framework that empowers users to utilize powerful third-party generators without disclosing sensitive information. SEAG introduces a lightweight model that locates sensitive entities, generates corresponding aliases, and constructs an entity replacement table. The table is used to replace sensitive words in the user's query and in the retrieved documents before they are forwarded to an external generator. For this purpose, two datasets were constructed: one for fine-tuning SEAG models to generate entity replacement tables, and another for evaluating the entire SEAG framework. The experimental results demonstrate the success of the SEAG framework. As for the User metric, which measures the ability of the model to provide a correct response to the user while hiding sensitive information from the external generator, all SEAG models achieved over 80% accuracy. Additional analysis further evaluated the ability of SEAG models Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 to hide all sensitive entities within given documents. The results show good performance with total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91%, respectively.

</details>


### [239] [The Role of Natural Language Understanding in Multimodal Video-Based Dengue Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12677)
*Danial Sharifrazi,Saadat Behzadi,Julakha Jahan Jui,Mojtaba Mohammadi,Nouman Javed,Roohallah Alizadehsani,Prasad N. Paradkar,Asim Bhatti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A YOLO- and CLIP-based multimodal framework for classifying infection status in mosquitoes from video frames; achieves high frame-level accuracy and sensitivity; improves robustness via vision-language alignment; temporal aggregation yields accurate video-level results; ablation shows fine-tuning and CLIP representations are essential; textual prompts provide semantic alignment but do not surpass a vision-only baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detect infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video data; mosquitoes are small, fast, and affected by background and lighting, making reliable feature extraction difficult—necessitating robust multimodal approaches.

Method: 1) Use YOLO to segment mosquito regions from the background. 2) Extract visual features from frames and align them with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space. 3) Fine-tune the multimodal model with supervised bidirectional contrastive learning. 4) Classify frames via frame-level image-text similarity and aggregate temporally for video-level decisions. 5) Conduct ablation studies to assess the contributions of fine-tuning, CLIP representations, and the textual branch.

Result: Frame-level accuracy: 98.54%; frame-level sensitivity: 99.91%. Temporal aggregation yields complete video-level performance. Ablations indicate fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations are essential; the textual branch provides semantic alignment but does not outperform a vision-only model.

Conclusion: Vision-language models offer a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data. CLIP-based multimodal representations enhance robustness to environmental variability, with textual prompts mainly supporting semantic alignment rather than increasing frame-level accuracy.

Abstract: Detecting infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video data is challenging because mosquitoes are small, move rapidly and irregularly, and are affected by environmental factors such as background, lighting, and shadows, which can make reliable feature extraction difficult. In this study, a YOLO- and Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP)-based vision-language framework is proposed to classify mosquito flight frames of uninfected and Dengue virus serotype 2 (DENV2)-infected mosquitoes. First, YOLO is used to isolate mosquito regions from the background. Then, visual features extracted from video frames are aligned with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space. The multimodal model was fine-tuned using supervised bidirectional contrastive learning and evaluated through frame-level image-text similarity-based classification. The results show that the proposed method achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity at the frame level. After temporal aggregation of frame-level information, the model achieved complete video-level performance. The ablation results showed that fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations were essential for this domain, while the textual branch provided semantic image-text alignment rather than an accuracy advantage over the vision-only model. These findings suggest that vision-language models can provide a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data.

</details>


### [240] [Beyond the Best Guess: Improving LLM Solution Coverage with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12679)
*Conor F. Hayes,Elliot Meyerson,Kajetan Schweighofer,Roberto Dailey,Babak Hodjat,Risto Miikkulainen,Xin Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ES-based post-training yields higher pass@k and broader solution coverage than RL for LLM discovery tasks, improving performance on math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome RL-induced narrowing of the output distribution and to enhance discovery by increasing test-time solution space coverage in LLMs.

Method: Comparative analysis of Evolution Strategies (ES) vs Reinforcement Learning (RL) as post-training methods for LLMs. ES uses population-based, gradient-free weight perturbations; evaluation focuses on pass@k and output diversity on math/science discovery tasks.

Result: ES consistently achieves higher pass@k than RL, with a broader output distribution and greater solution coverage, translating to better performance on standard math benchmarks.

Conclusion: ES provides a more robust foundation for post-training in discovery problems and related domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in discovery domains such as math and science. The usual approach is to present the problem to the model and use its answer as the proposed solution. However, beyond this best guess, discovery can be enhanced by increasing test-time compute. In a process called pass@k, the model is allowed to explore the solution space and generate diverse candidate solutions. Unfortunately, the standard approach to post-training LLMs through Reinforcement Learning (RL) may limit pass@k: the model's output distribution narrows around high-reward outputs, causing the solution coverage to collapse. The alternative is to use Evolution Strategies (ES), a population-based, gradient-free post-training method that optimizes directly in weight space through random perturbations. As this paper shows, ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage. This coverage in turn makes it possible to achieve better results in e.g. standard math benchmarks. Thus, ES provides a better foundation for post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

</details>


### [241] [Spatial Memory Agent: Experience-Grounded Procedure Memory for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743)
*Haokai Zhang,Yuhang Ding,Yunshu Zhou,Xinze Du,Shengtao Zhang,Zhiyue Zhao,Yuling Xi,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A parameter-update-free self-evolution framework for frozen Vision-Language Models (SMA) uses verified spatial experiences to distill transferable lessons, enabling improved spatial reasoning without external tools at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between post-training fine-tuning and agentic tool use by enabling frozen VLMs to improve spatial reasoning through self-supervised memory and reflection, without updating model weights or relying on external spatial tools.

Method: SMA operates in a verifiable spatial environment with a frozen VLM. It queries the VLM for answers and rewards, uses verifier-guided reflection to distill compact transferable lessons, assigns each lesson a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS) initialized uniformly and calibrated from retrieval outcomes, and, during read-only deployment, retrieves lessons via semantic filtering and similarity-TRS ranking to guide inference without updating weights or using external tools.

Result: Across five spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy in most of 20 evaluations, demonstrating that parameter-update-free spatial self-evolution is feasible across different models and environments.

Conclusion: A practical, scalable path for improving spatial reasoning in frozen VLMs via experience-grounded memory and verifier-guided lesson transfer, offering a compelling alternative to fine-tuning or external tools for spatial intelligence.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is becoming a foundation for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. To improve the spatial reasoning ability of VLM agents, existing work has mainly followed two lines. One line uses post-training methods, such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Another line adopts an agentic paradigm in which the model calls external spatial tools, such as depth estimation and 3D reconstruction tools, to gather intermediate spatial evidence. We study a complementary and underexplored route: Can a frozen VLM agent improve its spatial reasoning through \textbf{parameter-update-free self-evolution}, without depending on external expert spatial tools at inference time? We present \textbf{Spatial Memory Agent (SMA)}, an \textbf{experience-grounded runtime framework} that converts verified spatial experience into reusable transferable lessons. In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA queries the frozen VLM, obtains a predicted answer and reward, and uses \textbf{verifier-guided reflection} to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience. SMA further assigns each lesson a \textbf{Transfer Reliability Score (TRS)}, which is initialized uniformly and calibrated from later retrieval outcomes as visit evidence of future transfer reliability. During \textbf{read-only deployment}, SMA retrieves lessons by semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking, allowing the retrieved memory to guide frozen model inference. Across five representative spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy among the evaluated methods in most of the 20 evaluations, establishing a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution across the evaluated frozen model scales and environments.

</details>


### [242] [Correct Is Not Governed: Provenance Integrity in Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12761)
*Jesus Salas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Matrix adds a deterministic causal-state layer to record authority and dependency provenance, verify completion evidence, and selectively invalidate work, enabling auditable governance of agentic workflows. Outcomes may match direct workflows, but governance preserves evidence and resists unsupported closures; its role is institutional integrity rather than general accuracy enhancement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the inadequacy of outcome-only evaluation in institutional settings by making decisions, claims, and responses to change inspectable through provenance and governance constraints.

Method: Introduce Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that logs authority and fact dependencies, validates completion evidence, and invalidates affected tasks. Compare governed versus direct workflows in controlled experiments and test a role-separated transfer scenario.

Result: Governed workflows often reach the same outcomes as direct ones, but uniquely preserve governing evidence, reject unsupported closures, and constrain recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge shows a completeness contract can over-block actions outside its authoring context. Matrix does not claim to improve general accuracy but acts as an institutional integrity layer for auditable, verifiable agentic work.

Conclusion: Matrix functions as an institutional integrity layer rather than a general accuracy booster, enhancing auditable and independently verifiable agentic workflows while acknowledging limitations and context-specific behaviors.

Abstract: Agentic workflows are commonly evaluated by whether they reach the correct outcome. That is insufficient in institutional settings, where a correct action may rely on the wrong authority, an unsupported completion claim, or work made stale by a later change. We define governed execution as work whose decisions, completion, and response to change are supported by inspectable provenance. We present Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work. Across controlled comparisons, governed and direct workflows often reached the same outcomes, but only the governed path consistently preserved governing evidence, refused unsupported closure, and limited recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge then failed: a deterministically enforced completeness contract severely over-blocked synthetic packets produced outside its authoring context. These results do not establish Matrix as a general accuracy enhancer; they support its primary role as an institutional integrity layer for making agentic work auditable and independently verifiable.

</details>


### [243] [PROVE-RT: Generating Mechanized Theorem Prover Scripts for Real-Time Systems using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762)
*Sadat Shahriyar,Shareef Ahmed,Abdullah Al Arafat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-assisted PROSA/ROCQ mechanization via PROVE-RT with retrieval-guided, staged generation achieving 44.7% success on a real-time systems corpus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scale and validate mechanized schedulability proofs for real-time systems; existing pen-and-paper proofs are hard to scale and maintain; LLMs alone lack domain-specific PROSA/ROCQ knowledge.

Method: PROVE-RT uses dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA docs, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. Builds a corpus from 1,191 papers containing 13,134 informal sketches with dependency information; evaluates LLM-assisted generation against direct prompting.

Result: Direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations; PROVE-RT achieves a 44.7% success rate on a curated evaluation set, showing retrieval-guided and staged generation improves mechanization.

Conclusion: Retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ; the approach is supported by a substantial PROSA-oriented corpus and demonstrates practical gains over naive prompting.

Abstract: Schedulability analysis is essential for certifying real-time systems, but existing tests are often developed through pen-and-paper proofs that are difficult to scale, validate, and maintain. Mechanized verification in PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, yet manually constructing such proofs requires substantial domain expertise and proof-engineering effort. Recent successes of large language models (LLMs) across a wide range of tasks make them promising candidates for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized theorem provers. However, state-of-the-art LLMs often lack the PROSA-specific knowledge required to correctly use its modeling abstractions and proof patterns.
  This paper introduces PROVE-RT, an LLM-assisted framework for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts to mechanize schedulability analyses in real-time systems literature. PROVE-RT guides generation through dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. We construct a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1, 191 real-time systems papers, containing 13, 134 informal sketches with dependency information. On a curated evaluation set, direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations, whereas PROVE-RT achieves a success rate of 44.7%. These results show that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ.

</details>


### [244] [ARAC: Benchmarking Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness on End-to-End Researchs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788)
*Jiale Cui,Yueyao Yuan,Kaixi Zhong,Xiaogang Xu,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARAC-Bench evaluates Auto-Research by mimicking human researcher workflows, using a two-part framework to measure alignment and completeness; it reveals gaps in current models while correlating with PhD-quality judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the fundamental evaluation challenge of aligning Auto-Research outputs with human research behavior, moving beyond final-answer quality to replicate high-quality research processes.

Method: Two synergistic components: (1) Academic Cognition Skills system that converts implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated rubrics; (2) a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol that decomposes the research process into Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis under modular, traceable constraints.

Result: Evaluated 11 SOTA frameworks; best alignment score is 67.9/100, indicating a sizable gap in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. candidate rankings shows a strong correlation (0.8141).

Conclusion: ARAC-Bench serves as a fine-grained diagnostic tool and provides a scalable reward signal for training autonomous research systems, highlighting both the potential and current limitations of computational agents in replicating human research processes.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Auto-Research has surfaced a fundamental evaluation challenge: how can we measure the alignment, logical coherence, and evolutionary completeness of its research trajectory with human research behavior? We propose Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness, ARAC-Bench: a Researcher-Mimicking Evaluation framework that shifts the objective from matching final answers to reproducing high-quality human research processes. The framework operates through two synergistic components: the Academic Cognition Skills system, which is the first to transforms implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics; and a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol, which decomposes the research process under strict modular constraints into three traceable, mutually independent dimensions: Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. Systematic evaluation of 11 SOTA frameworks yields a best alignment score of only 67.9 of 100, revealing a significant gap in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. Candidates rankings shows a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming that ARAC-Bench reliably reflects the dimensions researchers truly value. ARAC-Bench provides not only a fine-grained diagnostic tool but also a scalable reward signal for training the next generation of autonomous research systems.

</details>


### [245] [CABS+: Efficient and Scalable Model Merging via Conflict-Aware Sparsification and Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842)
*Yuchen Liu,Zongzhen Yang,Binhang Qi,Hailong Sun,Xiang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CABS+ enhances model merging by adaptive, gradient-free merging weight allocation and an asymmetric fitness objective, plus the Relative Synergy Score, yielding higher performance and efficiency than prior methods across diverse models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate parameter conflicts and inefficiency in merging multi-task models and to balance performance across tasks beyond grid-search-based scaling.

Method: Extend CABS with Adaptive Weight Allocation (gradient-free search) and asymmetric fitness; introduce Relative Synergy Score (RSS); comprehensive empirical study across 27 datasets and 5 models; compare with CABS, AdaMerging, WUDIMerging.

Result: CABS+ achieves +16.97% relative improvement over AdaMerging and +12.93% over WUDIMerging; uses <25% GPU memory of AdaMerging; ~4x faster merging time than WUDIMerging; demonstrates stability across task numbers and model architectures.

Conclusion: CABS+ is an effective, efficient model-merging framework; RSS provides a guide for model selection; gradient-free allocation and asymmetric objective address time complexity and interference problems; enables robust multi-task merging.

Abstract: Model merging has recently attracted significant attention as a promising paradigm for constructing unified multi-task models without requiring additional retraining. However, parameter conflicts and knowledge interference across tasks often degrade merged-model performance. Prior work introduced Conflict-Aware and Balanced Sparsification (CABS), which reduces parameter interference through structured pruning and sequential masking. However, CABS relies on grid search to determine scaling coefficients, resulting in exponential time complexity, while its optimization objective can be dominated by high-performance tasks, leading to suboptimal overall performance. To address these limitations, we extend CABS and propose CABS+. Specifically, Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) optimizes merging coefficients via a gradient-free search scheme to reduce time complexity, while an asymmetric fitness function promotes more comprehensive performance gains across tasks. Moreover, we conduct a systematic empirical study of key factors influencing model merging performance and propose Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection. We compare CABS+ with state-of-the-art model merging methods, including CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging, across 27 datasets and 5 models covering large language, small-scale language, and vision models. Extensive experiments verify the effectiveness and efficiency of CABS+. Compared with AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, CABS+ improves overall performance by 16.97% and 12.93%, respectively, exhibits stronger stability and robustness across varying task numbers and model architectures, uses less than 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and achieves nearly a 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

</details>


### [246] [Beyond Retrieval: Query-Conditioned Reuse of Long-Horizon Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12847)
*Yifei Li,Heng Wang,Lingling Zhang,Muye Huang,Xinyu Zhang,Jiashuai Liu,Hang Yan,Rongman Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-retrieval reuse is a bottleneck in long-horizon trajectory memory; introduces a simple target-bound memory format (QCR) and an evaluation framework; results show improved success and efficiency, supporting a separation between retrieval quality and usable task support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval can identify past trajectories but provides no guidance on how to reuse them when users, constraints, or env state change. The post-retrieval reuse step is a distinct bottleneck that limits long-horizon performance; a framework is needed to evaluate how retrieved experience is transformed into useful support.
