Method: Propose an evaluation framework that fixes candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget while varying the support delivered to the agent. Instantiate with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a target-bound note recording a reusable procedure, recover bindings, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. Evaluate across 2,391 target instances in WebArena, WorkArena, AppWorld. Compare QCR to Full Trajectory; use summary reranking to select reusable memory for most targets.

Result: QCR achieves 62.3% average Success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects reusable memory for 94.8% of targets; end-task Success is within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Direct trajectory injection loses utility as traces grow longer or source values change, whereas target-bound support preserves a larger share of the gains.

Conclusion: The framework decouples retrieval quality from turning retrieved experience into safe, useful task support. Target-bound reuse (QCR) appears effective for sustaining gains in long-horizon tasks and serves as a test bed for the reuse hypothesis rather than asserting a universal memory format.

Abstract: Retrieval can identify a past trajectory that may matter, yet it does not specify how an acting agent should use that trajectory after users, entities, constraints, or environment state have changed. We identify this post-retrieval reuse step as a distinct bottleneck for long-horizon trajectory memory and formulate an evaluation framework that holds candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget fixed while varying the support delivered to the agent. We instantiate the framework with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a deliberately simple target-bound note that records a reusable procedure, bindings to recover, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. QCR serves to test the reuse hypothesis rather than to claim a universally preferred memory format. Across 2,391 target instances in WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, QCR reaches 62.3% average Success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task Success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Analyses by trajectory length and source--target binding shift show that direct trajectory injection loses much of its utility as traces grow longer or source-specific values change, whereas target-bound support preserves a larger share of the measured gain. The resulting framework separates retrieval quality from the problem of turning retrieved experience into safe, useful support for a new task.

</details>


### [247] [Practice Makes Unsafe: Skill Misevolution in Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12851)
*Xutao Mao,Liangjie Zhao,Xiang Zheng,Cong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SkillMisevo-Gym/Bench to expose and measure lifecycle risks of self-improving LLM agents'; SafeEvolve repairs unsafe content; extensive empirical study showing persistence can cause unsafe reuse, and safety wrappers reduce harm with minimal benign impact; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of lifecycle-aware benchmarks for self-improving LLM agents, where successful trajectories can become persistent, unsafe policies across authoring, retrieval, and execution stages.

Method: Develop SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill states across agent frameworks; SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen design exposing malicious exposure with benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics; SafeEvolve wrapper to repair unsafe content and govern reuse. Conduct large-scale experiments across 25 agent-method configurations (525 tasks per config, 25 episodes), assessing exposure to malicious tasks and impact on carryover harm (ASR), with baseline comparisons.

Result: All 21 evolved configurations authored unsafe artifacts; only 15 led to fresh-session harm. In exposure sweep, malicious tasks raised carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. SafeEvolve reduced unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, with benign utility changing by only ~0.4 points.

Conclusion: Persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates are written and what future executors reuse; the authors provide a reproducible framework and release code to study lifecycle risks in self-improving agents.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents convert successful trajectories into persistent cross-task state. An unsafe success can thereby become reusable policy after its triggering input disappears. Skill evolution makes this failure measurable by distilling operational trajectories into executable, transferable, and inspectable procedures. Because evolution optimizes task outcomes rather than procedure safety, compromised experience can cause skill misevolution. Existing benchmarks measure current behavior or static artifacts but cannot attribute risk across authoring, retrieval, and later execution. To expose this lifecycle, we introduce SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state across agent frameworks, and SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen design from malicious exposure to carryover tasks, with concept-aligned benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics. We also introduce SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs subsequent reuse. Across 25 agent-method configurations, each covering 525 tasks in 25 episodes, all 21 evolved configurations author unsafe artifacts, while only fifteen lead to fresh-session harm. In the exposure sweep, three malicious tasks raise carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. Across representative skill evolution methods, SafeEvolve reduces unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, while mean benign utility changes by only 0.4 points. Together, persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates write and what future executors reuse. Code is available at https://github.com/henrymao2004/misevolve.

</details>


### [248] [AI and Consumer Rights in India Working Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863)
*Omir Kumar,Sriya Sridhar,Vibhav Mithal,Balaraman Ravindran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: India's Consumer Protection Act 2019 could address AI harms in principle but fails to fully account for AI-specific causation and the multi-actor AI value chain; improvements needed for proportionate, enforceable liability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate adequacy and proportionality of liability allocation for defective AI in consumer products/services under CP Act 2019; identify gaps.

Method: Legal-analytic assessment focusing on statutory definitions (product, harm, deficiency), causation challenges, and delineation of roles along AI value chain; consider enforcement implications.

Result: Definitions are technology-agnostic and potentially cover AI-related harms; but proving causation is challenging since failures often arise from design choices, not discrete defects; the value chain mapping (manufacturer, seller, service provider) doesn't align with data providers, model developers, deployers, users; current liability frameworks lack proportional mechanisms for multistakeholder harms; enforcement needs sector-specific clarifications; AI entities could be covered but need clarified responsibilities.

Conclusion: CP Act 2019 could cover AI actors, but to be effective, there must be clarifications on causation, role mapping, and sector-specific governance; consider legislative refinement or regulatory guidance to enable proportionate liability across AI stakeholder network.

Abstract: As AI systems proliferate in consumer facing applications, questions about liability for AI related harms remain unresolved. This working paper examines whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, adequately addresses harm caused by defective AI products and services, and whether it proportionately allocates liability across the AI value chain.
  The Act's broad definitions of product liability, harm, and deficiency appear technology agnostic and potentially applicable to AI related incidents including personal injury, psychological harm, biased outputs, and loss of control. However, significant gaps remain. Proving causation between AI defects and consumer harm presents a technical challenge, as AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects. Additionally, the Act's framework assumes distinct roles for manufacturers, sellers, and service providers, yet the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users that do not neatly map to these categories. Current liability frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms to effectively address complex, multistakeholder AI harms. While the Act may cover AI entities, enforcement requires clarification on sector specific overlaps.

</details>


### [249] [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents for Improving Comprehension and Reasoning in Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877)
*Runze Zhao,Zixin Tang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Leyuan Chang,Xiaopeng Fu,Boyu Qiao,Dongyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReflectFact is a self-reflective multi-hop fact verification framework that uses explicit reasoning path planning, evidence-drift verification, and reasoning reflection verification to produce grounded, correct verdicts, achieving state-of-the-art on HOVER and EX-FEVER.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses (i) lack of global verification objective awareness in multi-agent subtask reasoning, and (ii) conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence that can mislead reasoning, by introducing mechanisms for planning, grounding, and reflection.

Method: 1) Explicit Reasoning Path Planning to map an evidence-grounded reasoning path, resolve implicit entities, decompose claims into sub-questions, and integrate facts into a final verdict. 2) Evidence-Drift Verification to re-answer with quotations from supporting evidence when outputs rely on parametric priors, calibrating grounding. 3) Reasoning Reflection Verification to re-examine each step, regenerate reasoning when inconsistencies are found, and mitigate biases through a global task view; aggregate validated chains to verdicts.

Result: Empirically shows state-of-the-art performance on HOVER and EX-FEVER, outperforming strong baselines by 3.32 percentage points on HOVER and 2.78 on EX-FEVER.

Conclusion: ReflectFact successfully remedies comprehension and reasoning defects of prior methods, yielding more reliable multi-hop fact-verification verdicts through planning, grounding, and self-reflection.

Abstract: Multi-hop fact verification, which verifies claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, is critical for combating misinformation on social media yet remains highly challenging. Recent methods primarily rely on multi-agent collaboration to decompose fact verification into specialized subtasks. However, these methods face two critical limitations: (1) agents may perform individual subtasks without sufficient awareness of the global verification objective, causing their reasoning to deviate from the intended direction; and (2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and the provided evidence may undermine evidence-grounded reasoning and lead to incorrect verdicts. To address these challenges, we propose ReflectFact, a novel self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification. ReflectFact introduces three key tasks. Explicit Reasoning Path Planning builds an evidence-grounded reasoning path by resolving implicit entities, decomposing the claim into sub-questions, and integrating the verified facts into a verdict. Evidence-Drift Verification makes the agent re-answer by quoting the supporting evidence when a grounded answer merely echoes its parametric prior, thereby calibrating evidence deviation to ensure grounded comprehension. Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it once an inconsistency is detected, correcting reasoning flaws such as location bias and replacement bias through a global task perspective. Subsequently, the agent aggregates validated reasoning chains to yield reliable verdicts. Extensive experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER demonstrate that ReflectFact effectively remedies the comprehension and reasoning defects of existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and respectively outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32\% and 2.78\% on the two datasets.

</details>


### [250] [Predictive Memory Localization: Forecasting Selective Intervention Paths from Internal Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12892)
*Jinhao Jing,Tian Zeyu,Lucas Qingyang Fang,Zhisheng Chen,Shuang Chen,Yuhao Luo,Qiannian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PML converts memory localization signals into falsifiable forecasts of margin-level selective outcomes, enabling risk-aware interventions with strong AUROC across multiple datasets and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Localization alone cannot reveal selective operating regimes; a predictive object and causal framing are needed to forecast when a direction will have a selective effect and guide interventions.

Method: Define the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object for memory localization. Decompose random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage. Compare static localization and supervised geometry using a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. A large, frozen study across 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains (30,000 record-direction-layer paths; 210,000 path-strength evaluations). Layer-7 geometry-derived RFM/AGOP directions yielding 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 pp under a record-paired bootstrap. Evaluate across splits; |α|=0.1 is the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths |α|∈{0.25,0.5}. On held-out records, predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improving utility vs a train-tuned fixed-strength policy and reducing dense-scan evaluations. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions maintain selective-path gains with low-dose responses giving macro AUROC 0.801–0.828.

Result: Layer-7 geometry-derived directions achieve measurable gains over random and demonstrate robustness across splits and held-out data. The predictor-driven selector enhances utility and reduces unnecessary evaluations, and the macro AUROC indicates practical discriminative ability.

Conclusion: PML reframes memory localization as a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and enables risk-aware intervention decisions.

Abstract: Activation steering turns localized representations into control directions, but localization alone does not reveal whether a direction has a selective operating regime. We introduce Predictive Memory Localization (PML), which treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization. PML separates random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage, and compares static localization and supervised geometry with a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. Our frozen study covers 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, yielding 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations. At layer 7, the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction reaches 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points under a record-paired bootstrap. Across record-, dataset-, and domain-grouped splits, responses at $|α|=0.1$ are the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths $|α|\in\{0.25,0.5\}$. On held-out records, a predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage relative to a train-tuned fixed-strength policy, and avoids most evaluations in a dense scan. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains and low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC. PML therefore turns memory localization into a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and a risk-aware intervention decision.

</details>


### [251] [Agent Behavioral Contracts II: Certifying Compositional Reliability Without Assuming Independence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12895)
*Varun Pratap Bhardwaj,Garima Singh,Arun Pratap Bhardwaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Independence-based compositional reliability bounds in multi-agent systems can be misleading due to positive dependence when components share a model; a nonparametric, certificate-based approach shows larger joint failure than naive independence would predict; enlarging moment functionals tightens bounds; dependence modeling can be problematic; provides an assumption-free linear-program certificate and an anytime-valid bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test the common conditional-independence assumption used to derive compositional reliability bounds, quantify the impact of dependence on joint failure, and provide robust, finite-sample certificates under limited data.

Method: Preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions with deterministic code (no LLM judge). Two instances of one model in a two-agent handoff. Measure co-failure; report log odds ratio and phi. Test substitutions: different model reduces association; different vendor/model does not. Develop an assumption-free certificate via a linear program over joint moments within a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box. Bootstrap bound on a fitted dependence model. Increase moment functionals from ten to fourteen to tighten bounds. Provide an anytime-valid certificate under optional stopping.

Result: Co-failure occurs on 90.0% of missions where either agent fails (log OR 6.66, 95% CI [6.38, 7.00]; phi = 0.916). Positive dependence inflates joint failure; independence product over-credits redundancy when components share a model. Substituting a different model reduces association across all six contrasts; substituting a different vendor/model does not. The bootstrap bound on a fitted model regresses toward worse coverage as n grows. The linear-program-based, assumption-free bound yields an identified interval; enlarging moments from ten to fourteen narrows the interval by 85.7% and raises the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116. An anytime-valid certificate maintains type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping. Release includes contracts, scoring code, analysis scripts, and preregistration.

Conclusion: Dependence between co-executed components matters for reliability bounds; naive independence-based bounds can misestimate joint failure, especially when components share the same model. An assumption-free linear-program certificate provides finite-sample guarantees, though can be conservative, and enriching moment functionals substantially improves identifiability. Dependence modeling via bootstrap is fragile, and reporting preregistrations and artifacts supports reproducibility.

Abstract: Compositional reliability bounds for multi-agent systems multiply component reliabilities, a step licensed by a conditional-independence assumption that is routinely stated and rarely tested. We test it. Two instances of one model, in a two-agent handoff, co-fail on 90.0% of the missions on which either fails (log OR 6.66, 95% CI [6.38, 7.00]; phi 0.916), in a preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions scored by deterministic code with no LLM judge. Substituting a different model reduces the association in six of six contrasts; substituting a different vendor, model already different, does not -- a registered hypothesis reported as a null.
  The error is signed and runs against the operator: positive dependence inflates joint failure above the independence product, so redundancy is over-credited exactly when components share a model. The assumption-free alternative is often vacuous, and fitting a dependence model is worse: we prove a bootstrap bound on a fitted model's functional loses coverage of the truth as n grows, the identification gap being O(1) while the bootstrap haircut is O(n^{-1/2}). More data makes such a certificate worse, with no visible symptom.
  We give a finite-sample certificate assuming no dependence structure: a linear program over the joint, over a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box around measured co-execution moments. It is sound, sharp for the information supplied, and monotone in the moment family. Enriching ten moment functionals to fourteen narrows the identified interval by 85.7% and lifts the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116. A companion anytime-valid certificate holds type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping.
  Common dependence statistics are marginal-bounded and can reverse an apparent ordering of conditions when the compared agents fail at different rates. Contracts, scoring code, analysis scripts, and the preregistration are released.

</details>


### [252] [Polish Medical Visual Question Answering: Vision-Language Models Underutilize Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12928)
*Jakub Pokrywka,Łukasz Grzybowski,Antoni Lasik,Marek Kubis,Jeremi Ignacy Kaczmarek,Wojciech Kusa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Polish medical VQA benchmark from licensure exam questions; evaluates VQA/QA with image-containing and text-only sets; best model ~79% accuracy; GPT-5.6 near human on candidate-response subset; images less impactful; answer-choice baselines perform above chance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable evaluation of non-English, domain-specific VQA systems in medicine; assess image vs text contributions and grounding in a realistic exam-like dataset.

Method: Construct dataset from Polish medical licensure exam questions with images; include a text-only QA control; evaluate Polish-oriented, open-weight, and commercial VLMs; perform ablations removing image/question/both; categorize questions by image importance; compare to human reference and candidate responses.

Result: Best VQA model = 79.0% accuracy on full set; GPT-5.6 approaches human on subset with candidates; most models below human; ablations show answer derives more from text; models achieve above-chance with answer choices even without key components.

Conclusion: Highlights limitations of current VLMs in specialized medical VQA, especially in non-English; emphasizes importance of visual grounding and domain adaptation; provides a benchmark framework for multilingual, domain-specific VQA evaluation.

Abstract: We introduce a Polish-language medical visual question answering (VQA) benchmark, built from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for licensed physicians and dentists pursuing specialist certification. The benchmark comprises image-containing questions spanning diverse medical specialties and visual domains, together with a text-only question answering (QA) control set. We evaluate Polish-oriented, general-purpose open-weight, and commercial vision-language models. The task remains challenging: the best model achieves 79.0\% accuracy on the full VQA set, and only GPT-5.6 surpasses the approximate human reference on the subset with available candidate responses; all other evaluated models perform worse than humans. To assess visual grounding, we compare complete inputs with configurations omitting the image, the question, or both, and categorize questions by image importance. Models derive more useful information from the question text than from the image and perform worse on image-dominant questions. Across both QA and VQA, they nevertheless achieve above-chance accuracy from the answer choices alone, showing that non-trivial performance can persist even when key task components are missing.

</details>


### [253] [FlashDrive: Flash Vision-Language-Action Inference for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12932)
*Zekai Li,Yihao Liang,Hongfei Zhang,Jian Chen,Yesheng Liang,Zhijian Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashDrive proposes a co-design framework to accelerate Vision-Language-Action models for autonomous driving by addressing four cascading bottlenecks with lightweight, stage-specific algorithms and CUDA Graphs, achieving substantial end-to-end latency reduction with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA inference in autonomous driving is bottlenecked by four cascading stages, making real-time control infeasible. A holistic, cross-stage optimization is needed to reduce compute while preserving decision quality.

Method: Per-stage lightweight shortcuts: (1) temporal overlap enabling streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; (2) non-autoregressive diffusion drafter exploiting low per-token entropy and intra-block correlations for speculative decoding; (3) adaptive step caching aligned with the velocity field’s end-points/flat middle; system-level integration via CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion; applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization.

Result: End-to-end latency reduced from 717 ms to 151 ms (4.7x) on a single GPU; end-to-end rate increased from 1.4 Hz to 6.6 Hz; accuracy largely preserved with minADE6@6.4s changing by 0.08 m and minADE1 improving; closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation.

Conclusion: Cross-stage algorithm-system co-design with lightweight per-stage shortcuts and low-level system optimizations can render large VLA models viable for real-time autonomous driving, with substantial latency reductions and negligible accuracy loss; the approach is demonstrated on Alpamayo 1.5-10B with quantization, suggesting potential generalization to similar multi-stage inference tasks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promise to bring end-to-end reasoning to autonomous driving, but their computational cost remains far too high for real-time control. The core challenge is structural: VLA inference is not a single bottleneck but a cascade of four. Visual encoding wastes compute on overlapping video frames; language-model prefill recomputes context that could be carried over from the previous timestep; reasoning tokens are generated serially despite low entropy; and flow-matching denoising applies uniform compute to a non-uniform velocity field. Addressing any one stage in isolation leaves the others untouched. We propose FlashDrive, an algorithm-system co-design framework that targets all four stages simultaneously. Our key insight is that each bottleneck admits a distinct, lightweight algorithmic shortcut: temporal overlap enables streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; the low per-token entropy and strong intra-block correlations of driving-domain reasoning make a non-autoregressive diffusion drafter highly effective for speculative decoding; and the velocity field's structure---sharp at the endpoints, flat in the middle---permits adaptive step caching that concentrates compute where it matters. Layered on system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion, these techniques compound. Applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization, FlashDrive reduces end-to-end latency from 717ms to 151ms (4.7x) while leaving accuracy essentially unchanged: minADE6@6.4s shifts by only 0.08m, minADE1 improves, and closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation. By raising a 10B-parameter reasoning VLA from 1.4~Hz to 6.6~Hz on a single GPU, FlashDrive moves end-to-end autonomous driving substantially closer to real-time deployment.

</details>


### [254] [Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, and Fragility in Perturbation Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12935)
*Lei You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DECAF decomposes perturbation responses into evidence (E), contradiction (C), and fragility (F) along progressively revealed inputs, preserving total magnitude; this improves interpretability over magnitude alone and enables efficient trajectory-based explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond using reaction magnitude as a proxy for interpretability by distinguishing whether a model's response supports, contradicts, or is fragile with respect to the final decision, across the input reveal path.

Method: Track predictions for paired inputs as the reveal path progresses, map responses to three components (E, C, F) with endpoint-relative axioms so that Abs = E + C + F and the decomposition is unique; evaluate across controlled vision and tabular tasks; compare against magnitude baselines; assess on large-scale models and inferences along forward-only trajectories.

Result: DECAF’s E, C, and F components align with independently measured behavior far more often than magnitude alone (e.g., 96.4% vs 35.0% in a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit). Modifying the reveal path can boost total response but largely preserves E while F grows; short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform general baselines; on a 1B-scale DINOv2, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with significantly reduced compute and memory.

Conclusion: DECAF provides an exact, endpoint-relative decomposition of perturbation responses, yielding interpretable evidence about model behavior and enabling efficient trajectory-based explanations for large models.

Abstract: Perturbation methods explain model decisions by measuring prediction changes under altered inputs, but response magnitude tells us only how much a model reacts, not what that reaction means. The same magnitude can support the final factual-counterfactual difference, oppose it, or arise strongly along the perturbation path yet vanish at the endpoint. We therefore track how the contrast develops as paired inputs are progressively revealed, using the final contrast to interpret the trajectory. We introduce DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility), which routes aligned, opposed, and endpoint-null responses into evidence E, contradiction C, and fragility F. The decomposition preserves ordinary magnitude exactly, Abs = E + C + F, and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Across controlled vision and tabular settings, the three components track independently measured behavior. In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, we compare cases with nearly identical response magnitude but different independently measured behaviors. The largest DECAF component agrees with an observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, compared with 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing only the reveal path increases total response by nearly 80%, yet evidence barely changes while fragility grows by more than 4x. On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform the tested general-purpose attribution baselines. On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

</details>


### [255] [Moose: Latent concept learning with reasoning-shortcut awareness in $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12961)
*Olga Mashkova,Asaad Mohammedsaleh,Fernando Zhapa-Camacho,Robert Hoehndorf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moose compiles an EL++ TBox and finite ABox into an SDD to enable differentiable reasoning with partial supervision over OWL EL, achieving formal guarantees and outperforming baselines on ontology-guided tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge neuro-symbolic learning with expressive ontologies (EL++ in OWL) under partial supervision and address lack of reasoning-shortcut analysis in ontology settings.

Method: Compile EL++ TBox/ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD) as a differentiable weighted-model-count layer; add closure clauses outside EL++ for exhaustive families; prove termination, soundness, completeness; validate proofs in Lean; define a formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over OWL EL; evaluate on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo.

Result: Formal guarantees (termination, soundness, completeness) with polynomial intermediate sizes; differentiable WMC layer implemented via SDD; first RS analysis in OWL EL; Moose outperforms propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology-embedding baselines on the tasks.

Conclusion: Presents the first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning framework over OWL EL, integrates reasoning-shortcuts into ontology-based neuro-symbolic learning, and paves the way for scalable, differentiable reasoning with large EL-based ontologies.

Abstract: The OWL 2 EL profile is used in some of the largest production ontologies, including the Gene Ontology and SNOMED CT. Existing neuro-symbolic (NeSy) learning methods accept propositional theories or Datalog, and reasoning-shortcut (RS) awareness has not been investigated in ontology settings. We present Moose, a method that compiles an $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ TBox and finite ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD). The SDD acts as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer, and we add closure clauses outside the $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ profile on declared exhaustive families to overcome the limited expressivity of $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ under partial supervision. We show termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes, and validate the proofs in Lean. We then define the first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over an OWL EL ontology, i.e., learning per-individual classifiers for latent concepts from observed ABox literals, and evaluate Moose on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo. Moose improves over propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology embedding baselines, and presents the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in an OWL EL setting.

</details>


### [256] [OGR-MARL: Option-Guided Residual Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Heterogeneous USV Cooperative Pursuit in Constrained Port Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12995)
*Mao Jiayang,Wang Lanfeng,Peng Zhao-Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An option-guided residual MARL framework (OGR-MARL) decoupled from specific MARL backbones for heterogeneous USV pursuit in constrained port waterways; shows improved capture and zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address navigation, traffic, and role constraints that complicate MARL in constrained port waterways; standard MARL struggles with constrained exploration and rule compliance; propose a modular framework that guides behavior with rules and learns residual policies on top.

Method: OGR-MARL integrates shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning; decoupled from a particular MARL algorithm; instantiated with MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, MASAC to yield OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, OGR-MASAC.

Result: In a Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario, OGR-MASAC achieves a 75% capture rate, demonstrating effective rule-compliant coordination; zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed map shows generalization potential.

Conclusion: OGR-MARL provides a flexible, generalizable framework that enables learning corrective actions on top of rule-guided policies, compatible with multiple MARL backbones and suitable for transfer to more complex port scenarios without retraining.

Abstract: Heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained port waterways requires evader interception under navigation, traffic, and role constraints. This paper proposes OGR-MARL, an option-guided residual multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that is decoupled from a specific MARL algorithm. OGR-MARL integrates shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning, allowing different MARL algorithms to learn corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors rather than exploring constrained port environments from scratch. We instantiate OGR-MARL with representative continuous-control MARL backbones, including MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC, yielding OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC. Experiments in an abstract Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario show that the OGR-MASAC instantiation achieves a 75.0% capture rate, promising mission-effective rule compliance, and the best heterogeneous coordination among the tested methods. Without retraining, zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map achieves promising results, demonstrating the generalization potential of OGR-MARL in more complex port scenarios.

</details>


### [257] [Foundations of MT-PDCL: Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13018)
*Costin Bădică,Amelia Bădică*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MT-PDCL generalizes probabilistic definite clause logic to continuous domains using measure-theoretic semantics; inference is exact via Lebesgue integration over continuous spaces, replacing grounding; introduces a continuous immediate consequence operator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome grounding bottlenecks of standard PDPCL frameworks restricted to finite domains and discrete distributions; enable native handling of continuous measurable spaces while preserving a declarative syntax.

Method: Define stochastic variables over bounded index domains; equip interpretation spaces with standard Borel σ-algebras; model rules as mutually independent causal events under Continuous Distribution Semantics; define entailment via exact Lebesgue integration; introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator to combine priors with observations; replace finite boolean grounding with exact continuous integration.

Result: Demonstrates exact, algebraic, and differentiable inference via continuous integration; eliminates discrete grounding bottleneck; achieves expressive power of continuous probabilistic models within a definite clause framework; acknowledges a shift to the geometric curse of dimensionality rather than combinatorial explosion.

Conclusion: Trade-off: replace discrete combinatorics with high-dimensional integration; preserves pure declarative syntax; promising for rich continuous probabilistic modeling; future work likely needed on tractability, approximation, and scalable integration.

Abstract: Standard probabilistic logic programming frameworks typically rely on grounding logic programs into discrete propositional representations. This operational requirement restricts exact inference to finite domains and discrete probability distributions. In this paper, we introduce Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic (MT-PDCL), a generalized foundational framework that eliminates this finite-domain restriction. By explicitly defining stochastic variables over bounded index domains and equipping the interpretation space with standard Borel $σ$-algebras, MT-PDCL allows logical variables to operate natively over continuous measurable spaces. Building on Continuous Distribution Semantics, MT-PDCL models probabilistic rules as mutually independent causal events. However, rather than aggregating these derivations via finite boolean circuits, declarative entailment is formally defined through exact Lebesgue integration over the continuous measure space. We introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that unifies the integration of continuous prior distributions with the evaluation of exact continuous observations. We demonstrate that this approach replaces the combinatorial bottleneck of discrete grounding with exact, algebraic, and structurally differentiable inference. While this transition trades discrete combinatorics for the geometric curse of dimensionality, it achieves the expressive power of continuous probabilistic models while preserving the pure declarative syntax of definite clause logic.

</details>


### [258] [From Local Mismatch to Global Impact: Optimizing Cache Reuse Policy for Efficient Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13043)
*Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Zhikang Xie,Xiangyu Yue,Cheng Jin,Weizhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Global-Impact Cache (GCache) optimizes diffusion-model caching via a bilevel optimization framework with a Bernstein-parameterized error-propagation bound, achieving notable speedups and quality gains over prior cache strategies (e.g., 2.17x speedup on Wan2.1 with LPIPS 0.0316).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cache policies rely on local similarity heuristics that poorly reflect their impact on final generation quality due to non-uniform error propagation along the denoising trajectory.

Method: Derive an error-propagation upper bound; reparameterize the propagation exponent with Bernstein form; formulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization where the inner problem selects the optimal reuse policy and the outer problem aligns the error-weighting function with generation-quality loss, training to prioritize computation that most affects fidelity.

Result: GCache outperforms prior caching methods on both image and video generation, delivering a 2.17x speedup on Wan2.1 with LPIPS improving from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

Conclusion: The framework successfully reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, learning to allocate computation to where it most improves visual fidelity in diffusion-based generation.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved dominant performance in visual generation but suffer from substantial inference overhead. While cache-based acceleration has emerged as a promising solution, existing policies rely on local similarity heuristics, which we identify as being significantly misaligned with final generation quality. This discrepancy stems from the non-uniform propagation and accumulation of errors along the denoising trajectory. To address this, we propose Global-Impact Cache (GCache). We first establish a rigorous theoretical characterization of the error propagation upper bound. Recognizing that this bound can be overly conservative for complex, highly non-convex diffusion models, we further reparameterize the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form and reformulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization problem. In detail, GCache identifies an optimal reuse policy in the inner objective while aligning the error-weighting function with generation quality loss in the outer objective. This framework effectively reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, learning to prioritize computation where it most impacts visual fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation. Notably, on the state-of-the-art Wan2.1 video diffusion model, GCache maintains a 2.17x speedup while significantly enhancing generation quality, reducing LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

</details>


### [259] [BoardroomAI: Dependency-Aware Human-Steerable Multi-Agent Deliberation through Evolving Decision Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13046)
*Sanjeev Manivannan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Human-in-the-loop decision graph enabling persistent intervention with dependency-aware propagation; efficiency gains in simulation but context sufficiency remains a challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address shortcomings of transcript-based multi-agent systems by treating humans as persistent participants who can intervene, aiming to improve decision quality while reducing recomputation.

Method: Introduce a typed decision graph (decision-DAG) encoding evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; an intervention compiler to update the graph; dependency-aware propagation to identify affected subgraphs and selectively reactivate specialists; and an evaluation framework for intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Evaluated on 600 synthetic interventions and a 12-case pilot.

Result: Propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes; in the pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in 6 of 12 cases while abstaining in the other 6, indicating correct routing can still lack sufficient context for synthesis.

Conclusion: Prototype-level results demonstrate potential for human–agent coexistence in deliberation; introduces the notion of decision-sufficient context closure; further work needed on routing decisions with adequate contextual support for synthesis.

Abstract: Organizational decisions are co-created while evidence, constraints, and human priorities continue to evolve. In conventional transcript-based multi-agent systems, humans typically provide an initial problem, agents deliberate internally, and the system returns a final response. BoardroomAI instead treats the human as a persistent participant who can intervene by challenging assumptions, modifying constraints, changing priorities, introducing evidence, or redirecting the decision process. We operationalize this human--agent coexistence through four components: (i) a typed decision graph representing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; (ii) an intervention compiler that converts confirmed human actions into explicit graph updates; (iii) dependency-aware propagation that identifies affected subgraphs, preserves unaffected artifacts, and selectively reactivates relevant specialists; and (iv) an evaluation framework measuring intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Across 600 generated decision-DAG interventions, propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In a 12-case exploratory pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in the remaining six. These abstentions show that correct intervention routing may still provide insufficient context for synthesis, motivating a \emph{decision-sufficient context closure} for human-steered multi-agent deliberation. All results are synthetic and prototype-level.

</details>


### [260] [DMDIntel: Interpreting Large Language Models via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048)
*Amogh Joshi,Animesh Mukherjee,Sergey Utyuzhnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DMDIntel applies dynamic mode decomposition to LLM hidden states to produce interpretable token attributions for classification, outperforming PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for robust and accurate input attributions for LLM-based classification; existing attribution methods may struggle to capture dynamic and distributed hidden-state structure.

Method: Apply dynamic mode decomposition to decompose LLM hidden states into dominant modes; derive input token rankings by projecting token representations onto the modes; build an input attribution pipeline and evaluate across datasets and model families.

Result: Experiments on three datasets and three model families show that the ranked attributions produced by DMDIntel outperform state-of-the-art baselines such as PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP.

Conclusion: Dynamic mode decomposition offers an effective framework for interpretable, token-level attribution in LLM classification, achieving superior performance over several established attribution methods.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce DMDIntel which uses dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to make the predictions made by LLMs in a classification task interpretable. It develops an input attribution pipeline, that first decomposes the hidden states of an LLM into prominent patterns, also known as modes, and then associates ranks to the input tokens based on the projection values on those modes. Rigorous experiments across three datasets and three model families consistently show that the ranked attribution of input tokens obtained using DMDIntel by far outperforms state-of-the-art techniques such as principal component analysis, integrated gradients and SHAP.

</details>


### [261] [VALG: An Agentic System for ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060)
*Dechen Zhang,Xuan Tang,Xinxiang Yin,Xingwu Chen,Jian Qian,Difan Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VALG proposes an autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research, combining verification, adaptive formulation, and graph-based proofs to manage theorem development and routing of obstacles; evaluated on COLT 2026 open problems with mixed success; open source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organize ML theory research into an autonomous, traceable workflow that can simultaneously handle problem formulation, proof structure, and theorem targets; address iterative refining of assumptions and proofs.

Method: Multi-level VALG system with (1) Verification: check theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph; (2) Adaptive formulation: modify learning-theory problems in response to obstructions; (3) Graph-structured proof development: build and review local proofs in dependency order; obstacle diagnosis; source-relative theorem branches; fixed specification within branch; formulation-level obstructions trigger related relaxations preserving relation.

Result: Applied to nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems; two runs yielded internally finalized theorem candidates; seven yielded restricted-method, special-case, or conditional theorems; demonstrates clear separation between source-scope, relaxations, and blocked attempts.

Conclusion: VALG demonstrates feasibility of fully autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research, with mathematically distinct handling of scope matches, relaxations, and failed attempts; open-source at https://github.com/DechenZhang/VALG-ML-Theory-Agent.

Abstract: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups in which the data model, training protocol, oracle access, loss, metric, and randomness define the phenomenon that a theorem is meant to explain. Solving an open problem therefore requires the problem formulation, theorem target, and proof mechanism to be developed in concert. Researchers formulate hypotheses, test them through preliminary theoretical or empirical analysis, and refine both assumptions and proofs. We investigate whether this process can be organized as an autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research.
  We develop VALG, an agentic system that combines multi-level Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. Within each source-relative theorem branch, VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification, checks the theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG identifies whether the obstruction lies in a derivation, the proof structure, or the theorem formulation and routes the next attempt accordingly. Formulation-level obstructions initiate an explicitly related variant or relaxation, preserving the mathematical relation between the resulting theorem and the source problem.
  We evaluate VALG on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs produce internally finalized theorem candidates that match the scope of their source briefs; the remaining seven yield restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. These case studies show how VALG keeps source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts mathematically distinct. VALG is open source at https://github.com/DechenZhang/VALG-ML-Theory-Agent.

</details>


### [262] [Uniform Herding: Exemplar Replay with Representation Refresh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13061)
*Krishna Subedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Uniform Herding improves replay-based class-incremental learning under a bounded exemplar setting by uniformly distributing the active exemplars across observed classes and refreshing them via a bounded candidate pool, achieving higher final accuracy and lower forgetting than iCaRL on CIFAR-100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of preserving earlier classes when feature representations drift and only a bounded active exemplar set can be replayed; aim to refresh exemplars within a constrained candidate pool to maintain performance across increments.

Method: Uniform Herding allocates the current active set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh chosen exemplars in the current representation; evaluated with ResNet-18 on CIFAR-100 under 10 incremental tasks with active budget M=2000 and retrieval budget b=64, across three seeds.

Result: Final average accuracy: 44.00% ±0.51% (Uniform Herding) vs 42.33% ±1.20% (iCaRL); forgetting: 17.22% ±0.43% vs 24.87% ±1.11%. Retrieval budget variations have smaller impact than active budget changes. Within Uniform Herding, replacing NME or herding lowers accuracy, removing distillation increases forgetting; end-to-end comparison vs iCaRL; results limited to the tested protocol.

Conclusion: Uniform Herding yields stronger end-to-end performance than iCaRL within the tested protocol and clarifies the effect of exemplar refresh. However, findings are protocol-specific and may not generalize beyond the tested setup.

Abstract: As the feature representation changes, replay must preserve the earlier classes. However, only a bounded active exemplar set can be replayed. We propose Uniform Herding, which allocates the current active set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh their chosen exemplars in the current representation. On CIFAR-100 with ten class-incremental tasks, a ResNet-18 backbone, active budget $M=2{,}000$, retrieval budget $b=64$, and three seeds, Uniform Herding obtains $44.00\pm0.51\%$ final average accuracy and $17.22\pm0.43\%$ forgetting, compared with $42.33\pm1.20\%$ and $24.87\pm1.11\%$ for iCaRL. Within the Uniform Herding protocol, final accuracy decreased when NME or herding was replaced with the tested alternatives, while forgetting increased when distillation was removed. Changing the retrieval budget has a smaller effect across the tested range than changing the active budget. The comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end. It does not isolate the effect of refresh from the other protocol differences. These results are limited to the tested protocol.

</details>


### [263] [Explanatory Engagement Under Rare Anomalous Failure: Asymptotic Rarity in Model Behavior (or: The Asymptotic AI)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13063)
*Sam Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Explanatory engagement by LLMs does not universally rise as failure rates in a workflow become rarer; observed patterns depend on elicitation design, with some settings showing a rise-then-plateau, others showing flattening or floor artifacts, and model-specific self-monitoring emerging under certain conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether a low, controllable failure rate in LLM-driven workflows provokes increasing, more precise, or more confident explanations, or whether engagement collapses when failures become rare, and to identify how prompt design shapes this behavior.

Method: Three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) were evaluated on a repeated tool-call task where one call fails with probability p. Eight failure rates (0.2 to 0.0001) were tested under five elicitation conditions ranging from immediate prompting to no prompting, plus a companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells) to assess anomaly recognition. Metrics analyzed included explanation length and self-reported confidence, with analyses stratified by condition to reveal how elicitation moderates the presence or absence of an observed collapse.

Result: Across aggregated data, explanatory length did not monotonically increase as failures became rarer. Condition-level analyses showed: immediate_forced prompts yield a rise in engagement with length peaking at 28.4 words at p=0.05 and settling to 17.4–19.0 words at the rarest rates, with confidence rising from ~53% to the 70s–90s. grouped_runs produced no collapse as explanations were batched at run end. passive_unprompted produced a floor artifact in aggregate but revealed real self-monitoring in llama3.1:8b (structured confidence reports) and, to a lesser extent, in the other models (boilerplate). Elicitation structure thus acts as a primary moderator of collapse observability. The guaranteed-failure run showed model differences in anomaly recognition distinct from engagement. Limitations include discrete rate points limiting inference about continuous behavior; results motivate future work on intermediate rates and broader elicitation schemes.

Conclusion: The hypothesized universal collapse of explanatory engagement with rarer failures is not supported. Observed patterns depend on elicitation design and model, with evidence for model-specific self-monitoring under certain conditions; elicitation structure is a key moderator, and discrete-rate sampling limits interpretations—further work should explore continuous-rate regimes and additional prompts.

Abstract: Prior work on LLM behavior under anomalous conditions asks whether a model notices anomalies. We ask a narrower question: once a model sits in a workflow with a low, controllable failure rate, does its explanatory engagement - length, specificity, self-reported confidence - change as failure grows asymptotically rarer? We built a local, zero-cost harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) running a repeated tool-call task where one call fails at probability p, swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions from immediate prompting to none. We hypothesized a rise in engagement as failures grew rarer, then a collapse near a detectability threshold. Pooled across conditions this appeared false: length fell in a flat, monotonic pattern. Splitting by condition overturned that. Under immediate_forced, where the model must explain every failure instantly, the predicted rise is confirmed but followed by a plateau, not a collapse: length peaks at 28.4 words at p=0.05, settles to 17.4-19.0 words at the rarest rates, and confidence rises unevenly from about 53% to the 70s-90s. Under grouped_runs, explanation batched to run-end, no collapse appears. Under passive_unprompted, aggregate magnitude is a floor artifact, but a recovered logging gap revealed real, model-specific self-monitoring: llama3.1:8b volunteers structured confidence reports unprompted, sometimes eroding its own confidence as trials accumulate; the other two do so only once, as boilerplate. Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of collapse observability. A companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells, backfilling rates where random sampling gave zero real failures) shows models differ in whether they recognize an anomaly, distinct from engagement once recognized. Limitation: discrete rate points cannot capture behavior between them, a direction for future work.

</details>


### [264] [Behavioral Reprogramming of Open-Weights Models: Cognitive Plasticity and Alignment Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13069)
*Lucia Malíčková*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes proactive, Socratic LLM behavior through constrained, compute-efficient PEFT, identifying LoRA rank threshold (r=16) and optimal training window (e in [2,3]); scaling to 14B parameters reduces perplexity (1.414); DPO used to separate assertive behavior from syntax; demonstrates cross-lingual persona transfer with robust alignment in related languages but degradation in morphologically distant targets; provides an empirical framework for cross-lingual, compute-efficient behavioral modification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the default passive, sycophantic alignment of LLMs by inducing a proactive, question-generating Socratic conversational style under tight HPC constraints, exploiting parameter-efficient fine-tuning and large-scale hyperparameter search to map the limits of behavioral reprogramming and cross-lingual transfer.

Method: Massively parallel hyperparameter sweep across 405 HPC jobs to characterize PEFT bounds; identify LoRA rank threshold r=16; perform epoch ablation to find optimal training window e in [2,3] under dataset density affecting validation loss; scale model to 14B params for perplexity improvements; apply Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to separate enforceable behavior from local syntax; conduct cross-lingual, zero-shot tests to assess persona transfer and alignment across linguistically related and morphologically distant languages.

Result: Found that generalization converges optimally within a tight training window; 14B parameter scale yields lower localized perplexity (1.414); DPO decouples assertive behavior from syntax; cross-lingual tests show robust alignment within related language families and identifiable degradation in morphologically distant targets; establishes a framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

Conclusion: The paper provides empirical bounds for PEFT-based behavioral reprogramming of LLMs, demonstrates a practical R&D path for enabling proactive dialogue while preserving cross-lingual alignment properties, and highlights both capabilities and limitations under tight HPC constraints and across linguistic distances.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are predominantly aligned to function as passive, sycophantic assistants. We challenge this default paradigm by empirically evaluating the cognitive plasticity of open-weight architectures when subjected to rigorous behavioral reprogramming. Our objective is to induce a proactive, Socratic conversational framework, characterized by high-frequency question generation under strictly constrained high-performance computing (HPC) conditions. Through a massively parallelized hyperparameter sweep comprising 405 HPC jobs, we define precise mathematical bounds for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). We identify an architectural threshold at LoRA rank $r=16$ and demonstrate via extensive epoch ablation that generalization capacity strictly reaches its optimal convergence within an optimized training window of $e \in [2, 3]$ depending on dataset density (minimum validation loss of 0.919). Furthermore, scaling model capacity to 14B parameters yielded a lower localized evaluation perplexity (1.414). Subsequent Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) successfully decoupled the underlying assertive behavior from localized syntax, while rigorous cross-lingual stress testing reveals both the capabilities and the structural boundaries of zero-shot persona transfer, demonstrating robust alignment in closely related linguistic families alongside identifiable degradation pathways in morphologically distant targets. These findings establish a rigorous empirical framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

</details>


### [265] [EEG-PRIME: Prototype-Aligned Representation Learning with Multi-Level Conditioning for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13072)
*Shuailei Zhang,Muyun Jiang,Wei Zhang,Jinbo Chen,Zhiwei Guo,Yong Li,Yi Ding,Cuntai Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-stage EEG foundation model (EEG-PRIME) enabling cross-dataset, cross-task decoding via masked pretraining and prototype-aligned instruction tuning; demonstrates strong cross-subject/zero-shot transfer across 16 datasets and held-out sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain shifts in EEG across acquisition protocols and individuals lead to poor generalization; a foundation-model-style approach with targeted conditioning can achieve subject-invariant, task-agnostic decoding.

Method: Pretraining: EEG encoder trained via masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. Instruction tuning: conditioning for task semantics, dataset, subject invariance; Q-Former modulated by conditioning; frozen label embeddings serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity predictions across heterogeneous label spaces.

Result: Outperforms SOTA baselines and prior EEG foundation models across 16 datasets (MI, emotion, ADHD, covert speech, workload). On two held-out datasets, achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration without target-domain optimization, indicating strong zero-shot capability.

Conclusion: EEG-PRIME achieves instruction-aware, subject-invariant cross-dataset decoding, offering a practical zero-shot pathway for EEG decoding across diverse paradigms.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) decoding models often generalize poorly across datasets and subjects due to domain shifts in acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology. We propose EEG-PRIME, a two-stage EEG foundation model for cross-dataset multi-task decoding. EEG-PRIME combines masked pretraining with prototype-aligned instruction tuning to enable instruction-aware and subject-invariant decoding across diverse BCI paradigms. During pretraining, an EEG encoder learns transferable representations through masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. During instruction tuning, EEG-PRIME incorporates task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning. The resulting conditioning signal modulates the Q-Former through Layer-wise Query Modulation, while frozen text embeddings of class labels serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces. Experiments on sixteen datasets covering motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, and mental workload show consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models under cross-subject settings. On two additional held-out datasets, EEG-PRIME achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing, demonstrating promising zero-shot transfer capability.

</details>


### [266] [SPADE: Speculative Decoding for Precise and Low Cost Distributed Edge Cloud Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13076)
*Divya Jyoti Bajpai,Kishan Kumar Upadhyay,Manjesh Kumar Hanawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distributed speculative decoding across edge and cloud uses a compact edge draft model to propose tokens and a large cloud verifier to validate them in parallel, cutting cloud calls by 76% with no accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable cost-efficient, scalable deployment of large language models by shifting computation toward the edge while preserving the accuracy of the big model and avoiding retraining.

Method: Edge draft model rapidly generates candidate tokens; a large verifier model on the cloud validates tokens in parallel; accepted tokens are kept, while rejections trigger verifier correction; no retraining required; plug-and-play integration.

Result: Reduces cloud model calls by 76% with zero loss in accuracy compared to the full model; demonstrated on multiple NLP tasks using SpecBench and CNN/DailyMail datasets.

Conclusion: Presents a practical, scalable, and cost-efficient approach for deploying accurate LLMs in real-world environments without retraining.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in natural language understanding and generation, but their deployment is constrained by high computational demands. Deploying smaller LLMs directly on the edge can circumvent this, but with degraded accuracy. Deploying smaller cloud-based big LLMs preserves performance, but at the cost of expensive per-token computation. We present a distributed inference framework, \our{}, that integrates speculative decoding (SD) across edge and cloud. A compact draft model deployed on the edge generates candidate tokens rapidly, and a large verifier model on the cloud validates these tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained, while only rejections trigger verifier correction, substantially reducing the number of cloud queries. Our plug-and-play design shifts the bulk of computation to the edge, significantly lowers inference time and cloud cost, and preserves the accuracy of the big model without any retraining requirement. Our approach demonstrates a practical path toward scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate deployment of LLMs in real-world environments. Experimental results across multiple Natural Language Processing tasks using SpecBench and CNN/Dailymail datasets demonstrate that \our{} reduces the cloud model calls by $76\%$ with zero loss in accuracy as compared to the full model.

</details>


### [267] [Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging: A Semantic Superposition and Contextual Lamination Framework for Malpractice-Resilient Online Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13100)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,Kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sri Ram,Parani Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MCCT extends MSCCM by a Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory that hides assessment content via semantic superposition, with six operators, a conditional-entropy ambiguity measure, and an exact recovery guarantee for legitimate users; includes a rendering algorithm and evaluation protocol to enable resilient, accessible online assessments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To counter content-extraction attacks on online assessments (screenshots, screen-sharing, OCR, scraping) while preserving readability for legitimate candidates.

Method: Defines MCCT with Context Inversion, Lamination, Separation Channel, and Reading/Ambiguity Functionals; models extraction as an explicit channel; uses a Context Camouflage Tensor; derives a conditional-entropy based ambiguity measure; provides an exact filtering identity guaranteeing legitimate recovery; analyzes properties and presents a rendering algorithm with complexity and a pre-registered evaluation protocol.

Result: A theoretical framework yielding a closed-form measure of ambiguity, guarantees of legitimate content recovery, and formal properties on camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing; includes an implementable rendering algorithm and evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: MCCT offers a rigorous foundation for resilient, accessible digital assessment by camouflaging rendered content while preserving readability for legitimate users; practical deployment will require empirical validation and consideration of computational/ usability trade-offs.

Abstract: Contemporary online assessment systems rely primarily on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioural analytics, yet remain vulnerable to attacks that extract the assessment content itself through screenshots, screen sharing, optical character recognition, and automated scraping. This paper extends the Multi-dimensional Spatio-Temporal Context Camouflaging Model (MSCCM) within the MARS (Multi-modal Assessment Resilience Suite) by introducing the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT), a mathematical framework that protects rendered assessment content through semantic superposition. Authentic assessment content and synthetically generated camouflage are represented as a unified rendering while remaining recoverable only by legitimate candidates. The framework models the adversarial extraction process through an explicit extraction-channel operator and develops six coupled constructs: the Context Inversion Operator, Contextual Lamination Operator, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, and Context Camouflage Tensor. Computational ambiguity is formulated using conditional entropy, yielding a closed-form expression that quantifies uncertainty during unauthorized extraction, while legitimate recovery is guaranteed through an exact filtering identity. We further establish theoretical properties governing ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing, and present a rendering algorithm with computational complexity and a pre-registered evaluation protocol. MCCT provides a mathematically rigorous foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally resilient digital assessment by securing rendered assessment content while preserving readability for legitimate users.

</details>


### [268] [Robust Dempster-Shafer Evidence Fusion with Chaos-Conflict Measurement and Historical-Experience Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13108)
*Huiyu Li,Weibo Liu,Xinru Xu,Dongchen Gao,Meng Zhang,Junhua Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified evidence reasoning framework for multi-source fusion within Dempster-Shafer theory that jointly measures cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, leverages historical reliability via spectral clustering and regret theory, uses a hybrid combination rule, and a belief-interval decision strategy; validated on 16 datasets with improved metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address two limitations: (1) separate handling of inter-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty, and (2) lack of long-term reliability consideration in DST evidence fusion.

Method: Introduce chaos-conflict measurement with five properties; historical experience driven weighting via spectral clustering and regret theory to derive reliability; develop a hybrid combination rule balancing uncertainty preservation and consensus aligned with global conflict; adopt belief-interval decision strategy.

Result: Average F1 85.78 and mean AUC 93.30; outperforms eight DST baselines and three gradient boosting methods; ablation analysis confirms the contributions.

Conclusion: Provides an adaptive evidence fusion framework for multi-source decision making under epistemic uncertainty, with robust classification and improved evaluation metrics.

Abstract: Multi-source evidence fusion under Dempster-Shafer theory faces two persistent challenges: existing conflict measures assess inter-evidence inconsistency and intra-evidence uncertainty independently, yielding incomplete evaluations, and current fusion methods evaluate evidence sources exclusively through instantaneous comparisns without exploiting their long-term reliability across diverse decision contexts. This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that addresses both limitations. Specifically, a chaos-conflict measurement is introduced to jointly quantify cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, with five formally proven properties ensuring consistent assessment. A historical experience driven weighting scheme partitions the decision space via spectral clustering and applies regret theory to compute context-specific reliability profiles from past fusion outcomes. These mechanisms feed into a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level, followed by a belief-interval decision strategy that enables robust classification without discarding epistemic uncertainty. Experiments on 16 real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an average F1 score of 85.78 and a mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods. Ablation analysis confirms the contribution of each component we proposed. The framework offers an effective approach for adaptive evidence fusion in multi-source decision making.

</details>


### [269] [SkillEvo: Self-Renewing Evolution Gradients from Multi-Turn Interaction Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120)
*Qianxi Yan,Chunrong Chen,Jiuzhou Zhao,Min Zhang,Yongzhou Xu,Xiaochuan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillEvo engineer a closed-loop, gradient-based skill evolution for LLMs by coupling a multi-turn feedback generator with an independent governance layer. It aims to overcome decay of evolution signals in single-turn or end-to-end evaluation setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches either craft skills by single-shot generation or rely on end-to-end evaluations, which provide weak, diminishing feedback and fail to localize or repair underlying deficiencies. This stalls skill evolution as problems surface only across extended interactions.

Method: Two components: (1) A feedback generator that uses multi-turn user simulation to surface defects layer by layer; each revision both consumes feedback and yields new feedback. (2) An independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing gradient drift.

Result: SkillEvo outperforms baseline approaches, achieving 23.0-point gains over self-reflection-based evolution and 15.4-point gains over single-turn QA-driven evolution. Evaluation spans six cloud-service categories, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files.

Conclusion: Trustworthy feedback gradients combined with proactive governance enable sustained, scalable skill evolution beyond prior QA-based waves, suggesting a robust framework for continuous improvement of AI skills.

Abstract: Agent Skills are today either hand-authored or produced in a single LLM generation pass, and consequently possess no closed loop through which they might improve from the interaction failures they actually cause. Recent work does close this loop, but derives its feedback from single-turn question-answering evaluation. The consequence is a sharp asymmetry: once the first round has patched the gaps that a single exchange can reveal, the evolution gradient decays, the defects that surface only across multiple turns remain invisible, and evolution stalls. Governance in these systems is likewise driven by an end-to-end verification score, a scalar gate that can reject a degraded candidate but can neither localize nor repair its structural cause. We argue that the binding constraint on sustained skill evolution is neither editing capability nor the number of iterations, but whether the evaluation feedback keeps supplying trustworthy evolution gradients. We introduce SkillEvo, in which trustworthy feedback generates the gradient and controllable governance constrains its direction. The first component recasts multi-turn user simulation from an evaluation endpoint into a feedback generator: follow-up questions expose defects layer by layer, so that every round of revision both consumes feedback and produces new feedback. The second replaces the passive rejection of a scalar gate with an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing the gradient from drifting as degradation accumulates. Across six categories of cloud services, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single- turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

</details>


### [270] [Numeracy in Large Language Models: Fundamental Limitations and Paths to Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13129)
*Aoxin Ni*

Main category: cs.AI
