TL;DR: A survey and framework for numerical grounding in LLMs, introducing NGF (Representational and Procedural Grounding), organizing benchmarks and failure modes, evaluating across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, GSM-Symbolic, and discussing architectural interventions and deployment implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel at high-level reasoning but struggle with elementary numeracy; there is a need to distinguish and diagnose numerical understanding separately from broader mathematical reasoning.

Method: Propose Numerical Grounding Framework (RG and PG), categorize numeracy tasks; review diagnostic benchmarks and failure modes; analyze tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining data; conduct coordinated evaluation across three frontier model families on three benchmarks; assess architectural interventions such as digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings; discuss deployment strategies.

Result: NGF provides a structured lens to diagnose numeracy gaps; organizing benchmarks and failure modes; empirical signals that digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings help models trained from scratch; pretrained models benefit more from supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools; practical deployment recommendations and pathways for research directions.

Conclusion: More reliable numerical behavior in foundation models requires targeted architectural interventions, better data distributions, and practical tooling; NGF offers a clear research roadmap and evaluation framework for numeracy in LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks yet remain unreliable on elementary numerical tasks, including magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, and scientific notation. This survey examines basic numerical understanding as a capability distinct from high-level mathematical reasoning. We propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF), which decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (RG), mapping numeral forms to value, magnitude, and equivalent representations, and Procedural Grounding (PG), executing arithmetic operations in accordance with their mathematical definitions. Using NGF, we organize recent diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, structural explanations, and mitigation strategies. We review evidence concerning tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining-data distribution. We also apply NGF in a coordinated evaluation of three frontier model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, comparing atomic, contextual, and reasoning-assisted numeracy. Architectural interventions such as digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings can improve models trained from scratch but are generally unavailable to users of pretrained systems, for whom supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools are more practical. We conclude with deployment recommendations and research directions for more reliable numerical behavior in foundation models.

</details>


### [271] [Rethinking Normalization Placement for LLMs: Post-Norm under Curriculum Depth Growing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13156)
*Sheng Ren,Yadong Wang,Naiqiang Tan,Jiangang Kong,Jun Fang,Rui Liu,Jun Wang,Kai Chen,Lipeng Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Curriculum-depth growth alters the relationship between normalization placement (pre- vs post-norm) in transformers. In joint training, pre- and post-norm perform nearly identically; but with curriculum growth (appending blocks), post-norm significantly outperforms pre-norm, suggesting boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks. The results imply normalization placement and depth-curriculum are coupled design choices in distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the canonical pre-norm preference in Transformers persists when depth is introduced via a curriculum, and whether normalization placement interacts with forward conditioning introduced by appended blocks during the curriculum.

Method: Controlled distillation with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, comparing pre-norm and post-norm under two regimes: joint training and curriculum depth growth. Multiple controls (post-joint, single-block, freeze) and diagnostics (residual-scale stability, structural-token scale drift, identity-mapped final pre-grow block) to localize effects.

Result: Under joint training, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable (validation CE diff ≈ 0.0004). Under curriculum growth, post-norm improves over pre-norm by 0.0328 CE, an order-of-magnitude larger. Post-joint control with active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, ruling out compute alone. The ranking crossover occurs during curriculum: post-norm leads after blocks appended. Single-block and freeze controls localize the change to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics link post-norm to stable residual scales and pre-norm to drift in structural-token scales; final pre-grow block is nearly identity-mapped. These support boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended.

Conclusion: Normalization placement and the training curriculum are coupled design choices in distillation with appended blocks. Considering them together yields better guidance for optimizing forward conditioning and performance in deep transformers.

Abstract: Pre-norm is the standard normalization placement in modern Transformers because it facilitates joint optimization of full-depth models. We ask whether this preference persists when depth is introduced through a curriculum. In curriculum depth growth, each appended block receives the boundary representation produced by a trained prefix, making normalization placement relevant to forward conditioning. We therefore test whether placement and training curriculum interact. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable under joint training, differing by $0.0004$ validation CE, while post-norm improves over pre-norm by $0.0328$ under curriculum growth, an order of magnitude larger. A post-joint control matched by student active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, which rules out compute as the sole explanation. The ranking crosses over during the curriculum: post-norm takes the lead once blocks are appended. Single-block and freeze controls localize the ranking change to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics associate post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with structural-token scale drift; on a fixed batch, the final pre-grow block is also nearly identity-mapped. Together with the phase-wise crossover, these observations are consistent with boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended. The results motivate treating normalization placement and training curriculum as coupled design choices in this distillation setting.

</details>


### [272] [SkillShapley: Boundary-Adaptive Shapley Valuation for Skill Step Attribution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13173)
*Chang Liu,Yuqi Zhang,Yiman Zhong,Boyi Liu,Hengjun Wang,Shuyue Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillShapley is a Shapley-value-based framework to attribute the contribution of individual steps within agent skills, addressing the lack of step-level diagnostics in task-oriented skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how each step in an agent skill contributes to overall performance, overcoming the reliance on manual crafting and execution traces.

Method: A two-phase, Shapley-value-based attribution approach: (1) identify informative coalitional regions using discretized benchmark rewards to create performance cliffs, (2) adaptively sample coalitions to gather reusable marginal evidence, exploiting mostly additive step interactions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on SkillsBench shows SkillShapley effectively and efficiently identifies high- and low-value steps, yielding actionable insights for agent skill creation.

Conclusion: SkillShapley provides a principled, scalable method for step-level attribution in agent skills, enabling better design and refinement of procedural tasks by highlighting which steps drive performance.

Abstract: Agent skills are crucial external instructions that enable language agents to execute long procedural tasks such as coding or document processing. Existing agent skills are primarily created through human manual crafting or agent execution traces, with limited understanding of how each step contributes to overall skill performance on specific tasks; i.e., there remains an open problem in quantifying the contribution of individual steps within an agent skill. To address this issue, we first model skill-step attribution as a Shapley value-based contribution estimation problem, and then propose SkillShapley, a step-level attribution framework for agent skills. Notably, SkillShapley operates in two phases, motivated by key empirical insights, i.e., discretized benchmark rewards that create sharp performance cliffs, and step interactions that are largely additive rather than synergistic. Specifically, it first identifies informative coalitional regions, and then adaptively samples new coalitions that can yield reusable marginal evidence. Experiments on skills from the widely adopted SkillsBench demonstrate that our SkillShapley can effectively and efficiently identify high- or low-value skill steps, providing several key takeaways for agent skill creation.

</details>


### [273] [Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Bounded Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179)
*Zechuan Wang,Siyuan Lu,Hongxuan Zhang,Linjian Mo,Chenyi Zhuang,Leilei Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrEST is a hierarchical credit-assignment framework that combines verifier-bounded RL with dense token-level supervision via a privileged self-teacher. It uses turn-segmented verified advantages to address inter-turn dilution and entropy-gated self-teacher modulation to refine intra-turn token contributions, improving long-trajectory and session-level metrics over RL and distillation baselines on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench, while preserving the verifier-bounded performance ceiling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR offers a verifier-bounded ceiling but conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single trajectory-level reward, hindering fine-grained credit assignment. On-policy distillation provides dense token-level supervision but risks teacher-boundedness or gradient collapse. There is a need for dense credit assignment that keeps the verifier-bounded constraint.

Method: CrEST introduces a two-level credit-assignment mechanism. (1) Turn-segmented verified advantages split long trajectories into turns and assign verified advantages per segment to mitigate inter-turn dilution. (2) Entropy-gated self-teacher modulation uses a privileged self-teacher to modulate update magnitudes at the token level, enabling dense credit signals without dictating update directions. The framework preserves the verifier-bounded ceiling while delivering dense token-level feedback.

Result: Empirical results on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show CrEST consistently surpasses RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long trajectories and strict session-level metrics.

Conclusion: CrEST demonstrates that a teacher’s role can shift from guiding update directions to modulating update magnitudes, enabling dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded performance ceiling.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) offers a verifier-bounded performance ceiling for training multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but is either teacher-bounded or prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that retains RL's verifier-bounded ceiling while incorporating dense token-level signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that $\textbf{CrEST}$ consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics. Our work demonstrates that the teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining update directions to modulating update magnitudes, unlocking dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling.

</details>


### [274] [TsuGO: Probing Search Efficiency in LLM Reasoning via Go Life-and-Death Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13221)
*Shunwen Bai,Ziping Ma,Chaoyang Zhang,Yarong Wang,Jiale Liu,Zhen Qin,Qingpei Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new process-level benchmark, TsuGO, evaluates Search Efficiency in LLM reasoning using structured Go life-and-death problems; results show current LLMs struggle with search organization, with limited gains from longer chains or higher token use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing process-level evaluations emphasize coherence of chain-of-thought or static capabilities, but miss how models plan, allocate reasoning resources, and organize search. TsuGO aims to disentangle domain knowledge from search strategy in a controlled, adversarial task.

Method: Introduce closed, verifiable solution spaces (tsumego-like Go problems) with adversarial structure. Parse CoT into a structured search-tree representation. Measure candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking. Report metrics such as Search Efficiency and Token Efficiency, plus diagnostics and visualizations.

Result: Stronger models solve tsumego-like tasks by finding the correct candidate earlier and maintaining productive branches; many models behave like unguided search and do not reach neural-guided performance. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not guarantee better search. Current LLMs are far from stable tsumego solving.

Conclusion: Search organization and resource allocation are missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation. TsuGO disentangles domain knowledge from search strategy, enabling explicit evaluation of search efficiency and related diagnostics, highlighting the need to develop models and benchmarks that optimize reasoning-search processes.

Abstract: The evaluation of LLM reasoning is moving from final-answer accuracy to process-level assessment, yet existing methods still fail to capture how models plan reasoning paths and allocate reasoning resources--that is, how they organize search. Prior process-level methods focus on the coherence and redundancy of chain-of-thought (CoT), and most benchmark tasks have a single objective solvable by static capabilities such as derivation and tool use, leaving search organization unmeasured. We introduce TsuGO, a process-level reasoning benchmark for evaluating Search Efficiency in LLM reasoning through Go life-and-death problems. These problems provide closed and verifiable solution spaces with an inherent adversarial structure, making candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking necessary parts of reasoning rather than incidental trace patterns. By constraining the solution space, TsuGO disentangles domain knowledge from search organization, parses CoT into a structured search tree, and reports Search Efficiency together with Token Efficiency and other diagnostic metrics and visualizations. Experiments show that current LLMs remain far from stable tsumego solving: stronger models succeed by finding the correct candidate earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most models still behave much closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not necessarily imply better search. Our results identify search organization and reasoning-resource allocation as missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation.

</details>


### [275] [Capability Sheaves for Compositional Agent-Harness Repair: Controlled Quotients and a Real-Repository Stress Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13228)
*Saveliy Batruin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A theoretical framework using a capability sheaf to model multi-component agent systems, with exact CSP-based acceptance and cohomology-based diagnostics; empirical study shows invariance to stale representations but no clear real-world cohomological advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain and diagnose failures when locally successful components disagree on shared state in agent-based systems, and achieve invariance to stale representations through a cohomological perspective.

Method: Model components as a capability sheaf with stalks (typed behavior signatures), restriction maps, and global sections; define acceptance via an exact finite CSP; use a linearized relative cohomology class for diagnostics and search; validate with controlled experiments and dataset from PatchFuseBench SWE-bench discovery split; compare quotienting coboundaries and alignment of hidden state; perform pool-level ranking, candidate-indexed repair, and leave-one-repository-out evaluation.

Result: Exact CSP aligns with quotient (invariance to stale representatives); discovery gate performance ambiguous and not superior; candidate-indexed repair improves some metrics but lacks cross-repository consistency; leave-one-repository-out gate ties strong anchor and exceeds a matched gate; overall partial support for the invariance mechanism and identifiability correction, but no clear real-world cohomological advantage.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates an invariance mechanism and identifiability correction, but empirical results do not show a decisive cohomological advantage in real-world tasks; further work is needed to realize practical benefits and clarify evaluation across diverse repositories.

Abstract: Agent harnesses combine retrieval, routing, state, provenance, and verification, but locally successful components may disagree on shared state. We model this failure with a finite \emph{capability sheaf}: stalks encode typed behavior signatures, restriction maps retain shared fields, and accepted runs are useful global sections. An exact finite constraint-satisfaction problem (CSP) defines acceptance, while a linearized relative cohomology class provides a diagnostic and search feature.
  A controlled experiment over 20 task clusters introduces hidden interior mediators whose raw states are nuisance variables. Quotienting their coboundaries reduces the candidate budget from 2,000 to 1,000 per cluster; aligning the hidden state removes the gap. Exact CSP matches the quotient, so the result demonstrates invariance to stale representatives, not superiority over exact reasoning.
  We then test the method on a discovery split from the SWE-bench Multilingual pool of PatchFuseBench: 160 issues from 20 repositories, 875 real candidate patches, 2,579 source-aware edit atoms, and 153 newly executed patches. A first pool-level construction is constant because $[b-Dx]=[b]$ in $\operatorname{coker}D$ and therefore cannot rank configurations. A candidate-indexed repair is nontrivial on 848/875 candidates and varies within 120/160 issues. It resolves 118 issues versus 116 for a matched noncohomological selector, but the difference is not supported across repositories (exact sign-flip $p=0.75$). A leave-one-repository-out abstention gate reaches 127/160, tying the strong anchor and exceeding its matched gate by one issue ($p=1.0$). The discovery gate therefore fails and the confirmatory split remains sealed. The study supports the controlled invariance mechanism and an identifiability correction, but not a real-world cohomological advantage.

</details>


### [276] [vToken: Token-Level Virtualization for Reclaimable KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13263)
*Yuanhang Gao,Xiangrui Yang,Yuanfeng Chen,Hongjia Chen,Qianru Lv,Wenfei Wu,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Token-level virtualization (vToken) decouples logical token liveness from physical KV block placement to reduce memory fragmentation in LLM serving, boosting throughput and concurrency with low integration cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV caches grow with sequence length and batch size; PagedAttention uses fixed-size memory blocks, causing intra-block fragmentation when eviction happens at token granularity—leading to unreclaimable memory.

Method: Introduce vToken with token-table indirection to maintain a stable logical token view and asynchronously repack live tokens for physical reclamation; preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graphs; implemented in vLLM and evaluated on H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models.

Result: Against a Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2%–72.3% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37×; under a constrained active-KV budget it extends feasible concurrency by up to 2×, and reduces per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

Conclusion: vToken effectively mitigates intra-block fragmentation by decoupling token liveness from block placement, enabling more memory-efficient KV management and higher throughput with reduced integration effort, while maintaining compatibility with existing PagedAttention implementations.

Abstract: Large language model serving faces a critical memory bottleneck: the KV cache grows with sequence length and batch size. PagedAttention uses fixed-size memory blocks to reduce allocator-level fragmentation, but recent KV eviction algorithms operate at a token granularity finer than block-level management. This mismatch causes intra-block fragmentation, leaving a large fraction of allocated KV memory unreclaimable. We present vToken, a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that decouples logical token liveness from physical block placement. vToken maintains a stable logical token view through token-table indirection and realizes physical reclamation by repacking live tokens asynchronously. The design preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility. We implement vToken in vLLM and evaluate it with H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models. Compared with a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2\%--72.3\% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37$\times$. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it extends the maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2$\times$, while reducing the per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

</details>


### [277] [Sovereign by necessity? Frontier AI export controls, cyber security, and the limits of national AI capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13272)
*Alan Woodward,Andrew Rogoyski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier AI is dominated by a few firms in two states; licensing and jurisdiction over access to foreign users illustrate state control but pose practical challenges. The paper links frontier access to national cyber defense, notes potential sovereign capabilities for a limited set of states, and proposes a layered strategy balancing access guarantees, inference-level sovereignty, open-weight hedges, regional cooperation, talent development, and cyber resilience. The open-weight approach is powerful but politically fraught; near-term risk centers on deployment and containment over raw model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess governance and security implications of frontier AI, focusing on how access, sovereignty, and geopolitical dynamics interact with cyber defense and market concentration.

Method: Policy-oriented, conceptually analytical approach informed by empirical indicators (training costs, compute concentration) and contemporary events (licensing actions, autonomous cyber activity), emphasizing scenario analysis and strategic recommendations.

Result: Access to frontier AI is framed as part of national cyber defense; access can be revoked; sovereign capability is feasible only for a few states; open-weight hedges are powerful but politically exposed; near-term risks are shaped by deployment and containment practices rather than performance.

Conclusion: A layered strategy is proposed: negotiated access guarantees; sovereignty at inference; hedging with open-weight models; regional pooled capability; sustained talent development; and continued investment in cyber resilience. Sovereignty remains partial for most states, and open-weight strategies require careful governance to mitigate political risk.

Abstract: A small number of firms based in two states produce the most capable frontier AI models. The governments of those states have shown both the legal power and the political will to decide which other countries may use these systems. In June 2026 the United States required a leading developer to obtain licences before releasing its most advanced models to any foreign person, including foreign nationals resident in the United States. The affected models were withdrawn worldwide at short notice, partly because the restriction proved impractical to administer. This followed within months of the first documented case of a largely autonomous, AI-run cyber espionage campaign, and coincided with mounting evidence that frontier models alter the economics of both cyber attack and cyber defence. This article examines how these two developments interact, and situates them within the unusual market dynamics now driving large-scale AI development. It argues that access to frontier AI is becoming part of national cyber defence, that such access can be revoked, and that the obvious remedy of sovereign capability remains only partly feasible for all but a handful of states. Drawing on evidence about training costs, the concentration of computing power and the support offered by national AI programmes, it asks what sovereignty can realistically mean for small and middle powers, and for large powers as well. The article proposes a layered strategy: negotiated access guarantees, sovereignty at the level of inference, hedging with open-weight models, pooled regional capability, sustained talent development and continued investment in basic cyber resilience. The open-weight hedge proves at once more capable and more politically exposed than is commonly assumed. Much of the near-term risk lies in how capable models are deployed and contained rather than in their apparent performance.

</details>


### [278] [Towards Context-Aware Clinical Motion Understanding in Daily Living at Home: Freezing of Gait Detection with Egocentric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13283)
*Vayalet Stefanova,Diwas Lamsal,Margot Genbrugge,Maxim Yudayev,Christian Schlenstedt,Moran Gilat,Bart Vanrumste,Benjamin Filtjens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IMU-based TCN outperforms pretrained ego-video features for FOG detection in PD under LOSO; ego-video adds contextual information but does not beat IMU alone; results suggest fusion of modalities may enhance clinical motion understanding in daily living.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Add contextual information from egocentric vision to wearable-sensor-based motion analysis to disambiguate similar kinematic patterns in ADLs, focusing on freezing of gait in Parkinson's disease in daily living.

Method: Synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels from 13 PD participants in their homes; evaluate frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, plus an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch; leave-one-subject-out evaluation.

Result: IMU-based TCN achieved 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC; V-JEPA2 ego-video features yielded 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC. Ego-video alone did not surpass IMU-based sensing but showed above-chance discrimination; qualitative analyses suggest ego-video captures FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs.

Conclusion: Pretrained ego-video representations can add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living, supporting multimodal approaches for PD gait analytics.

Abstract: Understanding motion in daily living requires context beyond kinematics, because similar inertial patterns during activities of daily living (ADLs) can reflect intentional stopping, object interaction, or pathological movement impairment. Egocentric vision provides task-related context that may help disambiguate these cases. We investigate this challenge through freezing of gait (FOG) detection in Parkinson's disease (PD), a symptom strongly influenced by contextual factors during ADLs. Using synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels collected from 13 PD participants in their homes, we evaluate frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, alongside an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, under leave-one-subject-out evaluation. The IMU-based TCN achieved the strongest event-detection performance, reaching 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC, compared with 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC for V-JEPA2 ego-video features. Although ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing, it showed above-chance discrimination, and qualitative analyses suggest that egocentric vision may capture FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs. Together, these results support the use of pretrained ego-video representations to add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living.

</details>


### [279] [NAS-Driven Hardware Accelerator Exploration for Edge AI and Quantization Effects on the Pareto Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13293)
*Eleftherios Mylonas,Angelos Kouprizas,Michael Birbas,Alexios Birbas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study of post-search quantization (PTQ) effects on NAS-discovered Pareto fronts and a hardware-aware NAS pipeline that incorporates quantized architecture mapping. They propose a three-stage pipeline and show that INT4 PTQ perturbs the Pareto space, while an FP32 zero-shot surrogate offers better Pareto coverage than an INT4-trained surrogate across two search strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge AI requires architectures that balance accuracy, efficiency, and hardware deployability. Existing NAS+quantization approaches either couple architecture and quantization tightly (in NAS loops) or study PTQ effects in isolation. There is a gap in analyzing PTQ impact on NAS Pareto structures and in integrating quantized hardware mapping with automated hardware exploration.

Method: Proposed three-stage pipeline: (1) hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend trained on NAS-Bench-201; (2) a quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control; (3) an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Empirical study quantifies INT4 PTQ perturbations on the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space using formal stability metrics across all 15,625 architectures; compares FP32 zero-shot surrogate to INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto-space coverage under two standard search strategies.

Result: The pipeline provides a framework linking post-search quantization with hardware mapping and demonstrates that INT4 PTQ can perturb the NAS Pareto space. Importantly, an FP32 zero-shot surrogate achieves better Pareto-space coverage than a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate across two search strategies.

Conclusion: The work bridges the gap between PTQ effects and hardware-aware NAS by introducing a practical three-stage pipeline and showing the robustness limits of quantization-aware surrogates. It suggests FP32 surrogates may offer superior Pareto coverage in quantized NAS scenarios and highlights the need for Pareto-aware quantization controls in NAS workflows.

Abstract: Edge AI deployment demands neural architectures that are simultaneously accurate, computationally efficient, and hardware-deployable - a challenge addressed by hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search (NAS). While recent works incorporate quantization directly into the NAS loop, these approaches expand search complexity and tightly couple architecture and quantization design. The simpler post-search quantization strategy has received little analytical attention: the effects of Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) on the NAS-discovered Pareto structure remain uncharacterised, and no framework combines quantized architecture mapping onto reconfigurable accelerators with automated hardware exploration. This paper addresses both gaps. First, a three-stage pipeline is proposed: a hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201, a quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control, and an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Second, an empirical study characterises how INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space through formal stability metrics on ground-truth data for all 15,625 architectures, and demonstrates that an FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto space coverage across two standard search strategies.

</details>


### [280] [StateBridge: Training-free Hidden-state Alignment for Latent Communication in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13317)
*Yanwen Peng,Delvin Ce Zhang,Xi Wang,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateBridge is a training-free latent communication method for multi-agent LLMs that aligns sender final-layer hidden states to the receiver’s input space using a closed-form orthogonal transform, with norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring, producing a continuous prefix to the receiver. It surpasses baselines across math, code, and QA tasks on 22/26 model-task pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discretizing communication via text tokens causes information loss and a bottleneck in multi-agent LLM systems. Latent communication promises richer information transfer, but existing approaches either modify memory across layers or rely on trainable projectors, hurting portability.

Method: Compute a closed-form orthogonal transformation to align the sender’s final-layer hidden states with the receiver’s input space (training-free). Include lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring to match the pretrained input distribution. Prepend the aligned hidden states as a continuous prefix to the receiver’s input. Evaluate across math reasoning, code generation, and question answering using four models from two families.

Result: StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score in 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline across tasks and model families.

Conclusion: A simple, portable, training-free latent communication mechanism that preserves information beyond token-level discreteness, enhancing cross-model latent transfer without additional training.

Abstract: Large language model based multi-agent systems usually communicate in text, i.e., using discrete tokens. However, text introduces a discrete bottleneck. Converting the sender's continuous hidden states into discrete tokens discards information that token identities alone cannot capture. Recent work proposes latent communication as an alternative, where agents transmit hidden representations directly without converting them to text. However, existing latent methods either inject working memory layer by layer across the transformers, or require trained projectors that limit portability. We propose StateBridge, a training-free latent communication approach that aligns the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transformation. Lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring ensure compatibility with the pretrained input distribution. The aligned states are prepended to the input of the receiver agent as a continuous prefix. We evaluate StateBridge on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [281] [LLM-Guided Graph Generation for Structure-Based Local Improvement Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13333)
*Hai Xia,Vaidyanathan Peruvemba Ramaswamy,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A problem-agnostic pipeline uses LLM-generated graph representations to guide a structure-based local improvement (SLIM) for variable selection in MiniZinc, achieving strong cross-problem performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve variable selection for large neighborhood search across diverse constraint problems by extracting structure with minimal problem-specific engineering, enabling a shared graph representation and features for configuration.

Method: Prompt an LLM with semantic guidelines to produce a graph generator that maps each problem instance to a uniform weighted graph (nodes = decision variables, edges = constraint relationships). Use these graphs in a structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) for variable selection. Extract graph features from the shared representation to drive configuration selection. Evaluate on 20 MiniZinc competition problems, comparing algorithm selection and configurations vs. baselines.

Result: Algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration (19.3%). Configuration and feature ablation raise performance to 44.0%.

Conclusion: LLM-driven semantic generation enables effective automated structure and feature extraction for constraint optimization, producing a problem-agnostic graph representation that improves cross-problem performance of variable selection and configuration.

Abstract: Large neighborhood search normally selects a random subset of decision variables for iterative optimization. For efficiently solving different problems, researchers tend to design variable selection strategies by taking into account structural features from different domains. In this paper, we build an automatic pipeline that is problem-agnostic to all problems in the MiniZinc format. By prompting an LLM with our semantic guidelines, we guide the LLM to produce a graph generator that maps any instance of a problem type to a uniform weighted graph, where nodes represent decision variables and edges represent constraint relationships. These problem-agnostic graphs guide our structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) in variable selection. Meanwhile, the weighted graph enables all problem instances to share the same generic graph representation, from which the same graph features can be extracted and used for configuration selection. We evaluated our pipeline on instances across 20 MiniZinc competition problems, finding that algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration (19.3%). Configuration and feature ablation boost the performance further to 44.0%, demonstrating that LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automated structure extraction and feature extraction for constraint optimization.

</details>


### [282] [LongEarth-R1: Benchmarking and Aligning Vision-Language Models for Long-Horizon Earth Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13344)
*Yupan Ding,Jing Xiao,Zhenyuan Zhang,Chaofeng Chen,Liang Liao,Gui-Song Xia,Mi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces LongEarth-Bench, a long-horizon remote sensing benchmark with ~120k QA samples over ~15-frame sequences, plus a 30k subset with structured reasoning traces; proposes supervised fine-tuning and LongEarth-R1 with policy optimization, yielding state-of-the-art results on 12 long-sequence tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitation of existing remote sensing vision-language models that handle isolated images or short sequences, enabling reliable grounding and reasoning across extended temporal sequences and regions.

Method: Constructs a dataset of ~117k unique images yielding ~120k QA samples across 12 tasks; sequences average 15.14 frames (up to 30); includes a 30k-sample subset with structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers; employs supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and chain-of-thought supervision; LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards.

Result: LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks and remains competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

Conclusion: The LongEarth framework demonstrates effective long-horizon reasoning in remote sensing VLs through structured supervision and policy optimization, enabling robust grounding across extended sequences and evolutions; LongEarth-R1 sets a new performance standard on long-sequence tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon Earth observation reasoning requires models to organize multi-stage geographic evolution, localize spatial changes, detect temporal anomalies, and infer future from extended image sequences. However, existing remote sensing vision-language models mainly focus on isolated images, image pairs, or short sequences, limiting reliable grounding in the relevant frames and regions. We introduce LongEarth-Bench, a benchmark containing approximately 120k question-answering samples derived from 117k unique images. Its sequences average 15.14 frames and extend to 30 frames, covering 12 tasks across evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. A 30k-sample subset further provides structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. We develop LongEarth through supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Building on LongEarth, LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards. LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks while remaining competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

</details>


### [283] [Rules or Character? Scaling Laws for AI Safety Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13345)
*Satoshi Takahashi,Nobuji Kouno,Masaaki Komatsu,Ryuji Hamamoto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A stylized model balancing training-time character shaping (e.g., RLHF) and inference-time filtering; finds optimal allocation alpha* shifts slightly toward character shaping with deployment scale, driven mainly by baseline character fragility; CVaR and expected harm converge at large scale; design choice hinges more on distributional-shift resilience than scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill a formal gap on how to optimally balance safety mechanisms as deployment scales increase, accounting for scale-dependent degradation, common-mode failures, and fragility of shaped behavior.

Method: Comparative-statics stylized model with alpha in [0,1] as resource allocation between character shaping and rule enforcement; multiplicative Pareto damage; Monte Carlo simulation for CVaR; three deployment scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic); derive closed-form expected harm and simulate tail-risk.

Result: Optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary; shifts weakly toward character shaping with increasing deployment scale T (Delta alpha* from +0.01 to +0.21, depending on scenario). Dominant parameter is baseline character fragility p^(0)_frag, which can move alpha* by ~0.50 across its range, overpowering effects of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T.

Conclusion: Safety-architecture choices depend more on how reliably character shaping holds under distributional shift than on deployment scale per se; as scale grows, the reliability of the shaped policy governs the optimal design.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) safety systems combine character shaping (e.g., Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback [RLHF], Constitutional AI), which modifies behavioral distributions at training time, with rule enforcement (e.g., output filters, safety classifiers), which blocks harmful outputs at inference time, yet little formal analysis exists on how their optimal balance should change as deployment scales increase. We introduce a stylized comparative-statics model that parameterizes safety design as a resource allocation alpha in [0,1] between these two approaches, incorporating scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility -- the risk that shaped behavior degrades or collapses under novel conditions. Under a multiplicative Pareto damage model, we derive closed-form expected harm and supplement it with tail-risk (CVaR) analysis via Monte Carlo simulation. Across three scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic), the optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary and shifts weakly toward character shaping as deployment scale T grows, from negligible (Delta alpha* = +0.01) to pronounced (Delta alpha* = +0.21) depending on scenario. The dominant parameter is the baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag, which shifts alpha* by 0.50 across its range -- far exceeding the effect of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T. These results suggest that safety architecture decisions depend less on deployment scale per se than on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift.

</details>


### [284] [TopoIntent: Compiling Security Intent into Executable, Compliance-Checked Network Topologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13389)
*Xiaokang Qu,Jianliang Ma,Zao Fan,Tianshu Chu,Tianlong Fan,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopoIntent compiles security intent into executable, CIS-8.1.2–compliant network topologies using schema constraints, template retrieval, and staged fusion, with repair and export to Mininet/IPtables; evaluated on synthetic/finance-government sets showing near-perfect CIS satisfaction and improved ACL pass rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the lack of tooling to generate structured, compliant security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements, bridging business intent, regulatory constraints, and risk into actionable network designs.

Method: Use a schema contract to constrain generation; retrieve reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search; apply staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion; check topology against CIS Controls v8.1.2 safeguards; mark unresolved cases for manual review; repair structural gaps with additive, schema-preserving edits; export final topology to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs.

Result: On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in under 1.5 rounds; one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

Conclusion: TopoIntent demonstrates automated, compliant topology generation from security intents, with measurable gains in CIS-satisfaction and ACL policy outcomes; uses a synthetic evaluation dataset to validate the approach in the absence of public benchmarks.

Abstract: Enterprise security topology design requires translating business intent, regulatory requirements, and risk assumptions into zones, boundary devices, inter-zone paths, and access-control policies. Existing NetOps automation tools mainly operate after this design is fixed, providing limited support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements. We present TopoIntent, a system that compiles security intent into executable, compliance-checked network topologies. It uses a schema contract to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search, and applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion. The generated topology is checked against CIS Controls v8.1.2 safeguards visible at the topology layer, while unresolved cases are marked for manual review. Structural gaps are repaired through additive schema-preserving edits. The final topology is exported to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs, enabling executable reachability and allow/deny tests. Because no public benchmark exists for this requirement-to-topology task, we construct an evaluation set from reference security architecture diagrams. The retrieval set contains 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents across five scenarios, while the held-out set contains 7 templates and 14 intents from finance and government scenarios excluded from retrieval. On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in fewer than 1.5 rounds on average, and one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

</details>


### [285] [Jointly Predicting Courses and Grades Using a Transformer-Based Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13409)
*Paul Savala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE: a Transformer that jointly predicts the set of courses a student will take and their grades for an upcoming semester, addressing course concurrency and outperforming grade-only and other baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing predictive models treat student history as a simple sequence and ignore the concurrent nature of courses within a semester, leading to biased predictions especially for heavy course loads. Jointly predicting courses and grades aims to improve accuracy and enable early detection.

Method: Introduce TRACE with per-semester course encoding to capture concurrency. Propose a novel loss combining course-set prediction with grade prediction. Train on ten years of institutional data. Compare to grade-only architectures and baselines (LSTM, GNN). Allow incorporation of student attributes and retraining for new institutions.

Result: Joint prediction yields nearly 50% reduction in mean absolute error compared to an identical architecture predicting grades alone. Outperforms LSTM-based sequential models and graph neural networks. Demonstrates potential for interpretability and adaptation to new institutions via retraining. Supports integration into early-detection systems at higher education institutions.

Conclusion: Joint modeling of course selection and grades with per-semester encoding is effective for accurate predictions and transferability. Predicting courses during training yields significant gains and offers practical pathways for deployment and institution-wide analytics.

Abstract: Existing predictive models in learning analytics often treat student academic history as a simple sequence, overlooking the concurrent nature of courses taken within a semester. This simplification can lead to inaccurate performance predictions, particularly for students with heavy or challenging course loads. This paper introduces a TRansformer for Academic Course-grade Estimation (TRACE) that addresses this limitation by jointly predicting both the set of courses a student will take and their corresponding grades for an upcoming semester. Our approach encodes courses on a per-semester basis to capture the effects of course concurrency and utilizes a novel loss function combining course-set prediction with grade prediction. We demonstrate that predicting courses taken in addition to the grades in those courses leads to significant improvements in prediction quality. Trained on ten years of institutional data, our joint prediction model reduces mean absolute error by nearly 50% compared to an identical architecture that predicts grades alone. The model also outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models, as well as graph neural network-based approaches, and offers natural ways to incorporate student attribute data. This work demonstrates the utility of modern neural architectures for creating interpretable models that can be adapted to new institutions via retraining and recalibration, as well as the importance of key techniques, such as predicting courses taken during training. We discuss how this model could be incorporated into early detection systems at institutions of higher education.

</details>


### [286] [Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410)
*Mirko Tritella,Riccardo Pozzi,Matteo Palmonari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ParliamentRAG introduces a RAG system with a topic-dependent authority model to balance speaker influence, leverage topical expertise, and improve quotation fidelity in Italian Parliament transcripts; evaluated against NotebookLM on 15 policy topics, showing higher group coverage and quotation faithfulness but NotebookLM excels in prose styling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parliamentary proceedings are voluminous and fragmented, limiting multi-perspective access for citizens, journalists, and researchers. A retrieval-augmented generation approach with speaker authority weighting aims to enhance coverage of diverse viewpoints while reducing misattribution and speaker dominance.

Method: Develop a topic-dependent authority model that combines interpretable speaker attributes (profession, education, prior interventions) to estimate authority conditioned on the query. Retrieve relevant speech chunks, identify topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generate a synthesis with supporting quotations.

Result: Compared to Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol (automated metrics + blind A/B evaluation by six domain experts), ParliamentRAG achieved higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs 0.95) and perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions; NotebookLM performed better on prose-oriented dimensions.

Conclusion: The approach jointly addresses dominance, expertise weighting, and citation fidelity in RAG for parliamentary transcripts, improves multi-perspective access, and demonstrates favorable evaluations with respect to coverage and quotation faithfulness, with trade-offs in prose quality relative to NotebookLM.

Abstract: Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Applying Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to parliamentary transcripts introduces three specific risks: dominance of the most frequent speakers, inability to weight speakers according to topical expertise, and citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. We present ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that addresses these risks jointly. Its core contribution is a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions. Given a user query, the system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesizing their perspectives, accompanied by supporting quotations. ParliamentRAG is evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol combining automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.

</details>


### [287] [Beyond Final Scores: A Systematic Evaluation of Agents for Long-Horizon AI Research and Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13417)
*Yiwei Li,Wanli Yang,Hexiang Tan,Xiangzhou Huang,Zhengyu Chen,Ziran Li,Borun Chen,Shanglin Lei,Huaisheng Zhu,Hao Tian,Fei Sun,Xunliang Cai,Jingang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Seven frontier models evaluated on 36 long-horizon tasks using a rule-based framework; agents act like engineering optimizers with limited novelty; bottlenecks and experience reuse shape performance; offers directions for training, inference, experience management, harness design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess true progress in long-horizon autonomous experimentation beyond final scores and understand where decisions improve or fail over time.

Method: Introduce a framework with Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control; perform controlled comparisons; evaluate seven frontier models on 36 tasks; analyze within-run behavior and experience reuse.

Result: Agents can formulate and implement practical solutions but show high performance variability; strongest results rely on adapting/plausibly combining established techniques; genuine methodological novelty is rare; observed performance shaped by bottlenecks, experience reuse, and harness design on stability.

Conclusion: Provide concrete directions for improving training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design; to shift agents toward more autonomous research with stable, novel methodology.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of improving models, systems, and other technical artifacts through long-horizon experimentation. To understand the current state of this capability, however, evaluation must go beyond final scores, which neither reveal where progress is gained or lost nor indicate whether accumulated experience improves later decisions. We therefore present a systematic evaluation of seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks based on a new framework that uses rule-based metrics to characterize within-run behavior through Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control and controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks. The results show that current agents operate more like engineering optimizers than fully autonomous researchers: they can formulate and implement practical solutions, but their performance varies substantially across runs, their strongest solutions mainly adapt or combine established techniques, and genuine methodological novelty remains rare. Detailed analysis reveals that observed performance is shaped by multiple factors, including distinct process bottlenecks behind similar final outcomes, experience reuse that can help or mislead subsequent decisions, and harness designs that affect performance stability. These findings suggest concrete directions for improving model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design.

</details>


### [288] [Enhancing Virtual Agents through SLMs and Edge-Computing: An Exploratory Evaluation of Think and Memory Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13420)
*Aimilios Hadjiliasi,Louis Nisiotis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edge-based small language models enable partial CEAA cognitive processes (Think, Memory) for embodied agents; evaluated on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX with Qwen2.5 models, demonstrating memory-driven conversations and routing under latency constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of cognitively capable agents in complex virtual/Metaverse environments by leveraging edge computing and SLMs to support CEAA components (Think and Memory) while reducing cloud dependency and latency.

Method: Developed an edge-based virtual agent gateway on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX and integrated Qwen2.5 SLMs of varying sizes to support CEAA components, with emphasis on Think and Memory. Conducted simulation experiments to assess routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency, evaluating an SLM-driven prototype that partially implements CEAA processes.

Result: The prototype demonstrates partial implementation of CEAA components driven by SLMs, capable of processing service requests and supporting memory-driven conversations. Simulation results indicate feasible routing, memory access, and latency profiles suitable for interactive virtual worlds.

Conclusion: SLM-driven edge architectures can feasibly support cognitive orchestration in embodied agents by partially implementing Think and Memory; results justify further integration of CEAA components and optimization for latency, memory persistence, and scalability in immersive environments.

Abstract: Embodied intelligent virtual agents are expected to operate as persistent, adaptive, and context-aware entities within complex virtual and Metaverse worlds. However, implementing cognitively capable agents in such environments is conceptually and technologically challenging. Among a range of blueprints and development approaches, the Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) has been developed as an implementation-oriented framework for architecting components of perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and embodied action. Considering the recent advances in edge computing and generative AI language models, this paper explores the use of Small Language Models (SLMs) to support edge-based operation of selected CEAA components, focusing on "Think" and "Memory" as processes central to cognitive orchestration and persistence of virtual agents in interactive virtual worlds. An edge-based virtual agent gateway system was developed and evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using Qwen2.5 models of different sizes, exploring the system's capability to process service requests and handle memory-driven conversations. A series of simulation experiments evaluated routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency, demonstrating an SLM-driven prototype agent system that partially implements selected CEAA processes to support the development of embodied agents whose cognitive "brain" can operate efficiently and contextually for interactive experiences in immersive virtual worlds.

</details>


### [289] [RAIL: An Automatic Classifier of the Artificial Intelligence Readiness Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13428)
*Juan Irving Vasquez,Juan Terven,Laura-Ivoone Garay-Jimenez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified nine-level AI readiness scale (AIRL) paired with a panel-of-LLM-based classifier (RAIL) to automate readiness assessments from natural-language descriptions, addressing heterogeneity and gating in existing frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap among heterogeneous AI readiness frameworks by delivering a decidable, comparable scale and an automated, auditable assessment workflow suitable for investment, policy, and project management.

Method: Design AIRL with an environmental evidence ladder, nine ordinal levels, and dimensional caps (specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, algorithmic maturity) plus global rules for assignment; implement RAIL with one evidence agent and six dimension agents (LLMs with narrow mandates), a deterministic minimum-rule aggregator, and chief expert oversight that cannot raise ratings beyond caps; evaluate on several research works.

Result: AIRL enables readiness levels to be inferred from natural-language descriptions; RAIL produces consistent assessments and reduces overestimation compared to single monolithic LLM classifiers.

Conclusion: AIRL offers a coherent, comparable framework for AI readiness, and RAIL provides a scalable, automated implementation via specialized LLMs, with empirical validation indicating consistency; further calibration, metrics, and scalability studies would strengthen practical adoption.

Abstract: Assessing the maturity of artificial intelligence technologies is essential for investment decisions, project management, and policy monitoring, yet the available readiness frameworks are heterogeneous and difficult to apply automatically: the adaptation of Technology Readiness Levels to AI lacks AI-specific gating criteria, the Machine Learning Technology Readiness Levels presuppose access to internal process artifacts, and AI/data readiness dimension models employ scales that resist direct comparison. This paper makes two contributions. First, we unify these three frameworks into the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale built on an environmental evidence ladder and complemented by dimensional caps (covering specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity) together with a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines, so that a readiness level becomes decidable from a natural-language description of the work alone. Second, we propose RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts), a panel-of-experts classifier that operationalizes the scale: one evidence agent and six independent dimension agents, each a large language model with a narrowly scoped mandate, deliver verdicts that a deterministic minimum rule aggregates and a chief expert reviews under asymmetric authority, confirming or lowering the panel's recommendation but never raising it above the caps. The method was tested in the analysis of several research works showing consistency and avoiding overestimation from monolithic LLM classifiers.

</details>


### [290] [Academic League of Artificial Intelligence - An Integrative Perspective of Teaching, Research, and Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13447)
*Alison R. Panisson,Maria Eduarda W. M. Vianna,Italo Firmino da Silva,Heitor Henrique da Silva,Rafaela Fernandes Savaris,Bernardo Pandolfi Costa,Martin Augusto Gagliotti Vigil,Jim Lau,Agenor Hentz,Andréa Sabedra Bordin,Alexandre Leopoldo Gonçalves,Roberto Rodrigues-Filho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A flexible, replicable organizational framework for Academic Leagues to integrate teaching, research, and extension via student-centered, project-based governance, demonstrated by UFSC's LIA with multiple initiatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To promote extracurricular education and strengthen university-society integration by aligning teaching, research, and extension; develop leadership, collaboration, and knowledge preservation in engineering/computing education.

Method: Democratic governance combined with collaborative learning and dynamic project organization within a student-centered, project-based framework; integrates the three university pillars through initiatives such as competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered applications.

Result: Demonstrates viability and flexibility of the framework through representative initiatives; shows how diverse activities can be embedded within a common structure while fostering leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation; offers a replicable model and practical guidance for similar student organizations.

Conclusion: The framework provides a practical, adaptable blueprint to integrate teaching, research, and extension in engineering and computing education, enabling replication by academic leagues and related student organizations.

Abstract: Academic leagues have become important mechanisms for promoting extracurricular education and strengthening the integration between universities and society. This paper presents the organizational framework adopted by the Academic League of Artificial Intelligence (LIA) at the Federal University of Santa Catarina (UFSC), designed to integrate teaching, research, and university extension through a student-centered, project-based approach. The framework combines democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization to foster both technical and transversal competencies. The framework is illustrated through representative initiatives, including competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered applications with social impact. These projects demonstrate how diverse educational, scientific, and extension activities can be developed within a common organizational structure while promoting leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation. The reported experience indicates that the proposed framework provides a flexible and replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, offering practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations.

</details>


### [291] [A Unifying Perspective on Causal World Models: From Observations to Representations to Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13456)
*Avinash Kori,Fabrizio Russo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Causal World Models (CWMs): a formal, multi-level causal framework for world modelling, linking perceptual representations, entity interactions, and environment dynamics to causal reasoning and decision-making, with identifiability considerations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current world models are often purely generative or lack structured causal reasoning. There is a need for representations that capture entity properties, interactions, and environment-driven dynamics to enable robust prediction, planning, and generalization across distributions.

Method: Develop a formal definition of Causal World Models (CWMs) across abstraction levels, grounding them in established lines of work: causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making; analyze identifiability and recovery up to equivalence.

Result: Provides a formal definition of CWMs and synthesizes connections to related literature, clarifying identifiability conditions and the extent to which WM components can be recovered from data up to certain equivalence relations.

Conclusion: CWMs ground world models in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making, outlining a research agenda and connecting WM theory to causal inference and planning.

Abstract: World Models (WM) are increasingly seen as a foundation for intelligent agents that can predict, plan, and act beyond their training distribution. In this paper, we study WMs from a causal perspective across multiple levels of abstraction, ranging from perceptual observations to building a conceptual representation of the structure governing the environment dynamics. We argue that useful WMs must go beyond generative capabilities alone: they should also capture entity properties, entity-to-entity interactions, and entity-to-environment interactions that determine and explain the dynamics of a system. We provide a formal definition of Causal WMs (CWMs) grounded in the tasks they are intended to support, connecting world modelling with existing work in causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making. Finally, we relate CWMs to the literature on identifiability, clarifying when the components of a WM can be recovered from data and up to which equivalence. With this, we ground WMs in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

</details>


### [292] [MARC v1: An Open-Source Multi-Agent Framework for Clinical AI Reasoning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13476)
*Saisha Shetty,Satvik Tripathi,Austin Lin,Colin Zhao,Theodore Kim,Don Enwerem,Jacinta Arnold,Shahriar Faghani,Tessa S Cook*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MARC is an open-source, multi-agent framework for clinical reasoning that replaces monolithic LLM prompts with deterministic, role-specialized agents, plus a Decomposer to auto-generate prompts from plain language. It is YAML-configurable, model-agnostic, and supports API or local CPU deployments, enabling traceable, stage-wise failure attribution and clinician accessibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome limitations of single-prompt LLM approaches by introducing structured, interpretable, and traceable multi-agent coordination; reduce manual prompt engineering; enable domain experts (clinicians) to leverage LLMs without programming.

Method: Deterministic orchestration of role-specific agents (extraction, reasoning, answer generation, evaluation) with explicit context passing and traceable intermediate outputs; a Decomposer module generates task-specific agent prompts from plain-language descriptions; YAML-based configuration; supports API-based and local CPU deployments; model-agnostic architecture.

Result: Proposal and release of MARC as an open-source framework with decomposer, traceable outputs, and configurable deployment options; designed to be interpretable and accessible to clinicians, with full implementation at the provided GitHub repository.

Conclusion: MARC achieves an interpretable, model-agnostic, clinician-friendly multi-agent framework for clinical reasoning, eliminating manual prompt engineering via the Decomposer and offering YAML-based configuration and deployment flexibility.

Abstract: We present Multi-Agent Reasoning and Coordination (MARC), an open-source framework that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning. MARC coordinates role-specialized agents for extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation, with explicit context passing and traceable intermediate outputs, enabling stage-wise failure attribution. We additionally introduce a Decomposer module that generates task-specific agent prompts from a plain-language description, eliminating manual prompt engineering. The framework supports both API-based and local CPU-compatible deployments and is entirely configurable via YAML, without code modifications. MARC is designed to be model-agnostic, interpretable, and accessible to clinical domain experts without programming expertise. The full framework is available at https://github.com/Penn-RAIL/MARC-v1.

</details>


### [293] [AlayaWorld: Interactive Long-Horizon World Modeling - Full Technical Report (v1.1)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13492)
*AlayaWorld Team,Kaipeng Zhang,Chuanhao Li,Yifan Zhan,Yongtao Ge,Yuanyang Yin,Jiaming Tan,Kang He,Liaoyuan Fan,Mingliang Zhai,Ruicong Liu,Xiaojie Xu,Xuangeng Chu,Zhen Li,Zhengyuan Lin,Zhixiang Wang,Zian Meng,Zihui Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Improved AlayaWorld version with six design changes to conditioning: motion-aware latent conditioning, streaming 3D point-cache, unified VAE encoding/decoding, hard memory dropout, and removal of camera AdaLN; aims to enhance conditioning fidelity and temporal coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conditioning signals should closely match the generated content in both latent representation and temporal structure, enabling better temporal coherence and controllability.

Method: Six modifications: (1) replace static-frame conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encode re-rendered spatial memory as a continuous sequence; (3) align the temporal-memory window in pixel space; (4) use hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens; (5) unify VAE encoding/decoding protocol across training and inference; (6) remove the camera AdaLN branch; viewpoint control via re-rendered spatial condition only.

Result: The abstract emphasizes design changes and intended improvements (conditioning fidelity and temporal consistency) but does not provide quantitative results or metrics.

Conclusion: The six coordinated changes aim to unify conditioning with generated content and memory, improving motion-aware conditioning, temporal coherence, and viewpoint control through re-rendered spatial conditions.

Abstract: This report presents an improved version of AlayaWorld. While the backbone architecture, chunk-wise autoregressive generation scheme, and training data remain unchanged from the previous release, we substantially revise how conditioning signals are represented and integrated into the model. The new design is guided by a simple principle: conditioning signals should match the generated content as closely as possible in both latent representation and temporal structure. To this end, we make two major changes. First, we replace the previous depth-warping-based spatial memory with a streaming 3D point-cache renderer. Second, we redesign the conditioning pipeline so that visual conditions are encoded in the same causal-VAE latent space, with temporal statistics consistent with those of the generated video. Concretely, the new version introduces six modifications: (1) replacing static-frame image conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encoding re-rendered spatial memory as a continuous sequence; (3) aligning the temporal-memory window in pixel space; (4) adopting hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens rather than zeroing them; (5) unifying the VAE encoding and decoding protocol across training and inference; and (6) removing the camera AdaLN branch, such that viewpoint control is provided entirely through the re-rendered spatial condition.

</details>


### [294] [QuoteBench: How Matched Scores Can Hide Command-Path Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13547)
*Shangao Li,Yao Zhang,Volker Tresp,Yuanyuan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuoteBench reveals that matched final-state validation is necessary to separate command-generation errors from execution-path errors in LLM command agents; large boundary gaps across configurations show that raw generation scores misrepresent capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interfaces for LLM-driven Bash commands can serialize, wrap, and reparse model output, making it hard to tell whether failures arise during generation or during execution. The paper defines a boundary-focused evaluation to quantify this gap between generation contract and execution transport.

Method: 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families are evaluated with exact final-state validation across eight same-window configurations. The setup crosses the generation contract with an added parser around the execution path, using a deliberately unescaped parser. Escaping at the interpolation point reproduces the replayed reply's raw-path outcome; boundary recovery must come from model changes in generation. Replay and disclosure are tested across configurations to measure the boundary gap and its remediation.

Result: Replaying the same reply through the added parser reduces success by 55.4 to 73.2 percentage points. Disclosure recovers 30.4 to 60.7 points for six configurations, and zero or slightly negative for the remaining two.
